arXiv:2609.10450v1 [math.PR] 09 Sep 2026 
 
 
 Aldous’ spectral gap phenomena
 in stochastic exchange models 
 
 
 Pietro Caputo, Matteo Quattropani, and Federico Sau
 
 Address:  Pietro Caputo
 Università degli Studi Roma Tre
 
 Email address:  pietro.caputo@uniroma3.it 
 
 Address:  Matteo Quattropani
 Università degli Studi Roma Tre
 
 Email address:  matteo.quattropani@uniroma3.it 
 
 Address:  Federico Sau
 Università degli Studi di Milano
 
 Email address:  federico.sau@unimi.it 
 

 Abstract. 
    
 We consider a broad class of exchange dynamics with arbitrary weighted graph or hypergraph update structures, which includes the Kipnis–Marchioro–Presutti (KMP) model and the energies of Kac’s walk on the sphere. These are conservative continuous-spin systems whose reversible measures are Dirichlet distributions. We prove that their spectral gap is always attained by a polynomial of degree at most two in the energy variables. Equivalently, through an intertwining with the associated discrete particle systems, the dominant mode is always represented by either a one-particle or a two-particle observable. We interpret this as a manifestation of an Aldous-type spectral gap phenomenon. In particular, the resulting two-particle spectral gap identity settles a recent conjecture of Alon and Puder. 
    
 We also characterize sharply when one particle suffices and when a genuinely two-particle mode dominates. Under a common rescaling of the Dirichlet parameters, segment-like geometries are precisely those for which the gap is always of one-particle type, mean-field geometries are precisely those for which it is always of two-particle type, and all other geometries exhibit a nontrivial transition. 
    
 The proof relies on the analysis of the so-called hidden model, a dual representation of the original process. The mechanism underlying the degree-two reduction is remarkably robust and extends to a much broader class of stochastic exchange models and their associated particle systems, including the harmonic process, the immediate exchange model, averaging-type processes, and nonreversible variants. 
    
 Finally, we analyze boundary-driven versions of the KMP model, in which the bulk exchange dynamics interacts with reservoirs, generally resulting in nonreversible processes. In contrast to the conservative setting, we prove that the spectral gap of a boundary-driven model is always of one-particle type. 
  
 
 
 Keywords:  Kipnis–Marchioro–Presutti model; stochastic exchange models;
spectral gap; Aldous-type spectral gap identities;
Kac’s walk; particle systems on hypergraphs.
 
 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification Primary 60K35; Secondary 60J25, 60J27, 82C22, 05C65.

 1.  Introduction, models and main results 

 
 Stochastic exchange models are a broad family of Markov processes in which a continuous quantity, such as energy, wealth, or mass, is randomly redistributed among agents interacting through the edges of a graph or hypergraph. These models play a prominent role across a variety of fields, ranging from statistical physics to economics and the social sciences. Important examples include the Kipnis–Marchioro–Presutti (KMP) model  [ KMP82 ] , Kac’s walk on the sphere  [ Kac56 ,  DSC00 ,  CCL03 ,  CLL20 ] , the averaging process  [ AL12 ] , the harmonic process  [ FG22 ] , and the immediate exchange model  [ HP14 ] . 
 
 
 A fundamental feature of these dynamics is their intertwining relation with discrete particle systems, which allows one to describe the action of the generator on polynomial observables in terms of interacting random walks on the underlying (hyper)graph. In this work we prove that the spectral gap of these models is always attained by a polynomial observable of degree at most two in the energy variables. In terms of the associated particle systems, this means that the dominant relaxation mode is always described by either a one-particle or a two-particle observable, a property that we refer to as an Aldous-type phenomenon. 
 
 
 The name originates from a conjecture of David Aldous concerning a different dynamics, namely the interchange process on graphs, asserting that the spectral gap is always realized by a one-particle eigenfunction. This conjecture was later proved for arbitrary weighted graphs in  [ CLR10 ] . An analogous statement was then conjectured for interchange processes on arbitrary weighted hypergraphs; see, for instance,  [ BC24 , Conjecture 1.7] . In that generality, the conjecture remains open; see  [ AKP25 ,  BC24 ]  for some partial progress. See also  [ Ces16 ,  AP26 ,  LP26 ]  and references therein for some further related results. 
 
 
 In the setting of stochastic exchange models, however, it is known that one-particle eigenfunctions do not suffice in general, and that genuinely two-particle observables are sometimes required; see, e.g.,  [ CLL20 , Remark 4] . It remained, however, to understand
whether two particles are always sufficient to characterize the spectral gap. Related phenomena and questions have recently been investigated for a broad class of stochastic exchange models in  [ KQS25 ] , for the symmetric inclusion process and the related diffusion  [ KS24 ,  KS26 ] , and for random walks on the unitary group generated by weighted hypergraphs in  [ AP26 ] . These works provide substantial progress and proved the validity of Aldous-type phenomena in several special cases, but the question of whether two particles always suffice remains unresolved. 
 
 
 The present work settles this issue in the affirmative for stochastic exchange models, answering several questions raised in  [ KQS25 ] . It also proves a conjecture of Alon and Puder stated in  [ AP26 , Conjecture 1.15] , yet, not addressing the more general statement concerning random walks on the unitary group formulated in  [ AP26 , Conjecture 1.14] . 
 
 
 1.1.  KMP models: Aldous’ spectral gap phenomenon 

 
 Let  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 ,  [ n ] ≔ { 1 , … , n } [n]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,n\} , and
consider the simplex 
 

 
 (1.1) 
 
 Ω ≔ { η ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] n : ∑ x ∈ [ n ] η x = 1 } ​ . \textstyle\Omega\coloneqq\big\{\eta\in[0,1]^{n}:\sum_{x\in[n]}\eta_{x}=1\big\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In the original KMP model,  ( η x ) x ∈ [ n ] (\eta_{x})_{x\in[n]}  represent the energies in a one-dimensional chain of oscillators, and at rate one independently each pair  ( η x , η x + 1 ) (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+1}) ,  x = 1 , … , n − 1 x=1,\dots,n-1 , undergoes exchange updates 
 

 
 (1.2) 
 
 ( η x , η x + 1 ) ⟼ ( U ⁡ ( η x + η x + 1 ) , ( 1 − U ) ​ ( η x + η x + 1 ) ) ​ , (\eta_{x},\eta_{x+1})\longmapsto(U(\eta_{x}+\eta_{x+1}),(1-U)(\eta_{x}+\eta_{x+1}))\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  U U  is a uniform random variable in  [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . In the absence of external reservoirs, the total energy  ∑ x = 1 n η x = 1 \sum_{x=1}^{n}\eta_{x}=1  is conserved—hence, we sometimes speak of the  conservative   KMP {\rm KMP}  model—and the equilibrium measure is simply the uniform distribution over  Ω \Omega . Convergence to stationarity for this model was extensively analyzed in  [ CLL20 ] , which, among other things, proved that the spectral gap is given by
 λ = 1 − cos ⁡ ( π / n ) \lambda=1-\cos(\pi/n)  with the linear eigenfunction 
 

 
 (1.3) 
 
 η ⟼ ∑ y = 1 n − 1 sin ⁡ ( π ​ y / n ) ​ ∑ x = 1 y ( η x − 1 n ) . \eta\longmapsto\sum_{y=1}^{n-1}\sin(\pi y/n)\sum_{x=1}^{y}\left(\eta_{x}-\tfrac{1}{n}\right)\,. 
 
 
 If one replaces the graph segment  { 1 , … , n } \{1,\dots,n\}  by the complete graph over  n n  vertices, computations as in  [ CCL03 ,  Cap08 ]  show that the spectral gap is now given by  λ = ( n + 1 ) / 3 \lambda=(n+1)/3  with the quadratic eigenfunction 
 

 
 (1.4) 
 
 η ⟼ ∑ x = 1 n ( η x 2 − 2 n ⁡ ( n + 1 ) ) ​ . \eta\longmapsto\sum_{x=1}^{n}\left(\eta_{x}^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n(n+1)}\right)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 In this work, we consider a generalized version of the
KMP model where the underlying graph is a completely arbitrary weighted hypergraph, and the redistribution variable  U U  in ( 1.2 ) is replaced by a general inhomogeneous Dirichlet distribution.
While there are no closed formulas for the spectral gap and the associated eigenfunction in this generality, we shall prove that, for the conservative KMP model, the dominant mode is always a polynomial of degree at most two. In contrast, we show that polynomials of degree one always suffice for its non-conservative variant; see Section  1.3.2 . 
 
 
 1.1.1.  Setting and first main result 

 
 Fix positive site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]} , and nonnegative block weights  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} . The  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model  is the process where each block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  is updated independently with rate  w B w_{B} , and when an update occurs at  B B , the variables  η B = ( η x ) x ∈ B \eta^{B}=(\eta_{x})_{x\in B}  undergo the transition 
 

 
 (1.5) 
 
 η B ⟼ η ⁡ ( B ) ​ U ​ , U = ( U x ) x ∈ B ∼ Dir ⁡ ( α B ) ​ , ​ η ​ ( B ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ B η x ​ . \textstyle\eta^{B}\longmapsto\eta(B)U\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad U=(U_{x})_{x\in B}\sim{\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B})\;\text{,}\;\;\eta(B)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in B}\eta_{x}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  Dir ⁡ ( α B ) {\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B}) , with  α B = ( α x ) x ∈ B \alpha^{B}=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in B} , denotes the Dirichlet distribution on the probability simplex over  B B , with density proportional to  ∏ x ∈ B u x α x − 1 \prod_{x\in B}u_{x}^{\alpha_{x}-1} , or equivalently the law of  ( V x / V ⁡ ( B ) ) x ∈ B (V_{x}/V(B))_{x\in B} , where the  V x V_{x}  are independent  Gamma ⁡ ( α x , 1 ) {\rm Gamma}(\alpha_{x},1)  random variables, with density proportional to  v α x − 1 ​ e − v v^{\alpha_{x}-1}e^{-v}  on  ( 0 , ∞ ) (0,\infty) , and  V ⁡ ( B ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ B V x V(B)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in B}V_{x} . The Dirichlet distribution is the multivariate analogue of the Beta distribution; in particular, if  B = { x , y } B=\{x,y\} , then  U x ∼ Beta ⁡ ( α x , α y ) U_{x}\sim{\rm Beta}(\alpha_{x},\alpha_{y})  and  U y = 1 − U x ∼ Beta ⁡ ( α y , α x ) U_{y}=1-U_{x}\sim{\rm Beta}(\alpha_{y},\alpha_{x}) . 
 
 
 Note that when  B = { x , x + 1 } B=\{x,x+1\}  and  α x = α x + 1 = 1 \alpha_{x}=\alpha_{x+1}=1 , ( 1.5 ) coincides with the exchange update in ( 1.2 ).
Note also that if  μ = Dir ⁡ ( α ) \mu={\rm Dir}(\alpha)  denotes the Dirichlet distribution on  Ω \Omega  with parameters  α \alpha , then the update at  B B  replaces the energy variables  η B = ( η x ) x ∈ B \eta^{B}=(\eta_{x})_{x\in B}  by a sample from their conditional distribution under  μ \mu  given the variables  η B c = ( η y ) y ∉ B \eta^{B^{\mathrm{c}}}=(\eta_{y})_{y\notin B} .
In other words,
 ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  is a block heat-bath dynamics with respect to  μ \mu  and, more precisely, the Markov process with state space  Ω ⊆ ℝ n \Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}  with infinitesimal generator 
 

 
 (1.6) 
 
 ℒ ​ f ​ ( η ) = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ( μ B ​ f ​ ( η ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ , η ∈ Ω ​ , \mathcal{L}f(\eta)=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\left(\mu_{B}f(\eta)-f(\eta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  f f  is any bounded measurable function on  Ω \Omega , and
 μ B ​ f ​ ( η ) ≔ μ ⁡ ( f ∣ η B c ) \mu_{B}f(\eta)\coloneqq\mu(f\mid\eta^{B^{\mathrm{c}}}) 
is the conditional expectation with respect to  μ \mu  given  η B c \eta^{B^{\mathrm{c}}} . Being a finite linear combination of orthogonal projections,  ℒ \mathcal{L}  is a bounded self-adjoint operator on the (real) Hilbert space  L 2 ​ ( μ ) = L 2 ​ ( Ω , μ ) L^{2}(\mu)=L^{2}(\Omega,\mu) . The spectral gap is the second largest eigenvalue of  − ℒ -\mathcal{L} , which is given by 
 

 
 (1.7) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ ) = inf f ∈ L 2 ​ ( μ ) f ≠ 0 , μ ⁡ ( f ) = 0 ⟨ f , − ℒ ​ f ⟩ μ ⟨ f , f ⟩ μ ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L})=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in L^{2}(\mu)\\
f\neq 0,\,\mu(f)=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle f,-\mathcal{L}f\rangle_{\mu}}{\langle f,f\rangle_{\mu}}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 where  ⟨ f , g ⟩ μ = μ ⁡ ( f ​ g ) \langle f,g\rangle_{\mu}=\mu(fg)  denotes the scalar product in  L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu) . Equivalently,  gap ⁡ ( ℒ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L})  is the largest constant  λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0  such that, for every  f ∈ L 2 ​ ( μ ) f\in L^{2}(\mu) , 
 

 
 (1.8) 
 
 ‖ e t ​ ℒ ​ f − μ ⁡ ( f ) ‖ μ ≤ e − λ ​ t ​ ‖ f − μ ⁡ ( f ) ‖ μ , t ≥ 0 ​ . \|e^{t\mathcal{L}}f-\mu(f)\|_{\mu}\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|f-\mu(f)\|_{\mu}\,,\qquad t\geq 0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 where  ( e t ​ ℒ ) t ≥ 0 (e^{t\mathcal{L}})_{t\geq 0}  denotes the semigroup generated by  ℒ \mathcal{L} , and  ∥ ⋅ ∥ μ \|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\mu}  is the norm in  L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu) .
It is possible to verify—and also follows from Theorem  1.4  or Remark  2.4  below—that 
 

 
 (1.9) 
 
 gap ( ℒ ) > 0 ⟺ w induces a connected hypergraph . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L})>0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad w\ \text{induces a connected hypergraph}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, we refer to the standard notion of hypergraph connectedness; see ( 1.36 ). 
 
 
 Let  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  denote the set of all polynomials of degree at most  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  in the variables  η ∈ Ω \eta\in{\Omega} , that is, the linear span of all monomials  η x 1 ⋯ η x j \eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{j}} , with  x i ∈ [ n ] x_{i}\in[n]  and  j ≤ k j\leq k , while  𝒫 0 \mathscr{P}_{0}  denotes the space of constant functions. A simple computation shows that 
 

 
 (1.10) 
 
 ℒ ​ 𝒫 k ⊆ 𝒫 k ​ , k ≥ 0 ​ , \mathcal{L}\mathscr{P}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 that is,  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  is left invariant by  ℒ \mathcal{L} . As each  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  is a finite dimensional vector space, the invariance in ( 1.10 ), the density of polynomials in  L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu) , and the self-adjointness of  ℒ \mathcal{L}  guarantee that
the spectrum of  ℒ \mathcal{L}  is pure point, and that all eigenfunctions are polynomials. Moreover, 
 

 
 (1.11) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ ) = inf k ≥ 1 gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 k ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L})=\inf_{k\geq 1}{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 k ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})  is obtained as in ( 1.7 )
with the restriction that  f ∈ 𝒫 k f\in\mathscr{P}_{k} .
Our first main result states that one may actually restrict to  k = 2 k=2 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1   (KMP) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and all nonnegative block weights  w w , the spectral gap of the  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model satisfies 
 

 
 (1.12) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 2 ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 1.1.2.  Particle systems and second main result 

 
 As a starting point, we observe that the invariance property ( 1.10 ) admits a sharper formulation: the action of  ℒ \mathcal{L}  on polynomials transfers to a particle system’s generator acting on their coefficients. We now recall some basic facts, postponing all the details to Section  2.1 . 
 
 
 Fix  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 . Since  ∑ x ∈ [ n ] η x = 1 \sum_{x\in[n]}\eta_{x}=1  on  Ω \Omega , every element of  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  can be represented in homogeneous degree  k k  form. Thus, any polynomial in  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  takes the form 
 

 
 (1.13) 
 
 ψ ^ ( η ) ≔ ∑ x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] ψ ( x 1 , … , x k ) η x 1 ⋯ η x k , η ∈ Ω , \widehat{\psi}(\eta)\coloneqq\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]}\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\,\eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for some  ψ : [ n ] k → ℝ \psi:[n]^{k}\to\mathbb{R} .
Then, as shown in Proposition  2.3 , for every  ψ ∈ ℝ [ n ] k \psi\in\mathbb{R}^{[n]^{k}} , 
 

 
 (1.14) 
 
 ℒ ​ ψ ^ = L k ​ ψ ^ ​ , \mathcal{L}\widehat{\psi}=\widehat{L_{k}\psi}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  L k L_{k}  denotes the generator of a labeled  k k -particle system on  [ n ] k [n]^{k} .
The dynamics is as follows. Start with  k k  labeled particles at positions  x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n] . Whenever a block  B B  is selected, at rate  w B w_{B} , sample
 U ∼ Dir ⁡ ( α B ) U\sim{\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B}) .
Each particle currently sitting in  B B  is then resampled inside  B B , conditionally independently given  U U , according to 
 

 
 (1.15) 
 
 x i ∈ B ⟼ y ∈ B with probability  ​ U y ​ , x_{i}\in B\longmapsto y\in B\quad\text{with probability }U_{y}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 whereas particles outside  B B  are kept fixed.
After averaging over the block updates and over the Dirichlet vectors, one obtains the Markov generator  L k L_{k} ; see ( 2.4 ) below for the explicit expression. We call the resulting process on  [ n ] k [n]^{k}  the  KMP {\rm KMP}  labeled-particle system or, more precisely, the discrete  ( k , α , w ) (k,\alpha,w) -KMP model. 
 
 
 When  k = 1 k=1 , the dynamics reduces to a random walk. Indeed, writing 
 

 
 (1.16) 
 
 α ⁡ ( B ) ≔ ∑ z ∈ B α z ​ , ​ B ⊆ [ n ] ​ , r x ​ y ​ ( α , w ) = α y ​ ∑ B ∋ x , y w B α ⁡ ( B ) ​ , ​ x ≠ y ​ , \alpha(B)\coloneqq\sum_{z\in B}\alpha_{z}\;\text{,}\;\;B\subseteq[n]\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad r_{xy}(\alpha,w)=\alpha_{y}\sum_{B\ni x,y}\frac{w_{B}}{\alpha(B)}\;\text{,}\;\;x\neq y\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 the identity
 𝔼 U ∼ Dir ⁡ ( α B ) ​ [ U y ] = α y α ⁡ ( B ) \mathbb{E}_{U\sim{\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B})}[U_{y}]=\frac{\alpha_{y}}{\alpha(B)} ,
 y ∈ B y\in B ,
shows that the single particle jumps on  [ n ] [n]  with rates given in ( 1.16 ). For  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , the particles genuinely interact: several may jump during the same block update, and their transition probabilities are sampled from the same random vector  U U . 
 
 
 Just as  ℒ \mathcal{L}  in ( 1.6 ) is a block heat-bath generator with respect to  μ = Dir ⁡ ( α ) \mu={\rm Dir}(\alpha) , the  k k -particle generator  L k L_{k}  admits an analogous interpretation. To identify the corresponding reference measure, let  η ∼ μ \eta\sim\mu  and, conditionally on  η \eta , let  X 1 , … , X k X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}  be independent with common law  η \eta  on  [ n ] [n] . Their joint law  μ k \mu_{k}  on  [ n ] k [n]^{k}  is given, for  x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n] , by 
 

 
 (1.17) 
 
 μ k ( x 1 , … , x k ) ≔ μ ( η x 1 ⋯ η x k ) = Γ ⁡ ( α 0 ) Γ ⁡ ( α 0 + k ) ∏ x ∈ [ n ] Γ ⁡ ( α x + 𝔫 x ​ ( x 1 , … , x k ) ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) , \mu_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\coloneqq\mu(\eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{0}+k)}\prod_{x\in[n]}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+\mathfrak{n}_{x}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}))}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  α 0 ≔ α ⁡ ( [ n ] ) = ∑ x ∈ [ n ] α x \alpha_{0}\coloneqq\alpha([n])=\sum_{x\in[n]}\alpha_{x}  and
 𝔫 x ( x 1 , … , x k ) ≔ ∑ i = 1 k 𝟙 { x i = x } \mathfrak{n}_{x}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathds{1}_{\left\{x_{i}=x\right\}} . Here,  Γ ⁡ ( a ) \Gamma(a) ,  a > 0 a>0 , is the gamma function, satisfying  Γ ⁡ ( a + 1 ) = a ​ Γ ​ ( a ) \Gamma(a+1)=a\Gamma(a) . The last identity in ( 1.17 ) follows by integrating  η x 1 ⋯ η x k \eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}}  against the Dirichlet density and using, for  β x > 0 \beta_{x}>0 , 
 

 
 (1.18) 
 
 ∫ Ω ∏ x ∈ [ n ] η x β x − 1 ​ 𝑑 η = ∏ x ∈ [ n ] Γ ⁡ ( β x ) Γ ⁡ ( ∑ x ∈ [ n ] β x ) ​ , \int_{\Omega}\prod_{x\in[n]}\eta_{x}^{\beta_{x}-1}\,{\rm d}\eta=\frac{\prod_{x\in[n]}\Gamma(\beta_{x})}{\Gamma(\sum_{x\in[n]}\beta_{x})}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  d ​ η {\rm d}\eta  denotes the Lebesgue measure on  Ω \Omega  in the coordinates  ( η 1 , … , η n − 1 ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] n − 1 (\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n-1})\in[0,1]^{n-1} , with  η n = 1 − ∑ x = 1 n − 1 η x \eta_{n}=1-\sum_{x=1}^{n-1}\eta_{x} .
That  L k L_{k}  is indeed the block heat-bath generator with respect to  μ k \mu_{k}  follows from ( 1.17 ) and the corresponding heat-bath property of  ℒ \mathcal{L} . Namely, conditionally on the particles outside a block  B B , the conditional resampling in ( 1.5 ) of  η B \eta^{B}  under  μ \mu  and the identity  μ k ( x 1 , … , x k ) = μ ( η x 1 ⋯ η x k ) \mu_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\mu(\eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}})  imply that the particles lying in  B B  are redistributed independently according to a common random probability vector  U ∼ Dir ⁡ ( α B ) U\sim{\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B}) . This is exactly the  B B -update defining  L k L_{k} . Hence,  μ k \mu_{k}  is reversible for  L k L_{k} , and  L k L_{k}  is self-adjoint on  L 2 ​ ( μ k ) = L 2 ​ ( [ n ] k , μ k ) L^{2}(\mu_{k})=L^{2}([n]^{k},\mu_{k}) . Consequently,  L k L_{k}  has all real eigenvalues. In analogy with ( 1.11 ), we define  gap ⁡ ( L k ) {\rm gap}(L_{k})  as the second smallest eigenvalue of  − L k -L_{k} . 
 
 
 As the representation in ( 1.13 ) depends only on the symmetric part of  ψ : [ n ] k → ℝ \psi:[n]^{k}\to\mathbb{R} , the intertwining relation in ( 1.14 ) is fully informative only for symmetric functions  ψ \psi  in 
 

 
 (1.19) 
 
 ℋ k ≔ { ψ : ψ ( x ς ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , x ς ⁡ ( k ) ) = ψ ( x 1 , … , x k ) for all ς ∈ 𝔖 k , x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] } , \mathscr{H}_{k}\coloneqq\big\{\psi:\psi(x_{\varsigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\varsigma(k)})=\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\ \text{for all}\ \varsigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]\big\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  𝔖 k \mathfrak{S}_{k}  denotes the symmetric group on  [ k ] [k] .
Since  L k L_{k}  clearly commutes with permutations of the labels, this subspace is  L k L_{k} -invariant, and 
 

 
 (1.20) 
 
 L k sym ≔ L k | ℋ k L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}\coloneqq L_{k}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k}} 
 
 
 corresponds to the generator of the unlabeled version of the same particle system. Equivalently,  L k sym L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}  describes the  L k L_{k} -dynamics in which labels are forgotten and only the particle-occupation numbers at each site are retained. 
 
 
 As we shall prove in Proposition  2.3 , the intertwining ( 1.14 ) shows that  ℒ | 𝒫 k \mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}}  and  L k sym L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}  are isospectral, see ( 2.27 ); thus, 
 

 
 (1.21) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 k ) = gap ⁡ ( L k sym ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})={\rm gap}(L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, Theorem  1.1  can be equivalently rewritten as the following chain of identities: 
 

 
 (1.22) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( L k sym ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 k ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 2 ) = gap ⁡ ( L 2 sym ) ​ , k ≥ 2 ​ . {\rm gap}(L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})={\rm gap}(L_{2}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 2\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In other words, for arbitrary positive site weights  α \alpha  and nonnegative block weights  w w , the unlabeled particle systems satisfy a two-particle spectral gap identity. In the special case  α ≡ 1 \alpha\equiv 1 , this is precisely the particle-system identity predicted by  [ AP26 , Conjecture 1.15] ; hence, Theorem  1.1  settles that conjecture. 
 
 
 We now turn to the labeled dynamics. The operator  L k sym L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}  is obtained by restricting  L k L_{k}  to the subspace of symmetric observables; this restriction is proper exactly when  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 . Consequently, the spectrum of  L k sym L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}  is contained in that of  L k L_{k} , and, in particular, 
 

 
 (1.23) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( L k ) ≤ gap ⁡ ( L k sym ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}(L_{k})\leq{\rm gap}(L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Our second main result shows that this inequality always saturates to an identity. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.2   (Discrete KMP) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and all nonnegative block weights  w w , the spectral gap of the discrete  ( k , α , w ) (k,\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model satisfies 
 

 
 (1.24) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( L k ) = gap ⁡ ( L k sym ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}(L_{k})={\rm gap}(L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Together with ( 1.22 ), this yields 
 

 
 (1.25) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( L k ) = gap ⁡ ( L 2 sym ) ​ , k ≥ 2 ​ . {\rm gap}(L_{k})={\rm gap}(L_{2}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 2\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 1.3 . 
 
 The identity in ( 1.25 ) in Theorem  1.2  is a strengthening of Theorem  1.1 : the identity in ( 1.22 ) identifies the gap over symmetric observables as being governed by the two-particle system, while ( 1.25 ) further proves that non-symmetric observables contribute no smaller eigenvalues. 
 
 
 
 The spectral gap identity in ( 1.25 ) shows that at most two particles always suffice to determine the  KMP {\rm KMP}  spectral gap. As we shall see, both this phenomenon and the mechanism behind its proof are remarkably robust, extending to a broad class of stochastic exchange models and their associated particle systems; see Sections  1.3.3  and  7 . 
 
 
 For  KMP {\rm KMP}  models, however, these ideas can be sharpened considerably: according to the weights  α \alpha  and the geometry encoded by  w w , they determine whether the gap is already attained by a degree-one polynomial or instead requires a genuinely degree-two polynomial, thereby revealing a sharp dichotomy between one-particle and two-particle domination. We develop this characterization in the next subsection. 
 
 
 
 
 1.2.  KMP models: one-  vs.  two-particle gaps

 Note that, since  𝒫 k ⊆ 𝒫 k + 1 \mathscr{P}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{k+1} , one has

 Self-adjointness of  ℒ \mathcal{L} , together with the invariance of each  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} , implies  ℒ ​ 𝒫 k , ∗ ⊆ 𝒫 k , ∗ \mathcal{L}\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast} . Consequently, ( 1.11 ) refines to

 Thus, the gap is always either of one-particle type or of genuinely

 1.2.1.  Quantitative comparison 

 
 Our first result gives a quantitative comparison between the two quantities on the right-hand side of ( 1.30 ). 
 
 
 Theorem 1.4 . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and all nonnegative block weights  w w , 
 

 
 (1.31) 
 
 α w , min 1 + α w , min ​ ( 1 + 1 α 0 ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ≤ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ≤ 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ , \frac{\alpha_{w,{\rm min}}}{1+\alpha_{w,{\rm min}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\right){\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\leq{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\leq 2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  α w , min ≔ min B : w B > 0 α ( B ) \alpha_{w,{\rm min}}\coloneqq\min_{B\,:\,w_{B}>0}\alpha(B) . Further, if  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) > 0 {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)>0 , the second inequality is strict. 
 
 
 
 As shown in Remarks  5.9  and  5.13  below, both bounds are sharp: suitable choices of  α \alpha  and  w w  attain either one. Moreover, both multiplicative factors in ( 1.31 ) are strictly positive. Since  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) > 0 {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)>0  if and only if  w w  induces a connected hypergraph, ( 1.30 ) and ( 1.31 ) give a quantitative version of ( 1.9 ). 
 
 
 Since the prefactor in the lower bound is smaller than  1 1 , whereas that in the upper bound equals  2 2 , Theorem  1.4  does not determine whether, for any given  α \alpha  and  w w , 
 

 
 (1.32) 
 
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ≤ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) or gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) > gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\leq{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\qquad\text{or}\qquad{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)>{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Recall that both alternatives occur already in the segment and complete-graph examples from ( 1.3 ) and ( 1.4 ), corresponding, respectively, to 
 

 
 (1.33) 
 
 α ≡ 1 ​ , ​ w B = ∑ x = 1 n − 1  1 B = { x , x + 1 } and α ≡ 1 ​ , ​ w B = 𝟙 | B | = 2 ​ . \alpha\equiv 1\;\text{,}\;\;w_{B}=\textstyle{\sum_{x=1}^{n-1}}\,\mathds{1}_{B=\{x,x+1\}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha\equiv 1\;\text{,}\;\;w_{B}=\mathds{1}_{|B|=2}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, the segment realizes the first alternative in ( 1.32 ), whereas the complete graph realizes the second. In what follows, we classify the possible relations between  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)  and  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)  for general weighted hypergraphs. Remarkably, the segment and the complete graph—and their weighted-hypergraph analogues—emerge as the only two distinct geometries for which one of these quantities uniformly dominates the other. 
 
 
 
 1.2.2.  A phase transition 

 
 First, observe that a full-block update only produces a global shift by  w [ n ] ≥ 0 w_{[n]}\geq 0  in the spectrum of  − ℒ -\mathcal{L}  associated to nonconstant eigenfunctions; in particular, 
 

 
 (1.34) 
 
 gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) = gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ∘ ) + w [ n ] ​ , with ​ w B ∘ = 𝟙 B ≠ [ n ] ​ w B ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w^{\scriptscriptstyle\circ})+w_{[n]}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{with}\ w_{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\circ}=\mathds{1}_{B\neq[n]}\,w_{B}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore, for the sole purpose of comparing  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)  and  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w) , we may assume 
 

 
 (1.35) 
 
 w [ n ] = 0 w_{[n]}=0 
 
 
 with no loss of generality. Next, let  w w  induce a connected hypergraph, namely, 
 

 
 (1.36) 
 
 ∀ N ⊆ [ n ] , N ≠ ∅ , [ n ] , ∃ B ⊆ [ n ] : w B > 0 and B ∩ N ≠ ∅ ≠ B ∩ ( [ n ] ∖ N ) . \forall\,N\subseteq[n],N\neq\varnothing,[n]\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\exists\,B\subseteq[n]:w_{B}>0\ \text{and}\ B\cap N\neq\varnothing\neq B\cap([n]\setminus N)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 It is immediate to verify that ( 1.35 ) and ( 1.36 ) together imply  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 . 
 
 
 When comparing the one- and two-particle gaps, it will be crucial to vary the overall scale of the site weights while preserving their relative proportions. Thus, for fixed  α \alpha  and  w w , we consider the one-parameter family  τ ​ α \tau\alpha ,  τ > 0 \tau>0 . The key point is that this scaling affects the two gaps in fundamentally different ways. Under ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), we shall prove that 
 

 
 (1.37) 
 
 ( 0 , ∞ ) ∋ τ ⟼ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) (0,\infty)\ni\tau\longmapsto{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w) 
 
 
 is strictly increasing for every  α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]} . By contrast, the one-particle dynamics is invariant under this rescaling: since the jump rates ( 1.16 ) depend on  α \alpha  only through ratios, the map  τ ⟼ gap 1 ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) \tau\longmapsto{\rm gap}_{1}(\tau\alpha,w)  is constant; see Remark  5.2 .
The directional monotonicity of ( 1.37 ), established in Proposition  5.1 , is one of the crucial inputs of our argument, and motivates us to analyze the
limits as  τ → 0 \tau\to 0  and  ∞ \infty , for which we show in ( 5.26 ) and ( 5.71 ) that 
 

 
 (1.38) 
 
 lim τ → 0 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ≤ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ≤ lim τ → ∞ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ​ . \lim_{\tau\to 0}{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\leq{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\leq\lim_{\tau\to\infty}{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Altogether, this guarantees the existence of a sharp phase transition separating those instances in which either the one-particle or two-particle gap dominates. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.5   (Phase transition) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and all nonnegative block weights  w w  satisfying ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), there exists a unique  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ [ 0 , ∞ ] \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in[0,\infty]  such that 
 

 
 (1.39) 
 
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) = gap 1 ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) < gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ​ , \displaystyle{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{1}(\tau\alpha,w)<{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad 
 if ​ τ > τ c ​ ( α , w ) ​ , \displaystyle\text{if}\ \tau>\tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 (1.40) 
 
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) = gap 1 ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) > gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ​ , \displaystyle{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{1}(\tau\alpha,w)>{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad 
 if ​ τ < τ c ​ ( α , w ) ​ . \displaystyle\text{if}\ \tau<\tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 The statement above leaves open whether either extremal value  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ { 0 , ∞ } \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in\{0,\infty\}  can occur; we now give a complete geometric characterization of all three possible regimes. 
 
 
 
 1.2.3.  Characterization of the extremal geometries 

 
 The case  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0  is the regime in which the KMP spectral gap is always determined by  gap 1 ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\tau\alpha,w) , whereas  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty  corresponds to the opposite regime, in which it is always determined by  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w) . We shall show that these two extremal cases correspond, respectively, to segment-like and mean-field geometries, while all remaining weighted hypergraphs exhibit a genuine phase transition, with  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in(0,\infty) . This classification applies to general weighted hypergraphs and goes substantially beyond the previously known cases mentioned above, as well as the extension conjectured in  [ CLL20 , Remark 3] . 
 
 
 For clarity, we first state the characterization for weighted graphs (Theorem  1.6 ), where the relevant geometries take a particularly transparent form, and then turn to the general hypergraph setting (Theorem  1.8 ). 
 
 
 
 Graph case. 

 
 Here, we focus on graphs, namely, on weights  w w  supported on size-two blocks: 
 

 
 (1.41) 
 
 w B = 0 ​ , whenever ​ | B | ≠ 2 ​ . w_{B}=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{whenever}\ |B|\neq 2\,\text{.} 
 
 
 For connected graphs (i.e.,  w w  satisfying ( 1.36 ), ( 1.41 )), ( 1.35 ) is equivalent to requiring  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.6   (Graph-version of Theorem  1.8 ) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and for all nonnegative block weights  w w  satisfying ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ) and ( 1.41 ), one has: 
 
 (1)  
 
 τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   w w  induces a segment, i.e., up to a relabeling of  [ n ] [n] , 
 

 
 (1.42) 
 
 w B > 0 if and only if B = { x , x + 1 } ​ , ​ x = 1 , … , n − 1 ​ . w_{B}>0\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad B=\{x,x+1\}\;\text{,}\;\;x=1,\ldots,n-1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow  the weights  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w)  satisfy the mean-field condition: 
 

 
 (1.43) 
 
 w { x , y } α x + α y does not depend on ​ x , y ∈ [ n ] ​ , ​ x ≠ y ​ . \frac{w_{\{x,y\}}}{\alpha_{x}+\alpha_{y}}\qquad\text{does not depend on}\ x,y\in[n]\;\text{,}\;\;x\neq y\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Hence,  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in(0,\infty)  in all remaining cases. 
 
 
 
 Remark 1.7   (Segment  vs.  non-segment) . 
 
 Since the segment condition ( 1.42 ) depends only on the graph weights  w w , whereas the mean-field condition ( 1.43 ) imposes a specific relation between  α \alpha  and  w w , fixing  w w  as in Theorem  1.6  yields the following dichotomy: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Either  w w  induces a segment as in ( 1.42 ), and 
 

 
 (1.44) 
 
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) < gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ , for every ​ α ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) n ​ . {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)<{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{for every}\ \alpha\in(0,\infty)^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 Or  w w  does not induce a segment, and there exist  α + , α − ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) n \alpha^{+},\alpha^{-}\in(0,\infty)^{n}  such that 
 

 
 (1.45) 
 
 gap 1 ​ ( α + , w ) ​ < gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α + , w ) ​ , gap 1 ​ ( α − , w ) > ​ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α − , w ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha^{+},w)<{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha^{+},w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha^{-},w)>{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha^{-},w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Hypergraph case. 

 
 For general hypergraphs satisfying only ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), an analogous picture remains valid after passing to a suitable partition, as we explain below; then,
the extremal regimes  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ { 0 , ∞ } \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in\{0,\infty\}  are characterized, respectively, by hypergraph analogues of the segment and mean-field graphs. 
 
 
 Given block weights  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} , we say that a partition 
 

 
 (1.46) 
 
 [ n ] = N 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N m ​ , 2 ≤ m ≤ n ​ , [n]=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{m}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad 2\leq m\leq n\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 is  w w - compatible  if every block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  is either contained in a single atom  N j N_{j}  or is a union of atoms. Thus, sites belonging to the same atom are always updated together whenever the update involves sites outside that atom. 
 
 
 The discrete partition
 [ n ] = { 1 } ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ { n } [n]=\{1\}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\{n\} 
is always  w w -compatible. Moreover, compatibility depends only on the support of the block weights, and is preserved when positive-weight blocks are removed: if  w B ≥ w B ′ w_{B}\geq w^{\prime}_{B}  for every  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , then every  w w -compatible partition is also  w ′ w^{\prime} -compatible. Further, the join of two  w w -compatible partitions is again  w w -compatible, provided that it has at least two atoms. 
 
 
 If  w w  induces a connected  graph  ( 1.41 ), the discrete partition is in fact the only  w w -compatible one. Indeed, any non-discrete partition contains an atom  N ⊆ [ n ] N\subseteq[n]  with  2 ≤ | N | < n 2\leq|N|<n , and graph-connectedness yields an edge  B = { x , y } B=\{x,y\}  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  such that
 B ∩ N ≠ ∅ ≠ B ∩ ( [ n ] ∖ N ) B\cap N\neq\varnothing\neq B\cap([n]\setminus N) ,
which is incompatible with ( 1.46 ). For general hypergraphs, nontrivial compatible partitions may instead occur. For instance, if 
 

 
 (1.47) 
 
 w B = 𝟙 B = { 1 , … , n ′ } + 𝟙 B = { n ′ , … , n } ​ , 1 < n ′ < n ​ , w_{B}=\mathds{1}_{B=\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}\}}+\mathds{1}_{B=\{n^{\prime},\ldots,n\}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad 1<n^{\prime}<n\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 , then the partition
 [ n ] = { 1 , … , n ′ − 1 } ⊔ { n ′ } ⊔ { n ′ + 1 , … , n } [n]=\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}-1\}\sqcup\{n^{\prime}\}\sqcup\{n^{\prime}+1,\ldots,n\} 
is  w w -compatible. 
 
 
 Given a  w w -compatible partition as in ( 1.46 ), we define
 w ~ = ( w ~ B ) B ⊆ [ n ] \widetilde{w}=(\widetilde{w}_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]}  by discarding the blocks contained in a single atom and retaining those which are unions of at least two atoms: 
 

 
 (1.48) 
 
 w ~ B ≔ { w B if  ​ B = N j 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N j ℓ ​ , ​ ℓ ≥ 2 ​ , 0 otherwise ​ . \widetilde{w}_{B}\coloneqq\begin{cases}w_{B}&\text{if }B=N_{j_{1}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{j_{\ell}}\;\text{,}\;\;\ell\geq 2\;\text{,}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 Under ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), the hypergraph induced by  w ~ \widetilde{w}  is still connected and satisfies  w ~ [ n ] = 0 \widetilde{w}_{[n]}=0 . 
 
 
 Finally, a  coarsest partition  of  w w  is a  w w -compatible partition with the smallest number of atoms. Under ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), the coarsest partition is unique and has  m ≥ 3 m\geq 3 . Indeed, by connectedness, a  w w -compatible partition with  m = 2 m=2  would force any positive-weight block crossing the two atoms to be  [ n ] [n] , contradicting  w [ n ] = 0 w_{[n]}=0 ; uniqueness then follows by taking the join of two coarsest partitions. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.8   (Hypergraph-version of Theorem  1.6 ) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and for all nonnegative block weights  w w  satisfying ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), one has: 
 
 (1)  
 
 τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   w w  is segment-like, i.e., by taking its coarsest partition ( 1.46 )–( 1.48 ) and an appropriate relabeling of  [ m ] [m] , 
 

 
 (1.49) 
 
 w ~ B > 0 implies B = N i ⊔ N i + 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N j − 1 ⊔ N j ​ , ​   1 ≤ i < j ≤ m ​ . \widetilde{w}_{B}>0\qquad\text{implies}\qquad B=N_{i}\sqcup N_{i+1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{j-1}\sqcup N_{j}\;\text{,}\;\;1\leq i<j\leq m\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   ( α , w ) (\alpha,w)  is mean-field, i.e., for its coarsest partition ( 1.46 )–( 1.48 ), 
 

 
 (1.50) 
 
 c i ​ j = c i ​ j ​ ( α , w ) ≔ ∑ B ⊇ N i ⊔ N j w ~ B α ⁡ ( B ) does not depend on ​ i , j ∈ [ m ] ​ , ​ i ≠ j ​ . c_{ij}=c_{ij}(\alpha,w)\coloneqq\sum_{B\supseteq N_{i}\sqcup N_{j}}\frac{\widetilde{w}_{B}}{\alpha(B)}\qquad\text{does not depend on}\ i,j\in[m]\;\text{,}\;\;i\neq j\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Hence,  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in(0,\infty)  in all remaining cases. 
 
 
 
 Remark 1.9   (From hypergraphs to graphs) . 
 
 As already mentioned, for graph weights  w w  (i.e., satisfying ( 1.41 )) on  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3  sites, connectedness ( 1.36 ) forces the discrete partition  [ n ] = { 1 } ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ { n } [n]=\{1\}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\{n\}  to be the coarsest one. Hence, Theorem  1.6  indeed follows from Theorem  1.8 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 1.3.  Related models and extensions 

 
 We next place the main spectral gap identity for the KMP model, established in Theorem  1.1 , in a broader context.
We first discuss analogous Aldous-type spectral gap phenomena for Kac’s walk on the sphere and heat-bath dynamics for cone measures, systems whose structure is richer than that of the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model. We then establish a degree-one spectral gap identity for the non-conservative  KMP {\rm KMP}  model and, finally, show that our main theorem extends to a broad class of stochastic exchange models. 
 
 
 1.3.1.  Kac’s walk and generalizations 

 
 Kac’s walk on the sphere is the continuous-time Markov process on 
 

 
 (1.51) 
 
 𝕊 ≔ { v ∈ ℝ n : ∑ x ∈ [ n ] v i 2 = 1 } ⊆ ℝ n ​ , \mathbb{S}\coloneqq\left\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\textstyle\sum_{x\in[n]}v_{i}^{2}=1\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 defined as follows. Each unordered pair of coordinates  { x , y } \{x,y\}  is updated independently at rate  1 1 . When an update occurs at the pair  { x , y } \{x,y\} , the vector  ( v x , v y ) (v_{x},v_{y})  is replaced by a new pair  ( v x ′ , v y ′ ) (v_{x}^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{y}) , chosen uniformly on the circle 
 

 
 (1.52) 
 
 { ( v x ′ , v y ′ ) ∈ ℝ 2 : ( v x ′ ) 2 + ( v y ′ ) 2 = v x 2 + v y 2 } ​ . \{(v_{x}^{\prime},v_{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(v_{x}^{\prime})^{2}+(v_{y}^{\prime})^{2}=v_{x}^{2}+v_{y}^{2}\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The process is reversible with respect to the uniform distribution on  𝕊 \mathbb{S} .
Introduced by Kac in  [ Kac56 ]  as a mean-field toy model for energy-preserving collisions in kinetic theory, this process has since been studied extensively.
It was shown in  [ CCL03 ]  that the spectral gap equals  λ = ( n + 2 ) / 4 \lambda=(n+2)/4  with eigenfunction 
 

 
 (1.53) 
 
 v ⟼ ∑ i = 1 n ( v i 4 − 3 n ⁡ ( n + 2 ) ) , v\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(v_{i}^{4}-\tfrac{3}{n(n+2)}\right)\,, 
 
 
 a polynomial of degree 4 in the velocity variables  v = ( v x ) x ∈ [ n ] v=(v_{x})_{x\in[n]} .
We also refer to  [ Cap08 ]  for an alternative computation of the spectral gap, and to  [ Mas03 ]  for a characterization of all eigenvalues of this process. 
 
 
 In this work, we consider a generalization of Kac’s walk in two directions. First, the complete-graph geometry is replaced by a weighted hypergraph, allowing updates on arbitrary blocks and with arbitrary rate. Second, the Euclidean sphere  𝕊 ⊆ ℝ n \mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}  is replaced by the inhomogeneous  ℓ p n \ell_{p}^{n} -type sphere 
 

 
 (1.54) 
 
 𝕊 p ≔ { ζ ∈ ℝ n : ∑ x ∈ [ n ] | ζ x | p x = 1 } ⊆ ℝ n ​ , \mathbb{S}_{p}\coloneqq\left\{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\textstyle\sum_{x\in[n]}|\zeta_{x}|^{p_{x}}=1\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  p = ( p x ) x ∈ [ n ] p=(p_{x})_{x\in[n]}  is an arbitrary vector with positive entries. 
 
 
 We equip  𝕊 p \mathbb{S}_{p}  with the probability distribution  κ \kappa  given as follows. Let  Z = ( Z x ) x ∈ [ n ] Z=(Z_{x})_{x\in[n]}  be independent real random variables with densities proportional to  z ↦ exp ⁡ ( − | z | p x ) z\mapsto\exp(-|z|^{p_{x}}) , and define 
 

 
 (1.55) 
 
 Φ p ​ ( Z ) ≔ ∑ y ∈ [ n ] | Z y | p y ​ , ζ x ≔ Z x Φ p ​ ( Z ) 1 / p x ​ , ​ x ∈ [ n ] ​ . \Phi_{p}(Z)\coloneqq\sum_{y\in[n]}|Z_{y}|^{p_{y}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\zeta_{x}\coloneqq\frac{Z_{x}}{\Phi_{p}(Z)^{1/p_{x}}}\;\text{,}\;\;x\in[n]\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore,  ζ ∈ 𝕊 p \zeta\in\mathbb{S}_{p}  by construction, and its law  κ \kappa  is referred to as
the  cone measure  on  𝕊 p \mathbb{S}_{p} ; see, e.g.,  [ CS26 ]  or  [ BGMN05 ]  for a discussion of the homogeneous cases  p ≡ const . > 0 p\equiv{\rm const.}>0 . We note that when  p ≡ 2 p\equiv 2  one recovers the uniform distribution over  𝕊 \mathbb{S} . 
 
 
 For nonnegative block weights  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} , define the  ( p , w ) (p,w) -Gibbs sampler, or heat-bath dynamics, for the cone measure, as the Markov
process on  𝕊 p \mathbb{S}_{p}  with infinitesimal generator 
 

 
 (1.56) 
 
 𝒢 ​ h ​ ( ζ ) ≔ ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ( κ B ​ h ​ ( ζ ) − h ⁡ ( ζ ) ) ​ , κ B ​ h ​ ( ζ ) ≔ κ ⁡ ( h ∣ ζ B c ) ​ . \mathcal{G}h(\zeta)\coloneqq\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\left(\kappa_{B}h(\zeta)-h(\zeta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\kappa_{B}h(\zeta)\coloneqq\kappa(h\mid\zeta^{B^{c}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 with  ζ ∈ 𝕊 p \zeta\in\mathbb{S}_{p}  and  h : 𝕊 p → ℝ h:\mathbb{S}_{p}\to\mathbb{R}  bounded and measurable.
That is, with rate  w B w_{B} , the block variables  ζ B = ( ζ x ) x ∈ B \zeta^{B}=(\zeta_{x})_{x\in B}  are resampled according to the conditional distribution  κ \kappa  given the variables  ζ B c = ( ζ x ) x ∉ B \zeta^{B^{c}}=(\zeta_{x})_{x\notin B} . The generator  𝒢 \mathcal{G}  is, thus, a bounded self-adjoint operator in  L 2 ​ ( κ ) = L 2 ​ ( 𝕊 p , κ ) L^{2}(\kappa)=L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{p},\kappa) . Observe that the case  p ≡ 2 p\equiv 2  and  w B = 𝟙 | B | = 2 w_{B}=\mathds{1}_{|B|=2}  corresponds to Kac’s walk on the sphere. 
 
 
 For general  p = ( p x ) x ∈ [ n ] p=(p_{x})_{x\in[n]}  and  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} , this dynamics admits a natural projection, under which it reduces to a KMP dynamics. More precisely, define, for all  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] , 
 

 
 (1.57) 
 
 η x ≔ | ζ x | p x ​ , σ x ≔ sign ⁡ ( ζ x ) ​ . \eta_{x}\coloneqq|\zeta_{x}|^{p_{x}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\sigma_{x}\coloneqq{\rm sign}(\zeta_{x})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In this context, we refer to  η = ( η x ) x ∈ [ n ] \eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in[n]}  and  σ = ( σ x ) x ∈ [ n ] \sigma=(\sigma_{x})_{x\in[n]}  as the energy and spin/angle variables of  ζ \zeta , respectively.
On the one hand, when  ζ ∼ κ \zeta\sim\kappa , the corresponding energy variables  η \eta  belong to  Ω \Omega  and satisfy 
 

 
 (1.58) 
 
 η = ( η x ) x ∈ [ n ] ∼ Dir ⁡ ( α ) ​ , σ = ( σ x ) x ∈ [ n ] ​ i.i.d. uniform on  ​ { ± 1 } ​ , η ⟂ ⟂ σ , \eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in[n]}\sim{\rm Dir}(\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\sigma=(\sigma_{x})_{x\in[n]}\ \text{i.i.d.\ uniform on }\{\pm 1\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\perp\!\!\!\perp\sigma\,, 
 
 
 where  α x = 1 / p x \alpha_{x}=1/p_{x} ,  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] , and where  η ⟂ ⟂ σ \eta\perp\!\!\!\perp\sigma  indicates that  η \eta  and  σ \sigma  are independent.
Indeed, the random variables  | Z x | p x |Z_{x}|^{p_{x}}  are independent  Gamma ⁡ ( α x , 1 ) {\rm Gamma}(\alpha_{x},1) ; thus, their normalization by  Φ p ​ ( Z ) \Phi_{p}(Z)  gives the law
 Dir ⁡ ( α ) {\rm Dir}(\alpha) .
On the other hand, under the map  ζ ↦ η \zeta\mapsto\eta , the  ( p , w ) (p,w) -Gibbs sampler in ( 1.56 ) projects exactly onto the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model in ( 1.5 ) with parameters  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) . Consequently, for this choice of parameters,
 spec ⁡ ( ℒ ) ⊆ spec ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) {\rm spec}(\mathcal{L})\subseteq{\rm spec}(\mathcal{G}) .
Thus, the spectral gap of the Gibbs sampler, referred to as  gap ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{G}) , is at most the spectral gap of  ℒ \mathcal{L} , namely  gap ⁡ ( ℒ ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 2 ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})  as in Theorem  1.1 . We shall establish that they coincide. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.10   (Gibbs sampler for cone measure) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  p p  and all nonnegative block weights  w w , the spectral gap of the  ( p , w ) (p,w) -Gibbs sampler on  𝕊 p \mathbb{S}_{p}  satisfies 
 

 
 (1.59) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ | 𝒫 2 ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{G})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ℒ \mathcal{L}  is the generator of the  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model with  α x = 1 / p x \alpha_{x}=1/p_{x} ,  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] . Moreover, it is attained by a polynomial eigenfunction of degree at most  2 2  in the energy variables  η x = | ζ x | p x \eta_{x}=|\zeta_{x}|^{p_{x}} . 
 
 
 
 Combined with Theorem  1.6 1  and Remark  1.7 , this shows also that, in the graph case ( 1.41 ), the spectral gap of the  ( p , w ) (p,w) -Gibbs sampler on  𝕊 p \mathbb{S}_{p} 
is attained by a linear function in the energy variables  η x = | ζ x | p x \eta_{x}=|\zeta_{x}|^{p_{x}}  for every choice of positive weights  p p  if and only if  w w  is a weighted segment. To the best of our knowledge, this and analogous claims which may be deduced by combining Theorems  1.4 ,  1.6 ,  1.8  and  1.10  are new even in the special case of Kac’s walk corresponding to  p ≡ 2 p\equiv 2 , for which previously only the homogeneous complete graph case ( 1.53 ) was understood. 
 
 
 
 1.3.2.  Boundary-driven  KMP {\rm KMP} 

 
 Consider the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model in which, in addition to the conservative exchange dynamics ( 1.5 )–( 1.6 ), the system exchanges energy with external reservoirs.
Fix parameters  ω x ≥ 0 \omega_{x}\geq 0  and  β x , ρ x > 0 \beta_{x},\rho_{x}>0 , for  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] . At rate  ω x \omega_{x} , the energy at  x x  is updated according to 
 

 
 (1.60) 
 
 η x ⟼ U x ​ ( η x + ξ x ) ​ , \eta_{x}\longmapsto U_{x}\left(\eta_{x}+\xi_{x}\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 while all other coordinates remain unchanged; at each such update, the pair  ( U x , ξ x ) (U_{x},\xi_{x})  is sampled independently of the current configuration and of all previous updates, with 
 

 
 (1.61) 
 
 ( U x , ξ x ) ∼ Beta ⁡ ( α x , β x ) ⊗ Gamma ⁡ ( β x , ρ x ) ​ . (U_{x},\xi_{x})\sim{\rm Beta}(\alpha_{x},\beta_{x})\otimes{\rm Gamma}(\beta_{x},\rho_{x})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  Gamma ⁡ ( β , ρ ) {\rm Gamma}(\beta,\rho)  denotes the gamma distribution whose density is proportional to
 s ↦ s β − 1 e − s / ρ s\mapsto s^{\beta-1}e^{-s/\rho}  on  [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) . We write  𝐄 x \mathbf{E}_{x}  for expectation with respect to the product law in ( 1.61 ). 
 
 
 Letting  η x , U x , ξ x \eta^{x,U_{x},\xi_{x}}  denote the configuration obtained from  η \eta  through ( 1.60 ), the reservoir generator is 
 

 
 (1.62) 
 
 ℒ ∂ f ( η ) = ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ω x ( 𝐄 x [ f ( η x , U x , ξ x ) ] − f ( η ) ) , η ∈ Ω bd ≔ [ 0 , ∞ ) n , \mathcal{L}_{\partial}f(\eta)=\sum_{x\in[n]}\omega_{x}\,(\mathbf{E}_{x}\big[f(\eta^{x,U_{x},\xi_{x}})\big]-f(\eta))\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega_{\rm bd}\coloneqq[0,\infty)^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  f f  is a bounded measurable function on  Ω bd \Omega_{\rm bd} . The boundary-driven KMP generator is 
 

 
 (1.63) 
 
 ℒ bd = ℒ + ℒ ∂ ​ . \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}=\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{L}_{\partial}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  ℒ \mathcal{L}  denotes the conservative  KMP {\rm KMP}  generator associated with some block weights  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]}  and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]} , introduced on  Ω \Omega  in ( 1.6 ) and naturally extended to  Ω bd \Omega_{\rm bd}  through the same update rules. 
 
 
 Assume that the hypergraph induced by  w w  is connected and that  ω ≢ 0 \omega\not\equiv 0 . In this general setting, it is possible to show that the boundary-driven process admits a unique invariant measure. However, we omit a detailed proof of this fact, as it will not be used in the proof of Theorem  1.11  below. In the special case in which  ρ x ≡ ρ ∗ \rho_{x}\equiv\rho_{\ast}  is constant on  supp ⁡ ( ω ) {\rm supp}(\omega) , one can check that the process admits an explicit, product, reversible measure, given by 
 

 
 (1.64) 
 
 ν = ⨂ x ∈ [ n ] ​ Gamma ​ ( α x , ρ ∗ ) ​ . \nu=\underset{x\in[n]}{\bigotimes}{\rm Gamma}(\alpha_{x},\rho_{\ast})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, whenever  ρ x ≡ ρ ∗ \rho_{x}\equiv\rho_{\ast} ,  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  is self-adjoint on  L 2 ​ ( ν ) = L 2 ​ ( Ω bd , ν ) L^{2}(\nu)=L^{2}(\Omega_{\rm bd},\nu) .
If instead  ρ \rho  is non-constant on  supp ⁡ ( ω ) {\rm supp}(\omega) , the process is non-reversible, and its invariant measure—usually referred to as its nonequilibrium steady state—is generally neither in product form nor explicit.
Several important questions concerning this measure remain open, even on the segment. For further details, we refer to  [ DMFG24 ,  GRT25 ] , where the choice  β x = α x \beta_{x}=\alpha_{x}  is considered, and to the original KMP work  [ KMP82 ] , corresponding to the full-refresh update with  β x = ∞ \beta_{x}=\infty . We return to these cases and further possible generalizations in Remark  6.5  below. 
 
 
 For every  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , let  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  denote the finite-dimensional space of polynomials of degree at most  k k  in the variables  η ∈ Ω bd \eta\in\Omega_{\rm bd} ; when restricting to  Ω ⊆ Ω bd \Omega\subseteq\Omega_{\rm bd} ,  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  returns  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  from Section  1.1 . It follows directly from the update rules that 
 

 
 (1.65) 
 
 ℒ bd ​ 𝒬 k ⊆ 𝒬 k ​ , k ≥ 0 ​ . \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}\mathscr{Q}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{Q}_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, the conservative updates preserve polynomial degree (cf. ( 1.10 )), while a reservoir update ( 1.60 )–( 1.61 ) maps a polynomial of degree at most  k k  into another polynomial of either equal or lower degree. Hence, the restriction of  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  to  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  is a finite-dimensional operator and admits polynomial eigenfunctions. 
 
 
 When  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  is self-adjoint, the invariance in ( 1.65 ) also yields the invariance of the orthogonal polynomial layers 
 

 
 (1.66) 
 
 𝒬 k , ∗ ≔ 𝒬 k ∩ 𝒬 k − 1 ⟂ ν , k ≥ 1 . \mathscr{Q}_{k,\ast}\coloneqq\mathscr{Q}_{k}\cap\mathscr{Q}_{k-1}^{\perp_{\nu}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Together with the density of polynomials in  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu) , this allows one to fully recover the  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu)  spectrum from polynomial eigenfunctions.
This argument breaks down in nonequilibrium. Indeed, although  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  remains invariant, the orthogonal layer  𝒬 k , ∗ \mathscr{Q}_{k,\ast}  need not be invariant. Moreover, the restriction of  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  to  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  need not be self-adjoint or even diagonalizable, and its eigenvalues need not exhaust the spectrum of  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  on  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu) . 
 
 
 Regardless of this issue, one may always consider the polynomial spectrum
 spec ⁡ ( − ℒ bd | 𝒬 ) {\rm spec}(-\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}}) ,
 𝒬 ≔ ∪ k ≥ 0 𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}\coloneqq\cup_{k\geq 0}\,\mathscr{Q}_{k} , defined as the collection of all eigenvalues of  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  on  𝒬 \mathscr{Q} , counted with multiplicity.
We shall prove that this spectrum is real, nonnegative and independent of  ρ \rho . Accordingly, we define the polynomial spectral gap by 
 

 
 (1.67) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) ≔ inf k ≥ 1 gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd | 𝒬 k ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})\coloneqq\inf_{k\geq 1}{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{k}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd | 𝒬 k ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{k}})  denotes the second smallest eigenvalue of  − ℒ bd | 𝒬 k -\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{k}} .
In the reversible case,  gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})  coincides with the usual  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu) -spectral gap.
Our main finding is that, in contrast with the conservative setting of Theorem  1.1 , this gap is always attained by a polynomial of degree one. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.11   (Boundary-driven KMP) . 
 
 For all positive weights
 α , β , ρ \alpha,\beta,\rho 
and all non-negative weights
 w w  and  ω \omega ,
one has 
 

 
 (1.68) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd | 𝒬 1 ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular,  gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})  does not depend on  ρ \rho . 
 
 
 
 As in the conservative case, this result admits a particle-system interpretation: the relevant intertwining now involves the particle dynamics from Section  1.1.2  with an additional killing of particles at reservoir sites. We refer to Section  6  for further details. 
 
 
 
 1.3.3.  Stochastic exchange models 

 
 The arguments used to prove Theorems  1.1  and  1.2  for the conservative  KMP {\rm KMP}  model extend to a considerably broader class of processes, which we refer to as  stochastic exchange models ; see also  [ KQS25 ,  CQS25 ,  CKMP25 ] . Roughly speaking, these are Markov processes on  Ω \Omega  whose updates redistribute the energy through multiplication with random stochastic matrices. 
 
 
 More specifically, let  Υ \Upsilon  be a Borel measure on the space of row-stochastic matrices  M = ( M x ​ y ) x , y ∈ [ n ] ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] n × n M=(M_{xy})_{x,y\in[n]}\in[0,1]^{n\times n} . The dynamics on  Ω \Omega  is formally described, for  f : Ω → ℝ f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R} , by 
 

 
 (1.69) 
 
 ℒ Υ ​ f ​ ( η ) ≔ ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η ​ M ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ , η ∈ Ω ​ . \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}f(\eta)\coloneqq\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)\left(f(\eta M)-f(\eta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  η ​ M \eta M  denotes row-vector–matrix multiplication; in particular,  η ​ M ∈ Ω \eta M\in\Omega  whenever  η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega .
We defer the precise construction of stochastic exchange models and further properties to Section  7 . For now, let us simply note that the  KMP {\rm KMP}  generator in ( 1.6 ) corresponds to (cf. ( 1.5 )) 
 

 
 (1.70) 
 
 Υ = Υ KMP = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ Law ​ ( K B U ) ​ , U = ( U x ) x ∈ B ∼ Dir ⁡ ( α B ) ​ , \Upsilon=\Upsilon^{\rm\scriptscriptstyle KMP}=\textstyle{\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}}\,w_{B}\,{\rm Law}(K_{B}^{U})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad U=(U_{x})_{x\in B}\sim{\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  K B U ∈ ℝ n × n K_{B}^{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}  is the random row-stochastic matrix such that, for every  η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega , 
 

 
 (1.71) 
 
 ( η ​ K B U ) x = η ⁡ ( B ) ​ U x ​ , ​ x ∈ B ​ , ( η ​ K B U ) x = η x ​ , ​ x ∉ B ​ . (\eta K_{B}^{U})_{x}=\eta(B)U_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;x\in B\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad(\eta K_{B}^{U})_{x}=\eta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;x\notin B\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Observe that the  B × B B\times B  block of  K B U ∈ ℝ n × n K_{B}^{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}  has identical rows, each equal to  U = ( U x ) x ∈ B U=(U_{x})_{x\in B} . 
 
 
 As further examples, we anticipate that suitable variants of the  KMP {\rm KMP}  redistribution law in ( 1.70 ) yield: 
 
 •  
 
 the  averaging process  considered in  [ AL12 ,  QS23 ] , obtained by taking the redistribution vector to be deterministic, with  U x = α x α ⁡ ( B ) U_{x}=\frac{\alpha_{x}}{\alpha(B)}  for  x ∈ B x\in B ; 
 
 •  
 
 a block version of the  immediate exchange model  introduced in  [ HP14 ] , obtained by taking the rows of the  B × B B\times B  update block to be independent—rather than identical—Dirichlet vectors with suitable parameters; see Section  7.4.2 . 
 
 
 In all these examples,  Υ \Upsilon  is finite, and the corresponding generator  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  is bounded on  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathcal{C}(\Omega) , the space of continuous functions on  Ω \Omega  endowed with the uniform norm. There are, however, relevant models described by an infinite measure  Υ \Upsilon , most notably the  harmonic process   [ FG22 ] ; see Section  7.4.3 . Each of the examples just mentioned admits a Dirichlet distribution as a reversible measure; for the averaging process, this degenerates to a Dirac mass. 
 
 
 To encompass all the above examples, we always impose, as in  [ KQS25 ] , the condition 
 

 
 (1.72) 
 
 ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ M x ​ y < ∞ ​ , for every ​ x , y ∈ [ n ] ​ , ​ x ≠ y ​ . \textstyle\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)M_{xy}<\infty\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for every}\ x,y\in[n]\;\text{,}\;\;x\neq y\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This guarantees that ( 1.69 ) generates a Feller process on  Ω \Omega . By compactness, an invariant probability measure always exists (Remark  7.2 ), while Theorem  1.12  and Proposition  7.6  provide explicit equivalent
criteria for its uniqueness. 
 
 
 One key common feature of all these stochastic exchange models is that, under ( 1.72 ), the linearity of the updates in ( 1.69 ) guarantees 
 

 
 (1.73) 
 
 ℒ Υ ​ 𝒫 k ⊆ 𝒫 k ​ , k ≥ 0 ​ , \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}\mathscr{P}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  is the same space introduced in Section  1.1 , namely, the space of polynomials of degree at most  k k  in the variables  η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega .
As in the preceding discussion on the boundary-driven  KMP {\rm KMP}  model, the eigenvalues obtained by diagonalizing  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  on the finite-dimensional spaces  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  need not be in one-to-one correspondence with the spectrum of any  L 2 L^{2}  realization of the generator. Nevertheless, we may define the polynomial spectral gap by 
 

 
 (1.74) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) ≔ inf k ≥ 1 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})\coloneqq\inf_{k\geq 1}{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})  is the smallest real part among the eigenvalues of  − ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k -\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}}  after removing one copy of the zero eigenvalue corresponding to constant functions, with algebraic multiplicities taken into account.
With these definitions, the degree-two reduction established for the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model in Theorem  1.1  in fact holds throughout the whole class of stochastic exchange models. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.12   (Stochastic exchange models) . 
 
 For every Borel measure  Υ \Upsilon  satisfying ( 1.72 ), 
 

 
 (1.75) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 2 ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Moreover,  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  admits a unique invariant measure precisely when  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) > 0 {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})>0 . 
 
 
 
 We stated the above result for restrictions to polynomial observables. If, in addition, the process is reversible with respect to a probability measure which is absolutely continuous with respect to the uniform measure on  Ω \Omega , then distinct polynomial functions on  Ω \Omega  define distinct elements of the corresponding  L 2 L^{2} -space. Since polynomials are dense in this space, self-adjointness yields a complete orthonormal basis of polynomial eigenfunctions. Consequently,  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})  coincides with the usual  L 2 L^{2} -spectral gap. This applies, in particular, whenever the reversible measure is a nondegenerate Dirichlet distribution, thus, to the immediate exchange model and harmonic process; see Corollaries  7.9  and  7.11 . 
 
 
 We provide further motivation for this general spectral gap reduction in the next section, where we outline the key steps of its proof. 
 
 
 
 
 1.4.  Hidden models, proof strategy, and further remarks 

 
 As for the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model, every stochastic exchange model as introduced in Section  1.3.3  admits a natural particle representation. Consequently, the spectral problems for polynomial eigenfunctions underlying Theorem  1.1  and its extension in Theorem  1.12  can be equivalently reformulated in terms of eigenpairs of the associated finite-particle systems; see ( 7.30 ). The most effective viewpoint for proving the degree-two reductions in those theorems, however, comes from a different representation: the  hidden  ( temperature  or  parameter )  model , first identified for  KMP {\rm KMP}  in  [ DMFG24 ] ; see also  [ GRT25 ,  KQS25 ]  for subsequent developments and extensions. 
 
 
 1.4.1.  Hidden models 

 
 To introduce the hidden model in an intuitive way, first observe that the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model—as any other stochastic exchange model with generator  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  as in ( 1.69 )—is built from elementary random matrix updates of the form 
 

 
 (1.76) 
 
 η ⟼ η ​ M ​ , \eta\longmapsto\eta M\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  η ∈ Ω ⊆ ℝ n \eta\in\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n} , viewed as a row vector, is a probability distribution on  [ n ] [n] , and  M ∈ ℝ n × n M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}  is row-stochastic. For instance, the  KMP {\rm KMP}  update in ( 1.2 ), restricted to the coordinates  x , y = x + 1 ∈ [ n ] x,y=x+1\in[n]  (as the others remain unchanged), can be written as 
 

 
 (1.77) 
 
 ( η x , η y ) ⟼ ( η x , η y ) ​ ( U 1 − U U 1 − U ) = ( U ⁡ ( η x + η y ) , ( 1 − U ) ​ ( η x + η y ) ) ​ . (\eta_{x},\eta_{y})\longmapsto(\eta_{x},\eta_{y})\begin{pmatrix}U&1-U\\
U&1-U\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}U(\eta_{x}+\eta_{y}),(1-U)(\eta_{x}+\eta_{y})\end{pmatrix}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, at any time  t > 0 t>0 , after  ℓ = ℓ ⁡ ( t ) \ell=\ell(t)  updates ( 1.76 ) with matrices  M 1 , … , M ℓ M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell} , the energy configuration is
 η M 1 ⋯ M ℓ \eta M_{1}\cdots M_{\ell} .
Crucially, the update times and matrices are sampled independently of the current energy configuration. The same sequence may therefore be read in reverse chronological order and applied, by left multiplication, to a column vector, still yielding a Markov dynamics. 
 
 
 This backward evolution is precisely the  hidden model : its configurations are column vectors  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , and each update takes the form 
 

 
 (1.78) 
 
 θ ⟼ M ​ θ ​ , \theta\longmapsto M\theta\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 so that the same row-stochastic matrix  M M  now acts on the hidden-model configuration from the left.
In particular, the matrix representation of the  KMP {\rm KMP}  update in ( 1.77 ) gives the corresponding  θ \theta -variable update 
 

 
 (1.79) 
 
 ( θ x θ y ) ⟼ ( U 1 − U U 1 − U ) ​ ( θ x θ y ) = ( U ​ θ x + ( 1 − U ) ​ θ y U ​ θ x + ( 1 − U ) ​ θ y ) ​ . \begin{pmatrix}\theta_{x}\\
\theta_{y}\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}U&1-U\\
U&1-U\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{x}\\
\theta_{y}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}U\theta_{x}+(1-U)\theta_{y}\\
U\theta_{x}+(1-U)\theta_{y}\end{pmatrix}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Observe that, whereas the forward update in ( 1.77 ) redistributes the conserved total energy  η x + η y \eta_{x}+\eta_{y}  between the two sites, the hidden-model update in ( 1.79 ) replaces  θ x \theta_{x}  and  θ y \theta_{y}  by the same random convex combination and, in general, does not preserve their sum. The same description extends to the  KMP {\rm KMP}  hidden model on a weighted hypergraph: whenever a block is updated, all its coordinates are replaced by a common random convex combination.
Thus, for the  KMP {\rm KMP}  hidden-model dynamics, 
 
 (1)  
 
 every flat configuration  s ​ 𝟏 s\mathbf{1} ,  s ∈ ℝ s\in\mathbb{R} , is invariant; 
 
 (2)  
 
 the minimum coordinate is nondecreasing and the maximum coordinate is nonincreasing, so every hypercube  [ a , b ] n [a,b]^{n} ,  − ∞ < a < b < ∞ -\infty<a<b<\infty , is invariant; 
 
 (3)  
 
 the total mass need not be conserved. 
 
 
 
 
 The properties in  1 – 3  are not specific to the hidden model of the  KMP {\rm KMP} , but are shared by the hidden model of any stochastic exchange model from Section  1.3.3 . Indeed, every row-stochastic matrix  M M  satisfies  M ​ 𝟏 = 𝟏 M\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}  and each coordinate of  M ​ θ M\theta  is a convex combination of the coordinates of  θ \theta , yielding respectively the invariance of flat configurations in  1  and the maximum principle in  2 . Moreover, in general, 
 

 
 (1.80) 
 
 ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ( M ​ θ ) x ≠ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] θ x ​ , \textstyle\sum_{x\in[n]}(M\theta)_{x}\neq\sum_{x\in[n]}\theta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 so the hidden-model dynamics is typically non-conservative, i.e.,  3 . 
 
 
 Under an appropriate connectivity assumption, the hidden-model dynamics converges, in the long run, to a flat configuration with a possibly random height. Although the degeneracy of these steady states may at first appear as a complication, it is precisely what makes the hidden-model representation effective for the spectral gap problem. 
 
 
 
 1.4.2.  Eigenvalue problem for hidden models 

 
 The original  KMP {\rm KMP}  model, its particle representation, and its hidden model all encode the same polynomial eigenvalue problem. Indeed, for given weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]}  and  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} ,  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , and  ψ ∈ ℋ k \psi\in\mathscr{H}_{k}  as defined in ( 1.19 ), next to the  η \eta -variable polynomial  ψ ^ ∈ 𝒫 k \widehat{\psi}\in\mathscr{P}_{k}  introduced in ( 1.13 ), we also define 
 

 
 (1.81) 
 
 ψ ~ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] μ k ( x 1 , … , x k ) ψ ( x 1 , … , x k ) θ x 1 ⋯ θ x k , θ ∈ ℝ n , \widetilde{\psi}(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]}\mu_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\,\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\,\theta_{x_{1}}\cdots\theta_{x_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  μ k \mu_{k}  is the reversible measure of the  k k -particle system; see ( 1.17 ). Comparing  ψ ~ \widetilde{\psi}  in ( 1.81 ) with  ψ ^ \widehat{\psi}  in ( 1.13 ), the two polynomials differ only by the additional coefficient  μ k ​ ( x 1 , … , x k ) \mu_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})  attached to each monomial. 
 
 
 Recall that  ℒ \mathcal{L}  denotes the generator of the  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model, while  L k L_{k}  is the generator of the associated  k k -particle system. As seen in ( 1.14 ), the map  ψ ↦ ψ ^ \psi\mapsto\widehat{\psi}  on  ℋ k \mathscr{H}_{k}  intertwines these two generators; the map  ψ ↦ ψ ~ \psi\mapsto\widetilde{\psi}  in ( 1.81 ) yields the corresponding relation with the hidden-model dynamics. More precisely, if  ℒ \mathscr{L}  denotes the generator of the hidden model, then 
 

 
 (1.82) 
 
 ℒ ​ ψ ^ = L k ​ ψ ^ and ℒ ​ ψ ~ = L k ​ ψ ~ ​ , for every ​ ψ ∈ ℋ k ​ . \mathcal{L}\widehat{\psi}=\widehat{L_{k}\psi}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathscr{L}\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{L_{k}\psi}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for every}\ \psi\in\mathscr{H}_{k}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In view of these intertwining relations, we shall prove that, for every  λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0  and  ψ ∈ ℋ k \psi\in\mathscr{H}_{k} , 
 

 
 (1.83) 
 
 ℒ ψ ^ = − λ ψ ^ ⟺ L k ψ = − λ ψ ⟺ ℒ ψ ~ = − λ ψ ~ . \mathcal{L}\widehat{\psi}=-\lambda\widehat{\psi}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad L_{k}\psi=-\lambda\psi\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\mathscr{L}\widetilde{\psi}=-\lambda\widetilde{\psi}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  ψ \psi ,  ψ ^ \widehat{\psi} , and  ψ ~ \widetilde{\psi}  may only vanish simultaneously, the eigenvalue problem may be formulated interchangeably for the  KMP {\rm KMP}  generator, the  k k -particle system on symmetric observables, or the hidden model. 
 
 
 In view of the degeneracy of the hidden model steady states, the last formulation in ( 1.83 ) has the advantage of translating the problem of identifying the optimal  L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu)  convergence rate of the  KMP {\rm KMP}  dynamics toward its Dirichlet equilibrium, as in ( 1.8 ), into the more tractable task of quantifying the decay of spatial fluctuations in the hidden model. 
 
 
 
 1.4.3.  Spatial variance as a Lyapunov function 

 
 A natural first candidate for measuring spatial fluctuations of the hidden-model profile is the variance with respect to the probability measure induced by  α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]} . More precisely, for  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n]  and  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , set 
 

 
 (1.84) 
 
 π x ≔ α x α 0 ​ , π ⁡ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] π x ​ θ x ​ , Var π ​ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] π x ​ ( θ x − π ⁡ ( θ ) ) 2 ​ . \textstyle\pi_{x}\coloneqq\frac{\alpha_{x}}{\alpha_{0}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\pi(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in[n]}\pi_{x}\,\theta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in[n]}\pi_{x}\left(\theta_{x}-\pi(\theta)\right)^{2}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  θ ↦ Var π ​ ( θ ) \theta\mapsto{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)  is nonnegative and vanishes precisely on flat configurations, consider its optimal exponential contraction rate 
 

 
 (1.85) 
 
 δ ( α , w ) ≔ sup { δ ≥ 0 : e t ​ ℒ Var π ( θ ) ≤ e − δ ​ t Var π ( θ ) for all t ≥ 0 , θ ∈ ℝ n } , \delta(\alpha,w)\coloneqq\sup\{\delta\geq 0:e^{t\mathscr{L}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\leq e^{-\delta t}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\ \text{for all}\ t\geq 0,\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where, here and in what follows,  ( e t ​ ℒ ) t ≥ 0 (e^{t\mathscr{L}})_{t\geq 0}  denotes the hidden-model semigroup.
Following the strategy introduced in  [ KQS25 ] , and here extended to the weighted-hypergraph setting, one obtains a quantitative lower bound on
 inf k ≥ 2 gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) \inf_{k\geq 2}{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w) 
in two separate steps, as we now briefly sketch; for further details, we refer to Sections  2.2  and  5.2 . 
 
 
 First, one estimates the decay of the variance through an infinitesimal contraction computation. The resulting bound involves only  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) —and hence spectral information carried by linear observables of the energy variables—together with the factor 
 

 
 (1.86) 
 
 γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ≔ α w , min 1 + α w , min ​ ( 1 + 1 α 0 ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \gamma(\alpha,w)\coloneqq\frac{\alpha_{w,{\rm min}}}{1+\alpha_{w,{\rm min}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\right)\in(0,1) 
 
 
 appearing on the left-hand side of ( 1.31 ). In fact, a direct computation  [ KQS25 ]  yields 
 

 
 (1.87) 
 
 ℒ ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ≤ − γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ ℰ 1 ​ ( θ ) ≤ − γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ , \mathscr{L}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\leq-\gamma(\alpha,w)\,\mathcal{E}_{1}(\theta)\leq-\gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ℰ 1 \mathcal{E}_{1}  denotes the Dirichlet form of the one-particle dynamics with rates ( 1.16 ) (see ( 5.21 )), while the second inequality follows by the variational characterization of  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) . Grönwall’s inequality then gives 
 

 
 (1.88) 
 
 δ ⁡ ( α , w ) ≥ γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ . \delta(\alpha,w)\geq\gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Second, the quadratic vanishing of hidden-model eigenfunctions at flat configurations allows the decay estimate for the variance to be converted into the uniform bound 
 

 
 (1.89) 
 
 gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ , k ≥ 2 ​ . {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)\geq\delta(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 2\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, if  g = ψ ~ ≠ 0 g=\widetilde{\psi}\neq 0  is a hidden-model eigenfunction associated to  λ = gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) \lambda={\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w) ,  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , then 
 

 
 (1.90) 
 
 | g ⁡ ( θ ) | ≤ C ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ​ , θ ∈ [ a , b ] n ​ , |g(\theta)|\leq C{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in[a,b]^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for every  − ∞ < a < b < ∞ -\infty<a<b<\infty  and some  C = C ⁡ ( g , k , α , a , b ) > 0 C=C(g,k,\alpha,a,b)>0 .
Hence,  ℒ ​ g = − λ ​ g \mathscr{L}g=-\lambda g , positivity of the hidden-model semigroup  ( e t ​ ℒ ) t ≥ 0 (e^{t\mathscr{L}})_{t\geq 0} , the maximum principle in  2 , ( 1.90 ), and ( 1.85 ) give, for every  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0  and  θ ∈ [ a , b ] n \theta\in[a,b]^{n} , 
 

 
 (1.91) 
 
 e − λ ​ t ​ | g ⁡ ( θ ) | = | e t ​ ℒ ​ g ​ ( θ ) | ≤ e t ​ ℒ ​ | g | ​ ( θ ) ≤ C ​ e t ​ ℒ ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ≤ C ​ e − δ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ t ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ​ . e^{-\lambda t}|g(\theta)|=|e^{t\mathscr{L}}g(\theta)|\leq e^{t\mathscr{L}}|g|(\theta)\leq Ce^{t\mathscr{L}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\leq Ce^{-\delta(\alpha,w)t}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 As  g ≠ 0 g\neq 0  on  [ a , b ] n [a,b]^{n} , letting  t → ∞ t\to\infty  in ( 1.91 ) proves ( 1.89 ). 
 
 
 Altogether, ( 1.88 ) and ( 1.89 ) yield the lower bound 
 

 
 (1.92) 
 
 inf k ≥ 2 gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ≥ γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ , \inf_{k\geq 2}{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)\geq\gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which, while quantitative, does not yet identify the lowest-degree gap. 
 
 
 
 1.4.4.  A Perron-Frobenius theorem for nonnegative quadratic functions 

 
 The preceding argument does not allow, in general, to prove that the optimal variance-contraction rate  δ ⁡ ( α , w ) \delta(\alpha,w)  in ( 1.85 )  always  coincides with  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w) . A closer inspection, though, shows that this identity does hold in  some  instances, namely, in homogeneous mean-field settings, such as 
 

 
 (1.93) 
 
 α ≡ const . , ​ w B = 𝟙 | B | = ℓ ​ , for some ​ ℓ = 2 , … , n − 1 ​ . \textstyle\alpha\equiv{\rm const.}\;\text{,}\;\;w_{B}=\mathds{1}_{{|B|=\ell}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for some}\ \ell=2,\ldots,n-1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, in this case all the inequalities in ( 1.87 ) become identities (Remark  5.9 ), yielding 
 

 
 (1.94) 
 
 ℒ ​ Var π = − γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ Var π ​ . \mathscr{L}{\rm Var}_{\pi}=-\gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,{\rm Var}_{\pi}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, in this context,  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  is itself an eigenfunction of  − ℒ -\mathscr{L}  with eigenvalue  γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) , which necessarily coincides with  δ ⁡ ( α , w ) \delta(\alpha,w)  in ( 1.85 ). Moreover,  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  is a nonzero, quadratic polynomial in the  θ \theta -variables that vanishes precisely on constant configurations. Leveraging on the correspondence in ( 1.83 ), one then proves that  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  is a genuine degree-two eigenfunction, so that  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ≤ γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\leq\gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) . Combined with ( 1.89 ) for  k = 2 k=2 , this forces 
 

 
 (1.95) 
 
 γ ⁡ ( α , w ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) = δ ⁡ ( α , w ) = gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ . \gamma(\alpha,w)\,{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)=\delta(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Hence, in the homogeneous mean-field setting ( 1.93 ), one obtains not only 
 

 
 (1.96) 
 
 inf k ≥ 2 gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) = gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ , \inf_{k\geq 2}{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 but also the stronger structural fact that  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  is a nonnegative quadratic eigenfunction of  − ℒ -\mathscr{L}  associated with  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)  and vanishing precisely on flat configurations. These are exactly the properties used in the preceding argument: nonnegativity and quadratic vanishing yield the pointwise domination in ( 1.90 ), while the eigenvalue equation provides the sharp decay rate  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w) , rather than merely a computable lower bound. 
 
 
 For general geometries,  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  is typically no longer an eigenfunction. The central finding of our approach is that the properties above can nevertheless always be recovered simultaneously for any  KMP {\rm KMP}  hidden-model dynamics: for all positive site weights  α \alpha  and nonnegative block weights  w w , under suitable irreducibility assumptions there exists a nonzero quadratic function  g ∗ ≥ 0 g_{\ast}\geq 0 , strictly positive away from flat configurations, such that 
 

 
 (1.97) 
 
 ℒ ​ g ∗ = − gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ g ∗ ​ . \mathscr{L}g_{\ast}=-{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)\,g_{\ast}\,\text{.}
