Applying these conclusions to  e t ​ ℒ | ℛ e^{t\mathscr{L}}|_{\mathscr{R}}  and using the spectral mapping theorem gives the following strengthening of Proposition  3.1 . 
 
 
 Proposition 3.5   (Perron eigenfunctions II) . 
 
 Under the assumptions of either Proposition
 3.2  or Proposition
 3.3 , the eigenvalue
 − gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) -{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)  of  ℒ | ℛ \mathscr{L}|_{\mathscr{R}}  is algebraically simple. Moreover, the right and left Perron eigenfunctions  g ∗ g_{\ast}  and  h ∗ h_{\ast}  from Proposition  3.1  are unique up to multiplication by a positive scalar and satisfy 
 

 
 (3.12) 
 
 g ∗ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) and h ∗ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ′ ) ​ . g_{\ast}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{\ast}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.6 . 
 
 The condition  g ∗ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) g_{\ast}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})  is precisely the nondegeneracy of the right Perron eigenfunction:  g ∗ ​ ( θ ) > 0 g_{\ast}(\theta)>0 
for every nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} .
Actually, more is true: after fixing a normalization of  g ∗ g_{\ast} , there exist constants  C 1 , C 2 > 0 C_{1},C_{2}>0 , depending only on  α \alpha  and  w w , such that 
 

 
 (3.13) 
 
 C 1 ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ≤ g ∗ ​ ( θ ) ≤ C 2 ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ . C_{1}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\leq g_{\ast}(\theta)\leq C_{2}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular,  g ∗ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) g_{\ast}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})  provides a suitable substitute for  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  in ( 1.91 ). Indeed, if  λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0  and  g ∈ ℛ k , ∗ g\in\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast} ,  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , satisfy  ℒ ​ g = − λ ​ g \mathscr{L}g=-\lambda g , then, for every  − ∞ < a < b < ∞ -\infty<a<b<\infty , there exists  C = C ⁡ ( g , k , α , a , b ) > 0 C=C(g,k,\alpha,a,b)>0  such that, for every  t > 0 t>0  and  θ ∈ [ a , b ] n \theta\in[a,b]^{n} , 
 

 
 (3.14) 
 
 e − λ ​ t ​ | g ⁡ ( θ ) | = | e t ​ ℒ ​ g ​ ( θ ) | ≤ e t ​ ℒ ​ | g | ​ ( θ ) ≤ C ​ e t ​ ℒ ​ g ∗ ​ ( θ ) = C ​ e − gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ t ​ g ∗ ​ ( θ ) ​ , e^{-\lambda t}|g(\theta)|=|e^{t\mathscr{L}}g(\theta)|\leq e^{t\mathscr{L}}|g|(\theta)\leq Ce^{t\mathscr{L}}g_{\ast}(\theta)=Ce^{-{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)t}g_{\ast}(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the first and last steps used, respectively, that  g g  and  g ∗ g_{\ast}  are  ℒ \mathscr{L} -eigenfunctions, the second step is a consequence of the Markovianity of the  ℒ \mathscr{L} -semigroup, whereas the third step combined ( 3.13 ), Lemma  2.9  and ( 2.63 ). 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.3.  Proof of strong positivity 

 
 The proofs of Propositions  3.2  and  3.3  rely on two ingredients. The first, Lemma  3.7 , is a support argument requiring no geometric assumption on  w w : zeros of a nonnegative quadratic function propagate along every finite sequence of hidden-model updates. The second, Lemma  3.8 , is geometric: under the assumptions of either proposition, the linear range in ( 3.16 ) generated by such updates equals  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n}  whenever the initial configuration is nonconstant. 
 
 
 We start by introducing some notation.
Recall from ( 1.70 )–( 1.71 ) and ( 2.2 ) that, for the  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model, the support of its update measure equals 
 

 
 (3.15) 
 
 supp ( Υ ) = { K B U : B ⊆ [ n ] with w B > 0 , U ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] B and ∑ x ∈ B U x = 1 } . {\rm supp}(\Upsilon)=\big\{\textstyle K_{B}^{U}:B\subseteq[n]\ \text{with}\ w_{B}>0\,,\,U\in[0,1]^{B}\ \text{and}\ \sum_{x\in B}U_{x}=1\big\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, conditionally on the choice of  B B , the redistribution vector has law  Dir ⁡ ( α B ) {\rm Dir}(\alpha^{B}) , whose support is the whole probability simplex on  B B . Further, for  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , define 
 

 
 (3.16) 
 
 𝒮 ( θ ) = 𝒮 Υ ( θ ) ≔ span { K ℓ ⋯ K 1 θ : ℓ ≥ 0 , K 1 , … , K ℓ ∈ supp ( Υ ) } ⊆ ℝ n \mathcal{S}(\theta)=\mathcal{S}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}(\theta)\coloneqq{\rm span}\big\{K_{\ell}\cdots K_{1}\theta:\ell\geq 0\,,\,K_{1},\ldots,K_{\ell}\in{\rm supp}(\Upsilon)\big\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n} 
 
 
 as the linear range generated by applying finitely many successive updates from  supp ⁡ ( Υ ) {\rm supp}(\Upsilon)  to  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} . Since the empty sequence of updates is allowed, one always has  θ ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \theta\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) . 
 
 
 The next result is the first step in the proof of Propositions  3.2  and  3.3 . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.7   (Propagation of zeros) . 
 
 Let  g ∈ 𝒞 g\in\mathscr{C} ,  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , and  t > 0 t>0 . If 
 

 
 (3.17) 
 
 ( e t ​ ℒ ​ g ) ​ ( θ ) = 0 ​ , (e^{t\mathscr{L}}g)(\theta)=0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 then  g g  vanishes identically on  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}(\theta) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  θ ⁡ ( t ) ∈ ℝ n \theta(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}  be the hidden-model configuration at time  t > 0 t>0 , started from  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} .
Since  g ≥ 0 g\geq 0 , ( 3.17 ) implies
 g ⁡ ( θ ⁡ ( t ) ) = 0 g(\theta(t))=0  almost surely. 
 
 
 Fix  ℓ ≥ 1 \ell\geq 1  and matrices
 K B 1 U 1 , … , K B ℓ U ℓ ∈ supp ⁡ ( Υ ) K_{B_{1}}^{U_{1}},\ldots,K_{B_{\ell}}^{U_{\ell}}\in{\rm supp}(\Upsilon) . Every open neighborhood of 
 

 
 (3.18) 
 
 θ ′ ≔ K B ℓ U ℓ ⋯ K B 1 U 1 θ ∈ ℝ n \theta^{\prime}\coloneqq K_{B_{\ell}}^{U_{\ell}}\cdots K_{B_{1}}^{U_{1}}\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} 
 
 
 has positive probability under the law of the hidden model: one may require exactly  ℓ \ell  updates before time  t t , with the update matrices lying, in the prescribed order, in arbitrarily small neighborhoods of  K B 1 U 1 , … , K B ℓ U ℓ K_{B_{1}}^{U_{1}},\ldots,K_{B_{\ell}}^{U_{\ell}} . Hence, the configuration  θ ′ \theta^{\prime}  in ( 3.18 ) belongs to the support of  Law ⁡ ( θ ⁡ ( t ) ) {\rm Law}(\theta(t)) , and continuity of  g g  yields  g ⁡ ( θ ′ ) = 0 g(\theta^{\prime})=0 . The same argument, applied to the event that no update occurs before time  t t  (i.e.,  ℓ = 0 \ell=0 ), shows that  g ⁡ ( θ ) = 0 g(\theta)=0 . 
 
 
 This proves that  g g  vanishes on  { K ℓ ⋯ K 1 θ : ℓ ≥ 0 and K 1 , … , K ℓ ∈ supp ( Υ ) } \{K_{\ell}\cdots K_{1}\theta:\ell\geq 0\ \text{and}\ K_{1},\ldots,K_{\ell}\in{\rm supp}(\Upsilon)\} . As the zero set of a nonnegative quadratic form is a linear subspace,  g g  vanishes on the span in ( 3.16 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 The geometric ingredient is the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.8   (Full range) . 
 
 Assume that the hypergraph induced by  w w  is minimal as in Proposition  3.3 .
Then, for every nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , 
 

 
 (3.19) 
 
 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = ℝ n ​ . \mathcal{S}(\theta)+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, ( 3.19 ) holds true if  w w  induces a connected graph (Remark  3.4 ). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We shall actually prove 
 

 
 (3.20) 
 
 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) = ℝ n ​ , \mathcal{S}(\theta)=\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which is stronger than ( 3.19 ). We present the proof of ( 3.20 ) first for connected graphs, and then for minimal hypergraphs. 
 
 
 Graph case.  Assume that  w w  and induces a connected graph as in Proposition  3.2 . Fix a nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , and choose  y , z ∈ [ n ] y,z\in[n]  such that  θ y ≠ θ z \theta_{y}\neq\theta_{z} . 
 
 
 Fix  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] , and let us prove that  e x = 𝟏 { x } ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) e_{x}=\mathbf{1}_{\{x\}}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) . Choose  x 0 ∈ { y , z } x_{0}\in\{y,z\}  so that 
 

 
 (3.21) 
 
 θ x 0 ≠ θ x ​ . \theta_{x_{0}}\neq\theta_{x}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By the assumed connectivity, there exists a simple path  x 0 , x 1 , … , x ℓ = x x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}=x  along edges  { x i − 1 , x i } \{x_{i-1},x_{i}\}  with  w { x i − 1 , x i } > 0 w_{\{x_{i-1},x_{i}\}}>0 ,  i = 1 , … , ℓ i=1,\ldots,\ell . Starting from  θ { 0 } = θ ∈ ℝ n \theta^{\{0\}}=\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , we successively define, for every  i = 1 , … , ℓ i=1,\ldots,\ell , 
 

 
 (3.22) 
 
 θ { i } ≔ K { i } ​ θ { i − 1 } ​ , with ​ K { i } ≔ K { x i − 1 , x i } e x i − 1 ​ , \theta^{\{i\}}\coloneqq K^{\{i\}}\theta^{\{i-1\}}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{with}\ K^{\{i\}}\coloneqq K_{\{x_{i-1},x_{i}\}}^{e_{x_{i-1}}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  e x i − 1 e_{x_{i-1}}  stands for the probability vector concentrated at  x i − 1 ∈ [ n ] x_{i-1}\in[n] . Observe that each matrix in ( 3.22 ) belongs to  supp ⁡ ( Υ ) {\rm supp}(\Upsilon)  in ( 3.15 ) and, thus,  θ { 0 } , … , θ { ℓ } ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \theta^{\{0\}},\ldots,\theta^{\{\ell\}}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) . Moreover, by ( 2.2 ),  K { i } K^{\{i\}}  acts on  θ { i − 1 } \theta^{\{i-1\}}  only by overwriting the value at site  x i x_{i}  with  θ x i − 1 { i − 1 } \theta_{x_{i-1}}^{\{i-1\}} .
Consequently, 
 

 
 (3.23) 
 
 θ { ℓ } − θ { ℓ − 1 } = ( θ x 0 − θ x ) ​ e x ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) ⊆ ℝ n ​ . \theta^{\{\ell\}}-\theta^{\{\ell-1\}}=\left(\theta_{x_{0}}-\theta_{x}\right)e_{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By ( 3.21 ), the coefficient is nonzero, thus,  e x ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) e_{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) . Since  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n]  was arbitrary,  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) = ℝ n \mathcal{S}(\theta)=\mathbb{R}^{n} . 
 
 
 
 Hypergraph case. 

 
 Assume that the hypergraph induced by  w w  is minimal as in Proposition  3.3 . Fix a nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} . We again use only updates of the form  K B e x K_{B}^{e_{x}} , for some  x ∈ B x\in B , overwriting the values at  y ∈ B y\in B  with  θ x ∈ ℝ \theta_{x}\in\mathbb{R} . 
 
 
 We first show that  𝟏 ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) . Choose  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n]  with  θ x ≠ 0 \theta_{x}\neq 0 , and let  N N  be the set of coordinates currently equal to  θ x \theta_{x} . As long as  N ≠ [ n ] N\neq[n] , connectedness provides a block  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  crossing the partition  N ⊔ N c N\sqcup N^{\mathrm{c}} . Choosing  z ∈ B ∩ N z\in B\cap N  and applying  K B e z K_{B}^{e_{z}}  makes every coordinate in  B B  equal to  θ x \theta_{x} , and therefore strictly enlarges  N N . Iterating, one reaches the constant configuration  θ x ​ 𝟏 \theta_{x}\mathbf{1} . Thus, 
 

 
 (3.24) 
 
 𝟏 ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) ​ . \mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 By connectedness (see ( 1.36 )), there exists a block  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  on which  θ \theta  is nonconstant. Choose  x , y ∈ B x,y\in B  with  θ x ≠ θ y \theta_{x}\neq\theta_{y} . Then, 
 

 
 (3.25) 
 
 ( K B e x − K B e y ) ​ θ = ( θ x − θ y ) ​ 𝟏 B ​ . \big(K_{B}^{e_{x}}-K_{B}^{e_{y}}\big)\theta=(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y})\mathbf{1}_{B}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence,  𝟏 B ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathbf{1}_{B}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) , and ( 3.24 ) also gives  𝟏 B c ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathbf{1}_{B^{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta) . Since  w [ n ] = 0 w_{[n]}=0 , both  B B  and  B c B^{\mathrm{c}}  are nonempty. We have therefore obtained a partition of  [ n ] [n]  into at least two atoms whose indicator vectors belong to  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}(\theta) . 
 
 
 Starting from this partition, refine it successively as follows. Suppose that
 [ n ] = N 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N m [n]=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{m} 
is a partition with  2 ≤ m ≤ n 2\leq m\leq n  and  𝟏 N j ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathbf{1}_{N_{j}}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)  for every  j = 1 , … , m j=1,\ldots,m . If it is not  w w -compatible, there exist a block  D D  with  w D > 0 w_{D}>0  and an atom  N j N_{j}  such that 
 

 
 (3.26) 
 
 N j ∩ D ≠ ∅ ​ , N j ∖ D ≠ ∅ ​ , D ∖ N j ≠ ∅ ​ . N_{j}\cap D\neq\varnothing\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad N_{j}\setminus D\neq\varnothing\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad D\setminus N_{j}\neq\varnothing\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Choose  z ∈ D ∖ N j z\in D\setminus N_{j} . Since
 K D e z ​ 𝟏 N j = 𝟏 N j ∖ D K_{D}^{e_{z}}\mathbf{1}_{N_{j}}=\mathbf{1}_{N_{j}\setminus D} , both 
 

 
 (3.27) 
 
 𝟏 N j ∖ D and 𝟏 N j ∩ D = 𝟏 N j − 𝟏 N j ∖ D \mathbf{1}_{N_{j}\setminus D}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{1}_{N_{j}\cap D}=\mathbf{1}_{N_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{N_{j}\setminus D} 
 
 
 belong to  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}(\theta) . We may therefore replace  N j N_{j}  by the two nonempty atoms  N j ∩ D N_{j}\cap D  and  N j ∖ D N_{j}\setminus D . 
 
 
 Each refinement increases the number of atoms, so after finitely many steps one obtains a  w w -compatible partition all of whose atom indicators belong to  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}(\theta) . By minimality, this partition must be the discrete one. Hence,  𝟏 { x } = e x ∈ 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathbf{1}_{\{x\}}=e_{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)  for every  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] , thus,
proving ( 3.20 ). ∎ 
 
 
 We may now conclude the proof of strong positivity. 
 
 
 Proof of Propositions  3.2  and  3.3 . 
 
 Fix  t > 0 t>0  and  0 ≠ g ∈ 𝒞 0\neq g\in\mathscr{C} . By ( 3.4 ), one has  e t ​ ℒ ​ g ∈ 𝒞 e^{t\mathscr{L}}g\in\mathscr{C} . If  e t ​ ℒ ​ g ∉ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) e^{t\mathscr{L}}g\notin{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}) , then ( 3.3 ) yields a nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}  such that 
 

 
 (3.28) 
 
 ( e t ​ ℒ ​ g ) ​ ( θ ) = 0 ​ . (e^{t\mathscr{L}}g)(\theta)=0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By Lemma  3.7  and the definition of  ℛ \mathscr{R} ,  g g  vanishes, respectively, on  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}(\theta)  and  ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathbb{R}\mathbf{1} , whereas Lemma  3.8  gives  𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = ℝ n \mathcal{S}(\theta)+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathbb{R}^{n} . Hence,  g g  vanishes identically, a contradiction. This concludes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We mention below some possible refinements of Lemma  3.8 , which will play a role in Section  5.2.3 . 
 
 
 Remark 3.9 . 
 
 The full-range property in Lemma  3.8  depends on both the geometry induced by  w w  and the chosen updates. However, the proof uses only matrices of the form  K B e x K_{B}^{e_{x}} : setting 
 

 
 (3.29) 
 
 supp ( Υ 0 ) ≔ { K B e x : B ⊆ [ n ] with w B > 0 , x ∈ B } ⊊ supp ( Υ ) , {\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{0})\coloneqq\big\{K_{B}^{e_{x}}:B\subseteq[n]\ \text{with}\ w_{B}>0\,,\ x\in B\big\}\subsetneq{\rm supp}(\Upsilon)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 the proof actually shows that, for every nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , 
 

 
 (3.30) 
 
 𝒮 Υ 0 ( θ ) ≔ span { K ℓ ⋯ K 1 θ : ℓ ≥ 0 , K 1 , … , K ℓ ∈ supp ( Υ 0 ) } = ℝ n . \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{0}}(\theta)\coloneqq\operatorname{span}\big\{K_{\ell}\cdots K_{1}\theta:\ell\geq 0\,,\ K_{1},\ldots,K_{\ell}\in{\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{0})\big\}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 While the inclusion in ( 3.29 ) yields
 𝒮 Υ 0 ​ ( θ ) ⊆ 𝒮 Υ ​ ( θ ) = 𝒮 ⁡ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{0}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}(\theta)=\mathcal{S}(\theta) ,
the strictness of that inclusion makes ( 3.30 ) a strictly stronger statement than ( 3.20 ). 
 
 
 In Section  5.2.3  below, we will need a refinement of Lemma  3.8 , now involving (recall ( 2.2 )) 
 

 
 (3.31) 
 
 supp ⁡ ( Υ ∞ ) ≔ { K B π : B ⊆ [ n ] ​ with ​ w B > 0 } ⊊ supp ⁡ ( Υ ) ​ , {\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{\infty})\coloneqq\big\{K_{B}^{\pi}:B\subseteq[n]\ \text{with}\ w_{B}>0\big\}\subsetneq{\rm supp}(\Upsilon)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with  π = α / α 0 \pi=\alpha/\alpha_{0}  as given in ( 1.84 ). For the corresponding linear range 
 

 
 (3.32) 
 
 𝒮 Υ ∞ ( θ ) ≔ span { K ℓ ⋯ K 1 θ : ℓ ≥ 0 , K 1 , … , K ℓ ∈ supp ( Υ ∞ ) } , \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)\coloneqq{\rm span}\big\{K_{\ell}\cdots K_{1}\theta:\ell\geq 0\,,\ K_{1},\ldots,K_{\ell}\in{\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{\infty})\big\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 we shall show that  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) ⊆ 𝒮 Υ 0 ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{0}}(\theta)  (see ( A.2 )) and that, under the assumptions of Lemma  3.8 ,  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = ℝ n \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathbb{R}^{n} . The proof of this last claim is more delicate than that of Lemma  3.8  and is deferred to Appendix  A . 
 
 
 
 Finally, we conclude this section by noting a partial extension of (strong) positivity on even higher-degree polynomials. 
 
 
 Remark 3.10   (Even-degree cones) . 
 
 Thus far, we have focused exclusively on the quadratic positive cone
 𝒞 ⊆ ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast} .
More generally, we could have considered, for every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (3.33) 
 
 𝒞 k , ∗ ≔ { g ∈ ℛ k , ∗ : g ⁡ ( θ ) ≥ 0 ​ for every ​ θ ∈ ℝ n } ​ . \mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}\coloneqq\{g\in\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}:g(\theta)\geq 0\ \text{for every}\ \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 It is not difficult to check that this is a proper cone in  ℛ k , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}  with nonempty interior if and only if  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  is  even ; recall from Lemma  2.8  that
 Var π k / 2 ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 k , ∗ ) {\rm Var}_{\pi}^{k/2}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}_{k,\ast})  if  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  is even. Moreover, Markovianity of the hidden-model semigroup ensures
 e t ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 k , ∗ ⊆ 𝒞 k , ∗ e^{t\mathscr{L}}\mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}  for every  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 .
Thus, letting  𝒞 k , ∗ ′ \mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}^{\prime}  denote the dual cone, the Perron–Frobenius
argument of Proposition  3.1  yields nonzero right and left
Perron eigenfunctions
 g k , ∗ ∈ 𝒞 k , ∗ g_{k,\ast}\in\mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}  and  h k , ∗ ∈ 𝒞 k , ∗ ′ h_{k,\ast}\in\mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}^{\prime} 
such that 
 

 
 (3.34) 
 
 ℒ ​ g k , ∗ = − gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ g k , ∗ and h k , ∗ ​ ℒ = − gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) ​ h k , ∗ ​ . \mathscr{L}g_{k,\ast}=-{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)g_{k,\ast}\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{k,\ast}\mathscr{L}=-{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)h_{k,\ast}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The strong-positivity and uniqueness conclusions of Proposition
 3.5 , however, seem specific to the quadratic cone and need
not hold for  k ≥ 4 k\geq 4 : taking, e.g.,  k = 4 k=4  and the three-site segment, the hidden-model semigroup does not map the polynomial  θ ⟼ g ⁡ ( θ ) = ( θ 1 − θ 2 ) 2 ​ ( θ 2 − θ 3 ) 2 ∈ 𝒞 4 , ∗ ∖ { 0 } \theta\longmapsto g(\theta)=(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}(\theta_{2}-\theta_{3})^{2}\in\mathscr{C}_{4,\ast}\setminus\{0\}  into  int ⁡ ( 𝒞 4 , ∗ ) {\rm int}(\mathscr{C}_{4,\ast}) , as  e t ​ ℒ ​ g ​ ( θ ) = 0 e^{t\mathscr{L}}g(\theta)=0  for every  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0  if either  θ 1 = θ 2 \theta_{1}=\theta_{2}  or  θ 2 = θ 3 \theta_{2}=\theta_{3} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.  Hypergraph reduction and gaps of  KMP {\rm KMP} 

 
 Before turning to the comparison of  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)  and  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)  (Section  5 ), we reduce the underlying weighted hypergraph to a minimal one; readers interested only in graphs, rather than general hypergraphs, may proceed directly to Section  5 , as ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ) and ( 1.41 ) alone already imply minimality. 
 
 
 Throughout, we fix positive site weights
 α \alpha  and nonnegative block weights
 w w . 
 
 
 Recall that the hypergraph strong-positivity result of
Proposition  3.3  requires minimality, whereas the
assumptions of our main theorems from
Section  1.2  impose only
( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), namely,
 w [ n ] = 0 w_{[n]}=0  and connectedness. This apparent discrepancy is removed by
collapsing the atoms of its coarsest partition into new sites. The main goal of this section is to prove that the resulting quotient hypergraph is minimal and, more importantly, preserves the relevant gaps: for each  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ,  gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)  coincides with the corresponding gap of the quotient
 KMP {\rm KMP}  model with aggregated site weights.
This is the content of
Propositions  4.1 – 4.2  below. Thus, after this reduction, minimality may be assumed without loss of
generality when proving the main results in Section  5 . 
 
 
 We first need a few additional definitions. Recall from ( 1.46 ) that a partition 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 [ n ] = N 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N m ​ , 2 ≤ m ≤ n ​ , [n]=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{m}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad 2\leq m\leq n\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 is  w w -compatible if every block  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  is either contained in one
atom or is a union of them. Such a partition exists, since the discrete
partition is  w w -compatible. Fix one and, for  j ∈ [ m ] j\in[m]  and  J ⊆ [ m ] J\subseteq[m] , set 
 

 
 (4.2) 
 
 N J ≔ ⨆ j ∈ J N j ​ , α ¯ j ≔ α ⁡ ( N j ) = ∑ x ∈ N j α x ​ , w ¯ J ≔ { w N J if ​ | J | ≥ 2 ​ , 0 if ​ | J | ≤ 1 ​ . N_{J}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{j\in J}N_{j}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\bar{\alpha}_{j}\coloneqq\alpha(N_{j})=\textstyle\sum_{x\in N_{j}}\alpha_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\bar{w}_{J}\coloneqq\begin{cases}w_{N_{J}}&\text{if}\ |J|\geq 2\;\text{,}\\
0&\text{if}\ |J|\leq 1\,\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 We call the weighted hypergraph on  [ m ] [m]  with site weights
 α ¯ = ( α ¯ j ) j ∈ [ m ] \bar{\alpha}=(\bar{\alpha}_{j})_{j\in[m]}  and block weights
 w ¯ = ( w ¯ J ) J ⊆ [ m ] \bar{w}=(\bar{w}_{J})_{J\subseteq[m]}  the  quotient hypergraph  associated with
( 4.1 ). 
 
 
 Proposition 4.1   (Minimal-hypergraph reduction) . 
 
 Assume ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), and let
( 4.1 ) be its coarsest partition. Then the associated quotient hypergraph is
minimal. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose that the quotient hypergraph were
disconnected, and let  C 1 , … , C ℓ C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell} ,  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 , denote its
connected components. Merging the original atoms according to these
components gives the partition 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 [ n ] = N 1 ′ ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N ℓ ′ ​ , N a ′ ≔ ⨆ j ∈ C a N j ​ . [n]=N_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{\ell}^{\prime}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad N_{a}^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{j\in C_{a}}N_{j}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This partition is again  w w -compatible. Indeed, a block contained in
some  N j N_{j}  remains internal. If instead a block is of the form
 N J N_{J}  with  w ¯ J = w N J > 0 \bar{w}_{J}=w_{N_{J}}>0 , then the quotient block  J J  cannot meet two distinct connected
components, and hence  N J N_{J}  is contained in one of the
 N a ′ N_{a}^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Since the original hypergraph is connected and  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , some
block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  must meet at least two atoms. Thus, at least one component  C a C_{a} 
contains at least two quotient sites, and consequently
 2 ≤ ℓ < m 2\leq\ell<m . This contradicts the coarseness of
the original partition ( 4.1 ). Hence, the quotient hypergraph is
connected. 
 
 
 If  m = 2 m=2 , its only possible block connecting the two quotient sites is
 [ 2 ] [2] . However,
 w ¯ [ 2 ] = w [ n ] = 0 \bar{w}_{[2]}=w_{[n]}=0  by ( 1.35 ), contradicting quotient connectivity. Therefore
 m ≥ 3 m\geq 3 . Finally, if the quotient admitted a compatible partition 
 

 
 (4.4) 
 
 [ m ] = C 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ C ℓ ​ , 1 < ℓ < m ​ , [m]=C_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup C_{\ell}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad 1<\ell<m\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 then replacing each  C a C_{a}  by
 ⊔ j ∈ C a N j \sqcup_{j\in C_{a}}N_{j}  would produce a  w w -compatible partition of
 [ n ] [n]  with fewer than  m m  atoms. This is again impossible by
coarseness. The quotient hypergraph is therefore minimal.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Only in the next proposition, where  KMP {\rm KMP}  models with different numbers of sites are
considered simultaneously, we indicate the number of sites by a superscript.
Thus, for the original model on  [ n ] [n] , we write, for instance,
 ℒ [ n ] = ℒ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}=\mathcal{L}  and
 gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ) = gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)  for the objects
defined in ( 1.6 ) and ( 1.28 ),
respectively; the corresponding objects for the quotient model on  [ m ] [m]  carry the
superscript  [ m ] [m] . 
 
 
 Proposition 4.2   (Hypergraph reduction and gaps) . 
 
 Given a  w w -compatible partition as in ( 4.1 ), one has, for the corresponding weights defined in
( 4.2 ), 
 

 
 (4.5) 
 
 gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ) = gap k , ∗ [ m ] ​ ( α ¯ , w ¯ ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{w})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By  w w -compatibility of ( 4.1 ) and ( 4.2 ), the generator in ( 1.6 ) decomposes as
 ℒ [ n ] = ℒ ∙ [ n ] + ℒ ∘ [ n ] \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}=\mathcal{L}_{\bullet}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}+\mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]} ,
where 
 

 
 (4.6) 
 
 ℒ ∙ [ n ] ≔ ∑ J ⊆ [ m ] w ¯ J ​ ( μ N J [ n ] − I ) ​ , ℒ ∘ [ n ] ≔ ∑ j ∈ [ m ] ∑ B ⊆ N j w B ​ ( μ B [ n ] − I ) ​ , \textstyle\mathcal{L}_{\bullet}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}\coloneqq\sum_{J\subseteq[m]}\bar{w}_{J}\,(\mu_{N_{J}}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}-I)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[m]}\sum_{B\subseteq N_{j}}w_{B}\,(\mu_{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}-I)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with  μ B [ n ] \mu_{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]} ,  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , being the conditional  μ [ n ] \mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]} -expectation given  η B c \eta^{B^{\mathrm{c}}} .
Thus,  ℒ ∙ [ n ] \mathcal{L}_{\bullet}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}  and  ℒ ∘ [ n ] \mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}  retain, respectively, the updates across multiple atoms and those within a single atom of ( 4.1 ). 
 
 
 Besides the orthogonal projections  μ B [ n ] \mu_{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]} , for  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 , we also consider  E j ≔ μ N j [ n ] E_{j}\coloneqq\mu_{N_{j}}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]} ,  j ∈ [ m ] j\in[m] . Since the atoms  N 1 , … , N m N_{1},\ldots,N_{m}  are disjoint,  E 1 , … , E m E_{1},\ldots,E_{m}  are commuting orthogonal projections. Moreover, the corresponding conditional expectations commute: for nested blocks this follows from the tower property, whereas for disjoint blocks it follows from the fact that, under  μ [ n ] = Dir ⁡ ( α ) \mu^{[n]}={\rm Dir}(\alpha) , the conditional law of the configuration inside a block given its complement depends on the latter only through the total mass within that block, which is left unchanged by updates on a disjoint block.
Hence, 
 

 
 (4.7) 
 
 E j ​ μ B [ n ] = μ B [ n ] ​ E j ​ , E j ​ μ N J [ n ] = μ N J [ n ] ​ E j ​ , E_{j}\mu_{B}^{[n]}=\mu_{B}^{[n]}E_{j}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad E_{j}\mu_{N_{J}}^{[n]}=\mu_{N_{J}}^{[n]}E_{j}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for every  j ∈ [ m ] j\in[m] ,  B ⊆ N ℓ B\subseteq N_{\ell} ,  ℓ ∈ [ m ] \ell\in[m] , and  J ⊆ [ m ] J\subseteq[m] , with the further identities 
 

 
 (4.8) 
 
 E j ​ μ B [ n ] = E j if ​ B ⊆ N j ​ , μ N J [ n ] ​ E j = μ N J [ n ] if ​ j ∈ J ​ . E_{j}\mu_{B}^{[n]}=E_{j}\quad\text{if}\ B\subseteq N_{j}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mu_{N_{J}}^{[n]}E_{j}=\mu_{N_{J}}^{[n]}\quad\text{if}\ j\in J\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Recall ( 1.27 ). By the argument in the proof of Proposition  2.6 , these projections preserve
 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}  and each summand in 
 

 
 (4.9) 
 
 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] = ⨁ S ⊆ [ m ] 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( S ) ​ , with ​ 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( S ) ≔ ( ∏ j ∈ S ( I − E j ) ​ ∏ j ∈ [ m ] ∖ S E j ) ​ 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ . \textstyle\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}=\bigoplus_{S\subseteq[m]}\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(S)\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{with}\ \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(S)\coloneqq\big(\prod_{j\in S}(I-E_{j})\prod_{j\in[m]\setminus S}E_{j}\big)\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Let us first focus on the summand corresponding to  S = ∅ S=\varnothing .
Since  E ≔ E 1 ⋯ E m E\coloneqq E_{1}\cdots E_{m}  is conditional expectation given the atom masses
 η ¯ = ( η ⁡ ( N j ) ) j ∈ [ m ] \bar{\eta}=(\eta(N_{j}))_{j\in[m]} , Dirichlet aggregation and self-adjointness of  E E  give 
 

 
 (4.10) 
 
 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( ∅ ) = { F ⁡ ( η ¯ ) : F ∈ 𝒫 k , ∗ [ m ] } ​ . \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\varnothing)=\{F(\bar{\eta}):F\in\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By combining this with  μ N J [ n ] ​ F ​ ( η ¯ ) = ( μ J [ m ] ​ F ) ​ ( η ¯ ) \mu_{N_{J}}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}F(\bar{\eta})=(\mu_{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}F)(\bar{\eta})  and the fact that, by the first identity in ( 4.8 ),  ℒ ∘ [ n ] \mathcal{L}_{\circ}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}  vanishes on  𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( ∅ ) \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\varnothing) ,  ℒ [ n ] | 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( ∅ ) \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\varnothing)}  is unitarily equivalent to  ℒ [ m ] | 𝒫 k , ∗ [ m ] \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}} . 
 
 
 It remains to show that the spectral gap of  ℒ [ n ] \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}  on  𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}  is not attained by functions in  𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( S ) \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(S) ,  ∅ ≠ S ⊆ [ m ] \varnothing\neq S\subseteq[m] . Fix a nonempty  S ⊆ [ m ] S\subseteq[m] ,  j ∈ S j\in S , and
 f ∈ 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( S ) f\in\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(S) . Then, 
 

 
 (4.11) 
 
 ⟨ f , − ℒ [ n ] ​ f ⟩ μ [ n ] ≥ ∑ J ∋ j w ¯ J ​ ⟨ f , ( I − μ N J [ n ] ) ​ f ⟩ μ [ n ] = ‖ f ‖ μ [ n ] 2 ​ ( ∑ J ∋ j w ¯ J ) ​ , \textstyle\left\langle f,-\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}f\right\rangle_{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}}\geq\sum_{J\ni j}\bar{w}_{J}\,\langle f,(I-\mu_{N_{J}}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]})f\rangle_{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}}^{2}\left(\sum_{J\ni j}\bar{w}_{J}\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where for the first step we only kept updates of blocks  B = N J B=N_{J}  with  j ∈ J j\in J , while the second step used  μ N J [ n ] ​ f = 0 \mu_{N_{J}}^{[n]}f=0 ,  J ∋ j J\ni j , which follows from  f ∈ 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( S ) ⊆ ( I − E j ) ​ 𝒫 k , ∗ [ n ] f\in\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{[n]}(S)\subseteq(I-E_{j})\mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}^{[n]} ,  j ∈ S j\in S , implying  E j ​ f = 0 E_{j}f=0 , together with the second inequality in ( 4.8 ). Here,  ∑ J ∋ j w ¯ J \sum_{J\ni j}\bar{w}_{J}  may also be interpreted as the refresh rate of site  j ∈ [ m ] j\in[m]  in the quotient hypergraph.
We claim that 
 

 
 (4.12) 
 
 ( ∑ J ∋ j w ¯ J ) ≥ gap k , ∗ [ m ] ​ ( α ¯ , w ¯ ) ​ . \textstyle\big(\sum_{J\ni j}\bar{w}_{J}\big)\geq{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{w})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, for any continuous function  F = F ⁡ ( η ¯ ) F=F(\bar{\eta})  of the only coordinate  η ¯ j \bar{\eta}_{j} , 
 

 
 (4.13) 
 
 ⟨ F , − ℒ [ m ] ​ F ⟩ μ [ m ] = ∑ J ∋ j w ¯ J ​ ⟨ F , ( I − μ J [ m ] ) ​ F ⟩ μ [ m ] ≤ ‖ F ‖ μ [ m ] 2 ​ ( ∑ J ∋ j w ¯ J ) ​ , \textstyle\langle F,-\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}F\rangle_{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}}=\sum_{J\ni j}\bar{w}_{J}\,\langle F,(I-\mu_{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]})F\rangle_{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}}\leq\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}}^{2}\left(\sum_{J\ni j}\bar{w}_{J}\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the first step follows because updates not involving  j ∈ [ m ] j\in[m]  do not contribute to the Dirichlet form, while for the second step we used that  I − μ J [ m ] I-\mu_{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}  is an orthogonal projection. This proves ( 4.12 ) and, together with the aforementioned unitary equivalence, ( 4.5 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 4.3 . 
 
 Given a  w w -compatible partition as in ( 1.46 ), recall from ( 1.48 ) the definition of the block weights  w ~ \widetilde{w}  on  [ n ] [n]  obtained from  w w . Proposition  4.2  also shows
 gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ) = gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ~ ) {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,w)={\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,\widetilde{w}) ,  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 .
Indeed, since  w w  and  w ~ \widetilde{w}  induce the same quotient hypergraph,  gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ~ ) = gap k , ∗ [ m ] ​ ( α ¯ , w ¯ ) {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,\widetilde{w})={\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[m]}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{w}) , whereas the monotonicity  w B ≥ w ~ B w_{B}\geq\widetilde{w}_{B} ,  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , induces  gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ) ≥ gap k , ∗ [ n ] ​ ( α , w ~ ) {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,w)\geq{\rm gap}_{k,\ast}^{\scriptscriptstyle[n]}(\alpha,\widetilde{w}) . 
 
 
 
 In view of Propositions  4.1 – 4.2 , the analysis of
 gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)  under
( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ) may be reduced without loss
of generality to minimal hypergraphs. In the next section, we therefore no
longer need compatible partitions or quotient hypergraphs and revert to the
notation without the superscripts  [ n ] [n]  and  [ m ] [m] . 
 
 
 
 5.  One- and two-particle gaps of  KMP {\rm KMP} 

 
 In this section, we compare
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)  and
 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w) , and prove the results stated in
Section  1.2 . The Perron–Frobenius theory developed in
Section  3 , together with the strong-positivity results of
Section  3.2 , is central to this comparison. 
 
 
 The section is organized in three main parts: we first establish strict monotonicity in the scale parameter  τ \tau , and then analyze separately the limiting regimes  τ → ∞ \tau\to\infty  and  τ → 0 \tau\to 0 . These are the contents, respectively, of Sections  5.1 ,  5.2  and  5.3 . At the end, we collect these ingredients and show how they yield Theorems  1.4 ,  1.5 ,  1.6  and  1.8  in Section  5.4 . 
 
 
 Throughout this section, we fix positive site weights
 α \alpha  and nonnegative block weights  w w . For most of the analysis, in view of
Propositions  4.1 – 4.2 , we assume without loss of generality that
 w w  induces a minimal hypergraph.
Readers interested only in graphs ( 1.41 ) may simply assume that  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3  and that  w w  induces a connected graph. 
 
 
 5.1.  Two-particle gap monotonicity 

 
 The main goal of this section is to prove that the map in ( 1.37 ) is strictly increasing anytime the underlying hypergraph is minimal. 
 
 
 Proposition 5.1   (Monotonicity) . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and nonnegative block weights  w w  inducing a minimal hypergraph, the map  ( 0 , ∞ ) ∋ τ ⟼ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) (0,\infty)\ni\tau\longmapsto{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\in(0,\infty)  is continuous and strictly increasing. 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.2 . 
 
 This monotone behavior is specific to  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w) . Indeed, the one-particle rates of the  ( τ ​ α , w ) (\tau\alpha,w) - KMP {\rm KMP}  model, given by (cf. ( 1.16 )) 
 

 
 (5.1) 
 
 r x ​ y ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) = τ ​ α y ​ ∑ B ∋ x , y w B τ ​ α ​ ( B ) = r x ​ y ​ ( α , w ) ​ , x , y ∈ [ n ] ​ , ​ x ≠ y ​ , r_{xy}(\tau\alpha,w)=\tau\alpha_{y}\sum_{B\ni x,y}\frac{w_{B}}{\tau\alpha(B)}=r_{xy}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x,y\in[n]\;\text{,}\;\;x\neq y\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 do not depend on  τ > 0 \tau>0 . Consequently, the map
 τ ⟼ gap 1 ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) \tau\longmapsto{\rm gap}_{1}(\tau\alpha,w) 
is constant. 
 
 
 
 We now begin the proof of Proposition  5.1 . As in most of our arguments, we work at the level of the hidden model. We start by fixing some notation used throughout. 
 
 
 The weights  α \alpha  and  w w  are kept fixed and are often suppressed from the notation, whereas the dependence on  τ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) \tau\in(0,\infty)  is indicated by subscripts. For instance, we write  ℒ τ \mathscr{L}_{\tau} ,  g τ g_{\tau} , and  h τ h_{\tau}  in place of  ℒ \mathscr{L} ,  g ∗ g_{\ast} , and  h ∗ h_{\ast}  from Propositions  3.1  and  3.5 , respectively, and set 
 

 
 (5.2) 
 
 λ τ ≔ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ​ , τ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) ​ . \lambda_{\tau}\coloneqq{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\tau\in(0,\infty)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Note that the space  ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}  and the cone  𝒞 ⊆ ℛ \mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{R} , defined respectively in ( 3.1 ) and ( 3.2 ), depend neither on the weights  α , w \alpha,w  nor on  τ \tau . 
 
 
 We also introduce the following blockwise notation. Recall  π x = α x / α 0 \pi_{x}=\alpha_{x}/\alpha_{0} . For every nonempty block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] ,  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] , and  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , let 
 

 
 (5.3) 
 
 π x | B ≔ 𝟏 B ​ ( x ) ​ α x α ⁡ ( B ) = 𝟏 B ​ ( x ) ​ π x π ⁡ ( B ) ​ , π B ​ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ B π x | B ​ θ x ​ , \textstyle\pi_{x|B}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{B}(x)\frac{\alpha_{x}}{\alpha(B)}=\mathbf{1}_{B}(x)\frac{\pi_{x}}{\pi(B)}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\pi_{B}(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in B}\pi_{x|B}\,\theta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 

 
 (5.4) 
 
 Var π B ​ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ B π x | B ​ ( θ x − π B ​ ( θ ) ) 2 ​ . \textstyle{\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in B}\pi_{x|B}\left(\theta_{x}-\pi_{B}(\theta)\right)^{2}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 These are the  B B -conditional analogues of the quantities in ( 1.84 ); in particular, they are invariant under the rescaling  α ↦ τ ​ α \alpha\mapsto\tau\alpha . Moreover,
 Var π B ∈ 𝒞 {\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}\in\mathscr{C} . 
 
 
 To prove Proposition  5.1 , we first establish an explicit comparison formula for the hidden-model generators corresponding to two values of  τ \tau . 
 
 
 Lemma 5.3 . 
 
 Fix some positive site weights  α \alpha  and some nonnegative block weights  w w . Then, for every  0 < τ 1 < τ 2 < ∞ 0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\infty  and  g ∈ ℛ g\in\mathscr{R} , 
 

 
 (5.5) 
 
 ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ g = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ( 1 1 + τ 1 ​ α ​ ( B ) − 1 1 + τ 2 ​ α ​ ( B ) ) ​ g ​ ( 𝟏 B ) ​ Var π B ∈ ℛ ​ . \left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}}\right)g=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\left(\frac{1}{1+\tau_{1}\alpha(B)}-\frac{1}{1+\tau_{2}\alpha(B)}\right)g(\mathbf{1}_{B})\,{\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}\in\mathscr{R}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, 
 

 
 (5.6) 
 
 ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ 𝒞 ⊆ 𝒞 ​ , \left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}}\right)\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and, if  w w  additionally satisfies ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), 
 

 
 (5.7) 
 
 ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ int ​ ( 𝒞 ) ⊆ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) ​ . \left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}}\right){\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For every nonempty  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] ,  τ > 0 \tau>0  and  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , let 
 

 
 (5.8) 
 
 U B , τ ∼ Dir ⁡ ( ( τ ​ α x ) x ∈ B ) ​ , W B , τ ​ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ B ( U x B , τ − π x | B ) ​ θ x ∈ ℝ ​ . \textstyle U^{B,\tau}\sim{\rm Dir}\left((\tau\alpha_{x})_{x\in B}\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad W_{B,\tau}(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in B}(U_{x}^{B,\tau}-\pi_{x|B})\,\theta_{x}\in\mathbb{R}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, recalling ( 2.2 ),
 K B U B , τ ​ θ = K B π ​ θ + W B , τ ​ ( θ ) ​ 𝟏 B ∈ ℝ n K_{B}^{U^{B,\tau}}\theta=K_{B}^{\pi}\theta+W_{B,\tau}(\theta)\mathbf{1}_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} .
Since taking expectation with respect to  U B , τ U^{B,\tau}  gives  𝐄 B , τ ​ [ W B , τ ​ ( θ ) ] = 0 \mathbf{E}_{B,\tau}[W_{B,\tau}(\theta)]=0 , and since  g ∈ ℛ g\in\mathscr{R}  is quadratic, expanding yields 
 

 
 (5.9) 
 
 𝐄 B , τ ​ [ g ⁡ ( K B U B , τ ​ θ ) ] = g ⁡ ( K B π ​ θ ) + 𝐄 B , τ ​ [ W B , τ ​ ( θ ) 2 ] ​ g ​ ( 𝟏 B ) ​ . \mathbf{E}_{B,\tau}[g(K_{B}^{U^{B,\tau}}\theta)]=g(K_{B}^{\pi}\theta)+\mathbf{E}_{B,\tau}[W_{B,\tau}(\theta)^{2}]\,g(\mathbf{1}_{B})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The Dirichlet covariance formula for ( 5.8 ) gives 
 

 
 (5.10) 
 
 𝐄 B , τ ​ [ W B , τ ​ ( θ ) 2 ] = Var π B ​ ( θ ) 1 + τ ​ α ​ ( B ) ​ . \mathbf{E}_{B,\tau}[W_{B,\tau}(\theta)^{2}]=\frac{{\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}(\theta)}{1+\tau\alpha(B)}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Inserting this identity into the definition of  ℒ τ \mathscr{L}_{\tau}  and subtracting the resulting expressions at  τ 1 \tau_{1}  and  τ 2 \tau_{2}  proves
( 5.5 ). 
 
 
 If  g ∈ 𝒞 g\in\mathscr{C} , then  g ⁡ ( 𝟏 B ) ≥ 0 g(\mathbf{1}_{B})\geq 0 , and every coefficient on the right-hand side of ( 5.5 ) is nonnegative. Since  Var π B ∈ 𝒞 {\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}\in\mathscr{C} , this proves ( 5.6 ). 
 
 
 Finally, assume ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ), and let  g ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) g\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})  and  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}  be nonconstant. By connectedness ( 1.36 ), there exists a block  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  on which  θ \theta  is nonconstant; thus,
 Var π B ​ ( θ ) > 0 {\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}(\theta)>0 .
As by ( 1.35 ) every block with positive  w w -weight is a nonempty proper subset of  [ n ] [n] ,  𝟏 B ∈ ℝ n \mathbf{1}_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}  is nonconstant; thus,  g ⁡ ( 𝟏 B ) > 0 g(\mathbf{1}_{B})>0 .
Consequently, the  B B -summand in ( 5.5 ) is strictly positive, while all the others are nonnegative. Therefore,
 ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ g ​ ( θ ) > 0 (\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}})g(\theta)>0 
for every nonconstant  θ \theta , proving ( 5.7 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now complete the proof of monotonicity of  λ τ = gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) \lambda_{\tau}={\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)  in ( 5.2 ). 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  5.1 . 
 
 By the assumed hypergraph minimality and Proposition  3.5 , for every  τ > 0 \tau>0  there exist right and left Perron eigenfunctions 
 

 
 (5.11) 
 
 g τ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) and h τ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ′ ) g_{\tau}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{\tau}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}) 
 
 
 of  − ℒ τ -\mathscr{L}_{\tau} , associated with the eigenvalue  λ τ > 0 \lambda_{\tau}>0 . 
 
 
 Fix  0 < τ 1 < τ 2 < ∞ 0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\infty . By linearity and the eigenvalue equations in ( 3.8 ), 
 

 
 (5.12) 
 
 h τ 2 ​ ( ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ g τ 1 ) = − λ τ 1 ​ h τ 2 ​ ( g τ 1 ) + λ τ 2 ​ h τ 2 ​ ( g τ 1 ) = ( λ τ 2 − λ τ 1 ) ​ h τ 2 ​ ( g τ 1 ) ​ . h_{\tau_{2}}(\left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}}\right)g_{\tau_{1}})=-\lambda_{\tau_{1}}h_{\tau_{2}}(g_{\tau_{1}})+\lambda_{\tau_{2}}h_{\tau_{2}}(g_{\tau_{1}})=\left(\lambda_{\tau_{2}}-\lambda_{\tau_{1}}\right)h_{\tau_{2}}(g_{\tau_{1}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  g τ 1 ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) g_{\tau_{1}}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}) , the inclusion in ( 5.7 ) yields 
 

 
 (5.13) 
 
 ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ g τ 1 ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) ​ . \left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}}\right)g_{\tau_{1}}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 As  h τ 2 ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ′ ) h_{\tau_{2}}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}) , one further gets 
 

 
 (5.14) 
 
 h τ 2 ​ ( g τ 1 ) > 0 and h τ 2 ​ ( ( ℒ τ 1 − ℒ τ 2 ) ​ g τ 1 ) > 0 ​ . h_{\tau_{2}}(g_{\tau_{1}})>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{\tau_{2}}(\left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{1}}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau_{2}}\right)g_{\tau_{1}})>0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore, ( 5.12 ) implies
 λ τ 2 > λ τ 1 \lambda_{\tau_{2}}>\lambda_{\tau_{1}} ,
as claimed. 
 
 
 Finally,  τ ⟼ λ τ \tau\longmapsto\lambda_{\tau}  is continuous. Indeed, ( 5.5 ) shows that
 τ ⟼ ℒ τ | ℛ \tau\longmapsto\mathscr{L}_{\tau}|_{\mathscr{R}} 
is continuous in operator norm.
Since  ℛ \mathscr{R}  is finite-dimensional, this proves the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.4 . 
 
 Proposition  5.1  shows that hypergraph minimality—or, in view of Propositions  4.1 – 4.2 , conditions ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 )—ensures the  strict  monotonicity of  τ ↦ λ τ \tau\mapsto\lambda_{\tau} . Without these geometric assumptions, strict monotonicity may fail (take, e.g.,  w B = 𝟙 | B | = n w_{B}=\mathds{1}_{|B|=n} , in which case  gap k , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) = 1 {\rm gap}_{k,\ast}(\alpha,w)=1  for every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  and  α \alpha ), although the map remains  nondecreasing . Since this weaker statement will not be needed, we leave its verification to the reader. 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.5   (Directional  vs.  componentwise monotonicity) . 
 
 The monotonicity in Proposition  5.1  is  directional : it holds along the rays  τ ⟼ τ ​ α \tau\longmapsto\tau\alpha , but not under arbitrary componentwise increases of the site weights. Indeed, let  n = 3 n=3  and  w B = 𝟙 | B | = 2 w_{B}=\mathds{1}_{|B|=2} .
For
 α = ( 1 , 1 , 1 ) \alpha=(1,1,1) 
and  α ′ = ( s , s , 1 ) \alpha^{\prime}=(s,s,1) ,
 s ≥ 1 s\geq 1 ,
one has  α ≤ α ′ \alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}  componentwise, whereas 
 

 
 (5.15) 
 
 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) = 4 9 ​ , gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α ′ , w ) = 6 ​ s 2 + 19 ​ s + 8 − ( 2 ​ s + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ s 3 + s 2 + 8 ​ s + 16 ) 6 ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ ( 2 ​ s + 1 ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)=\frac{4}{9}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha^{\prime},w)=\frac{6s^{2}+19s+8-\sqrt{(2s+1)(2s^{3}+s^{2}+8s+16)}}{6(s+2)(2s+1)}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Taking  lim s → ∞ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α ′ , w ) = 1 3 < 4 9 \lim_{s\to\infty}{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha^{\prime},w)=\frac{1}{3}<\frac{4}{9} , thus, shows that increasing the site weights componentwise may decrease the two-particle gap. 
 
 
 
 Proposition  5.1  and Remark  5.2  establish Theorem  1.5  for minimal hypergraphs, while Propositions  4.1 – 4.2  lifts this result to the original setting of Theorem  1.5 , thereby recovering the statement in its full form.
Consequently, there exists a unique threshold  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ [ 0 , ∞ ] \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in[0,\infty]  separating the regime in which  gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)  is smaller than  gap 1 ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\tau\alpha,w)  from that in which the reverse inequality holds. To characterize the extremal cases  τ c ​ ( α , w ) ∈ { 0 , ∞ } \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)\in\{0,\infty\} , in Sections  5.2  and  5.3  we analyze the gaps of the limiting dynamics obtained as  τ → ∞ \tau\to\infty  and  τ → 0 \tau\to 0 , respectively. We begin with the former. 
 
 
 
 5.2.  The limit  τ → ∞ \tau\to\infty 

 
 To study the extremal regime  τ → ∞ \tau\to\infty , it is convenient to work directly with the limiting hidden-model dynamics. Indeed, for every nonempty block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , 
 

 
 (5.16) 
 
 Dir ⁡ ( τ ​ α B ) ⟹ δ π B as ​ τ → ∞ ​ , {\rm Dir}(\tau\alpha^{B})\Longrightarrow\delta_{\pi_{B}}\qquad\text{as}\ \tau\to\infty\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  π B \pi_{B}  is the  B B -conditional measure on  [ n ] [n]  given in ( 5.3 ).
Thus, each random Dirichlet redistribution degenerates into the deterministic weighted averaging of the coordinates in  B B . This defines a Markov dynamics also at  τ = ∞ \tau=\infty , namely, the hidden model associated with a block variant of the averaging process. The latter process, originally introduced by Aldous, has received considerable attention in recent years; see, e.g.,
 [ AL12 ,  QS23 ] . 
 
 
 We first introduce this limiting hidden dynamics and relate its generator to  ℒ τ \mathscr{L}_{\tau}  by operator-norm convergence. We then compute its action on the spatial variance, obtaining the limiting lower bound
 λ ∞ ≥ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{\infty}\geq{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) , together with the quantitative finite- τ \tau  estimate from Theorem  1.4 . Finally, we characterize the geometries for which equality holds and establish the strict upper bound
 λ ∞ < 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{\infty}<2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) , including the sharpness of the constant  2 2 . 
 
 
 5.2.1.  The limiting hidden model 

 
 Recall that  π x = α x / α 0 \pi_{x}=\alpha_{x}/\alpha_{0}  and  π ⁡ ( B ) = α ⁡ ( B ) / α 0 \pi(B)=\alpha(B)/\alpha_{0} . For every nonempty  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , the map  K B π K_{B}^{\pi}  replaces all coordinates in  B B  by their weighted average  π B ​ ( θ ) \pi_{B}(\theta)  and leaves the remaining coordinates unchanged. Define, for every  g ∈ ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ g\in\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast} , 
 

 
 (5.17) 
 
 ℒ ∞ ​ g ​ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ( g ⁡ ( K B π ​ θ ) − g ⁡ ( θ ) ) ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ . \mathscr{L}_{\infty}g(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\left(g(K_{B}^{\pi}\theta)-g(\theta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus,  ℒ ∞ \mathscr{L}_{\infty}  is the generator of the hidden model of the block  π \pi -averaging process; see also Section  7.4.1  below. 
 
 
 Next, we rigorously relate  ℒ ∞ \mathscr{L}_{\infty}  to the hidden-model generators  ℒ τ \mathscr{L}_{\tau}  from Section  5.1 . 
 
 
 Lemma 5.6 . 
 
 For every  τ > 0 \tau>0  and  g ∈ ℛ g\in\mathscr{R} , 
 

 
 (5.18) 
 
 ( ℒ τ − ℒ ∞ ) ​ g = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B 1 + τ ​ α ​ ( B ) ​ g ​ ( 𝟏 B ) ​ Var π B ​ . \left(\mathscr{L}_{\tau}-\mathscr{L}_{\infty}\right)g=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}\frac{w_{B}}{1+\tau\alpha(B)}\,g(\mathbf{1}_{B})\,{\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 (5.19) 
 
 ℒ τ | ℛ ⟶ ℒ ∞ | ℛ in operator norm as  ​ τ → ∞ ​ . \mathscr{L}_{\tau}|_{\mathscr{R}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\infty}|_{\mathscr{R}}\qquad\text{in operator norm as }\tau\to\infty\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from the same computation as in ( 5.5 ) by taking  τ 1 = τ \tau_{1}=\tau  and letting  τ 2 → ∞ \tau_{2}\to\infty . Passing to the limit, the operator-norm convergence follows since  ℛ \mathscr{R}  is finite-dimensional.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Finite-dimensional spectral continuity and Proposition  5.1  therefore give (recall ( 5.2 )) 
 

 
 (5.20) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( − ℒ ∞ | ℛ ) ⊆ ( 0 , ∞ ) ​ , λ ∞ ≔ sup τ > 0 λ τ = lim τ → ∞ λ τ = min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − ℒ ∞ | ℛ ) ​ . {\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}_{\infty}|_{\mathscr{R}})\subseteq(0,\infty)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\lambda_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{\tau>0}\lambda_{\tau}=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\lambda_{\tau}=\min{\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}_{\infty}|_{\mathscr{R}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.2.2.  Variance dissipation and gap lower bounds 

 
 We next compute the dissipation of the spatial variance  Var π ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) {\rm Var}_{\pi}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}) . Recall ( 1.84 ) and ( 5.3 )–( 5.4 ), and that the Dirichlet form of the one-particle dynamics with rates ( 1.16 ) reads, for every  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , as 
 

 
 (5.21) 
 
 ℰ 1 ​ ( θ ) ≔ ⟨ θ , − L 1 ​ θ ⟩ π = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ π ​ ( B ) ​ Var π B ​ ( θ ) ​ . \textstyle\mathcal{E}_{1}(\theta)\coloneqq\langle\theta,-L_{1}\theta\rangle_{\pi}=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\,\pi(B)\,{\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}(\theta)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma 5.7 . 
 
 For every  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , 
 

 
 (5.22) 
 
 ℒ ∞ ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) = − ℰ 1 ​ ( θ ) ​ . \mathscr{L}_{\infty}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)=-\mathcal{E}_{1}(\theta)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The formula for total variance gives, for every  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , 
 

 
 (5.23)

 After recalling ( 5.17 ) and ( 5.21 ),
summing with weights  w B w_{B}  proves the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Next, we combine Lemmas  5.6  and  5.7  to prove both the lower bound in Theorem  1.4  and its limiting counterpart, namely, the second inequality in ( 1.38 ).
Recall (cf. Theorem  1.4 ) 
 

 
 (5.24) 
 
 α w , min ≔ min B : w B > 0 α ( B ) . \alpha_{w,\rm min}\coloneqq\min_{B\,:\,w_{B}>0}\alpha(B)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Corollary 5.8 .

 As  π ⁡ ( B ) = α ⁡ ( B ) / α 0 \pi(B)=\alpha(B)/\alpha_{0}  and  s ↦ τ ​ s / ( 1 + τ ​ s ) s\mapsto\tau s/(1+\tau s)  is increasing, recalling ( 5.21 ) and ( 5.24 ),

 where the last step used the variational characterization of the spectral gap of the reversible one-particle dynamics with rates ( 1.16 ).
Let  h τ ∈ 𝒞 ′ ∖ { 0 } h_{\tau}\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}  be a left Perron eigenfunction from Proposition  3.1 .  Remark 5.9   (Sharpness of the lower bound in ( 1.31 )) .

( 5.25 ) is an equality for all  τ > 0 \tau>0 . Indeed, all

 5.2.3.  Geometries corresponding to  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty 

 
 We proceed with the characterization of equality in ( 5.26 ). Here, we impose minimality of the underlying hypergraph induced by  w w . Observe that, under minimality, the weights  w ~ \widetilde{w}  defined in ( 1.48 ) coincide with  w w . The proof of Theorem  1.6 2  and Theorem  1.8 2  will then follow from an application of the reduction procedure from Section  4 . 
 
 
 Proposition 5.10   (Characterization of  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty ) . 
 
 Assume that  w w  induces a minimal hypergraph. Then, 
 

 
 (5.31) 
 
 λ ∞ = gap 1 ( α , w ) ⟺ ∑ B ∋ x , y w B α ⁡ ( B ) does not depend on x , y ∈ [ n ] , x ≠ y . \lambda_{\infty}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{B\ni{x,y}}\frac{w_{B}}{\alpha(B)}\quad\text{does not depend on}\ x,y\in[n],x\neq y\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Equivalently,  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty  if and only if  ( α , w ) (\alpha,w)  satisfies the mean-field condition on the right-hand side of ( 5.31 ). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For distinct  x , y ∈ [ n ] x,y\in[n] , set 
 

 
 (5.32) 
 
 c x ​ y ≔ ∑ B ∋ x , y w B α ⁡ ( B ) ​ . c_{xy}\coloneqq\sum_{B\ni{x,y}}\frac{w_{B}}{\alpha(B)}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, the one-particle Dirichlet form in ( 5.21 ) and the corresponding variance read as 
 

 
 (5.33) 
 
 ℰ 1 ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ α 0 ​ ∑ x , y ∈ [ n ] α x ​ α y ​ c x ​ y ​ ( θ x − θ y ) 2 ​ , Var π ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ α 0 2 ​ ∑ x , y ∈ [ n ] α x ​ α y ​ ( θ x − θ y ) 2 ​ . \mathcal{E}_{1}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\alpha_{x}\alpha_{y}\,c_{xy}\left(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y}\right)^{2}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}^{2}}\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\alpha_{x}\alpha_{y}\left(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y}\right)^{2}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, the condition on the right-hand side of ( 5.31 ) is equivalent to 
 

 
 (5.34) 
 
 ℰ 1 = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ Var π ​ . \mathcal{E}_{1}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w){\rm Var}_{\pi}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 If ( 5.34 ) holds, Lemma  5.7  gives 
 

 
 (5.35) 
 
 ℒ ∞ ​ Var π = − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ Var π ​ . \mathscr{L}_{\infty}{\rm Var}_{\pi}=-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w){\rm Var}_{\pi}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence,  λ ∞ ≤ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{\infty}\leq{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) , and equality follows from ( 5.26 ). 
 
 
 Conversely, suppose that ( 5.34 ) fails. By Lemma  5.7  and the one-particle Poincaré inequality, 
 

 
 (5.36) 
 
 𝒟 ≔ − ℒ ∞ ​ Var π − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ Var π = ℰ 1 − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ Var π ∈ 𝒞 ∖ { 0 } ​ . \mathscr{D}\coloneqq-\mathscr{L}_{\infty}{\rm Var}_{\pi}-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w){\rm Var}_{\pi}=\mathcal{E}_{1}-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w){\rm Var}_{\pi}\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{0\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By Proposition  A.1 , the hidden-model semigroup associated to  ℒ ∞ | ℛ \mathscr{L}_{\infty}|_{\mathscr{R}}  is strongly positive.
Hence, as in Proposition  3.5 , there exists  h ∞ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ′ ) h_{\infty}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})  satisfying 
 

 
 (5.37) 
 
 h ∞ ​ ℒ ∞ = − λ ∞ ​ h ∞ ​ . h_{\infty}\mathscr{L}_{\infty}=-\lambda_{\infty}h_{\infty}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore, recalling that  𝒟 ∈ 𝒞 ∖ { 0 } \mathscr{D}\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{0\}  and  h ∞ ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ′ ) h_{\infty}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}) , 
 

 
 (5.38) 
 
 0 < h ∞ ​ ( 𝒟 ) = ( λ ∞ − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ) ​ h ∞ ​ ( Var π ) ​ . 0<h_{\infty}(\mathscr{D})=\left(\lambda_{\infty}-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\right)h_{\infty}({\rm Var}_{\pi})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  h ∞ ​ ( Var π ) > 0 h_{\infty}({\rm Var}_{\pi})>0 , this proves
 λ ∞ > gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{\infty}>{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) . 
 
 
 Finally, Proposition  5.1  and ( 5.20 ) give
 λ τ < λ ∞ \lambda_{\tau}<\lambda_{\infty} 
for every finite  τ > 0 \tau>0 . Hence  λ ∞ = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{\infty}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)  precisely when
 λ τ < gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{\tau}<{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) 
for every  τ > 0 \tau>0 , which is equivalent to  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.2.4.  Gap upper bounds 

 
 This section is devoted to the proof of the upper bound in Theorem  1.4 .
The idea behind it is quite simple: by lifting a one-particle gap-eigenfunction to the hidden model and considering its square, the failure of the usual derivation rule is measured by the carré-du-champ of  ℒ ∞ \mathscr{L}_{\infty} , which provides the strict correction below  2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) 2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) . 
 
 
 Recall ( 2.44 ) and ( 2.12 )–( 2.13 ); in particular,
for every  ψ : [ n ] → ℝ \psi:[n]\to\mathbb{R}  in  ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} , 
 

 
 (5.39) 
 
 ψ ~ ​ ( θ ) = ⟨ ψ , θ ⟩ π = ∑ x ∈ [ n ] π x ​ ψ ​ ( x ) ​ θ x ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ , \textstyle\widetilde{\psi}(\theta)=\left\langle\psi,\theta\right\rangle_{\pi}=\sum_{x\in[n]}\pi_{x}\,\psi(x)\,\theta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 belongs to  ℛ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{1,\ast} .
Further, recalling that  ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}  and writing  ( g ∘ K B π ) ​ ( θ ) = g ⁡ ( K B π ​ θ ) (g\circ K_{B}^{\pi})(\theta)=g(K_{B}^{\pi}\theta) ,  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , 
 

 
 (5.40) 
 
 Γ ∞ ​ ( g ) ≔ ℒ ∞ ​ ( g 2 ) − 2 ​ g ​ ℒ ∞ ​ g = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ( g ∘ K B π − g ) 2 ​ , g ∈ ℛ ​ , \textstyle\Gamma_{\infty}(g)\coloneqq\mathscr{L}_{\infty}(g^{2})-2g\mathscr{L}_{\infty}g=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\left(g\circ K_{B}^{\pi}-g\right)^{2}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad g\in\mathscr{R}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 denotes the carré-du-champ associated with  ℒ ∞ \mathscr{L}_{\infty} . Clearly,  Γ ∞ ​ ℛ 1 , ∗ ⊆ 𝒞 \Gamma_{\infty}\,\mathscr{R}_{1,\ast}\subseteq\mathscr{C} . 
 
 
 Proposition 5.11 . 
 
 If the hypergraph induced by  w w  is minimal, then 
 

 
 (5.41) 
 
 λ ∞ < 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ . \lambda_{\infty}<2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, by ( 5.2 ) and ( 5.20 ), 
 

 
 (5.42) 
 
 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ​ < 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ , τ > ​ 0 ​ . {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)<2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\tau>0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  ϕ ∈ ℋ 1 , ∗ \phi\in\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}  be a one-particle gap-eigenfunction, so that 
 

 
 (5.43)

 Hence, by an immediate extension of Proposition  2.3  to the case  τ = ∞ \tau=\infty , 
 

 
 (5.44) 
 
 ℒ ∞ ​ ϕ ~ = L 1 ​ ϕ ~ = − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ ϕ ~ ​ . \mathscr{L}_{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}=\widetilde{L_{1}\phi}=-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\widetilde{\phi}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Combining the definition in ( 5.40 ) with
( 5.44 ) gives 
 

 
 (5.45) 
 
 − ℒ ∞ ​ ( ( ϕ ~ ) 2 ) = 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ ( ϕ ~ ) 2 − Γ ∞ ​ ( ϕ ~ ) ​ . -\mathscr{L}_{\infty}((\widetilde{\phi})^{2})=2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)(\widetilde{\phi})^{2}-\Gamma_{\infty}(\widetilde{\phi})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Both  ( ϕ ~ ) 2 (\widetilde{\phi})^{2}  and

 (5.46) 
 
 ( ϕ ~ ) 2 ≠ 0 and Γ ∞ ​ ( ϕ ~ ) ≠ 0 ​ . (\widetilde{\phi})^{2}\neq 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Gamma_{\infty}(\widetilde{\phi})\neq 0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, if  Γ ∞ ​ ( ϕ ~ ) = 0 \Gamma_{\infty}(\widetilde{\phi})=0 , then 
 

 
 (5.47) 
 
 ϕ ~ ∘ K B π = ϕ ~ for every  B ⊆ [ n ]  with  w B > 0 ​ , \widetilde{\phi}\circ K_{B}^{\pi}=\widetilde{\phi}\qquad\text{for every $B\subseteq[n]$ with $w_{B}>0$}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and therefore  ℒ ∞ ​ ϕ ~ = 0 \mathscr{L}_{\infty}\widetilde{\phi}=0 . This contradicts
( 5.44 ), because minimality implies
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) > 0 {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)>0 . 
 
 
 By Proposition  A.1 , there exists a left Perron eigenfunction

Applying  h ∞ h_{\infty}  to ( 5.45 ) yields
