∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.12   (Extension to non-minimal hypergraphs) . 
 
 The conclusion of Proposition  5.11  is stated for minimal hypergraphs, hence, by Propositions  4.1 – 4.2 , under assumptions ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ). The general upper bound in Theorem  1.4  follows by treating separately the two possible failures of these conditions. 
 
 
 If ( 1.36 ) fails, then
 λ τ = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \lambda_{\tau}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)=0 ; see, e.g., Remark  2.4 .
Thus, ( 5.42 ) remains valid with the strict inequality replaced by equality.
Now suppose that ( 1.35 ) fails. By ( 1.34 ) and passing to the limit  τ → ∞ \tau\to\infty , we obtain 
 

 
 (5.49) 
 
 λ ∞ ​ ( α , w ) = λ ∞ ​ ( α , w ∘ ) + w [ n ] ≤ 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ∘ ) + w [ n ] < 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ∘ ) + 2 ​ w [ n ] = 2 ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ , \lambda_{\infty}(\alpha,w)=\lambda_{\infty}(\alpha,w^{\scriptscriptstyle\circ})+w_{[n]}\leq 2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w^{\scriptscriptstyle\circ})+w_{[n]}<2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w^{\scriptscriptstyle\circ})+2w_{[n]}=2{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where for the second step one applies either Proposition  5.11  if  w ∘ ≔ 𝟙 B ≠ [ n ] ​ w w^{\scriptscriptstyle\circ}\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{B\neq[n]}\,w  induces a connected hypergraph, or the preceding disconnected case otherwise. 
 
 
 Thus, the upper bound is strict whenever the hypergraph is connected, while equality occurs in the disconnected case, where both sides vanish. 
 
 
 
 The constant  2 2  in Proposition  5.11  cannot be improved, although equality is never attained in the nondegenerate case. 
 
 
 Remark 5.13   (Sharpness of the upper bound in ( 1.31 )) . 
 
 For every  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , there exist weights  α \alpha  and  w w  such that 
 

 
 (5.50) 
 
 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( α , w ) > ( 2 − ε ) ​ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\alpha,w)>\left(2-\varepsilon\right){\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, the factor  2 2  in the upper bound of Theorem  1.4  is sharp. 
 
 
 A concrete example is the three-site path with 
 

 
 (5.51) 
 
 α = ( 1 , s , 1 ) ​ , ​ s > 0 ​ , w B = { 1 if ​ B = { 1 , 2 } , { 2 , 3 } 0 else ​ . \alpha=(1,s,1)\;\text{,}\;\;s>0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad w_{B}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\ B=\{1,2\},\{2,3\}\\
0&\text{else}\,\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 Writing  c s = ( 1 + s ) − 1 c_{s}=(1+s)^{-1} , a direct computation gives 
 

 
 (5.52) 
 
 gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) = 1 − c s ​ , λ ∞ = lim τ → ∞ gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) = 1 − c s 2 ​ . {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)=1-c_{s}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\lambda_{\infty}=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)=1-c_{s}^{2}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus,  λ ∞ / gap 1 = 1 + c s → 2 \lambda_{\infty}/{\rm gap}_{1}=1+c_{s}\to 2  as  s → 0 s\to 0 . Choosing first  s = s ⁡ ( ε ) > 0 s=s(\varepsilon)>0  sufficiently small and then, by Proposition  5.1 ,  τ = τ ⁡ ( ε ) > 0 \tau=\tau(\varepsilon)>0  sufficiently large proves the claim. 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.  The limit  τ → 0 \tau\to 0 

 
 We now turn to the regime  τ → 0 \tau\to 0 , with the goal of proving the first inequality in ( 1.38 ) and characterizing the geometries for which  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0 . 
 
 
 As  τ → 0 \tau\to 0 , the Dirichlet redistribution law concentrates on the vertices of the simplex: for every nonempty  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 , 
 

 
 (5.53) 
 
 Dir ⁡ ( τ ​ α B ) ⟹ ∑ z ∈ B π z | B ​ e e z as  ​ τ → 0 ​ , {\rm Dir}(\tau\alpha^{B})\Longrightarrow\sum_{z\in B}\pi_{z|B}\,e_{e_{z}}\qquad\text{as }\tau\to 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  π z | B \pi_{z|B}  is defined in ( 5.3 ) and  ( e z ) z ∈ B (e_{z})_{z\in B}  are the vertices of the simplex on  B B . Consequently, in the limiting hidden-model dynamics, when the clock of  B B  rings, a site  Z ∈ B Z\in B  is sampled with law  π B \pi_{B} , all coordinates in  B B  are replaced by the common value  θ Z \theta_{Z} , and the coordinates outside  B B  are left unchanged. 
 
 
 The corresponding particle dynamics admits an equally simple description: all particles lying in the updated block are sent to the same sampled site  Z Z . Thus, once two particles meet, they subsequently move together as a single particle, effectively reducing the system to one with fewer particles. When one restricts attention to a fixed particle number, such mergers are instead recorded as killing: the process is stopped as soon as two particles coalesce. 
 
 
 We shall use both viewpoints. The hidden-model description gives a direct passage from the finite- τ \tau  setup to the limiting dynamics, whereas the particle-system description, through a direct intertwining at  τ = 0 \tau=0 , identifies the limiting eigenvalue with the principal Dirichlet eigenvalue of a killed two-particle chain. 
 
 
 5.3.1.  The limiting hidden model 

 
 We now introduce the candidate limiting hidden-model dynamics obtained by letting  τ → 0 \tau\to 0  in the generators  ℒ τ \mathscr{L}_{\tau} , and make this convergence precise in Lemma  5.14  below. 
 
 
 For every  g ∈ ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ g\in\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast} , define 
 

 
 (5.54) 
 
 ℒ 0 ​ g ​ ( θ ) ≔ ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ∑ z ∈ B π z | B ​ ( g ⁡ ( K B e z ​ θ ) − g ⁡ ( θ ) ) ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ . \mathscr{L}_{0}g(\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\sum_{z\in B}\pi_{z|B}\left(g(K_{B}^{e_{z}}\theta)-g(\theta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma 5.14 . 
 
 For every  τ > 0 \tau>0  and  g ∈ ℛ g\in\mathscr{R} , 
 

 
 (5.55) 
 
 ( ℒ 0 − ℒ τ ) ​ g = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ τ ​ α ​ ( B ) 1 + τ ​ α ​ ( B ) ​ g ​ ( 𝟏 B ) ​ Var π B ​ . \left(\mathscr{L}_{0}-\mathscr{L}_{\tau}\right)g=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\,\frac{\tau\alpha(B)}{1+\tau\alpha(B)}\,g(\mathbf{1}_{B})\,{\rm Var}_{\pi_{B}}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 (5.56) 
 
 ℒ τ | ℛ ⟶ ℒ 0 | ℛ in operator norm as  ​ τ → 0 ​ . \mathscr{L}_{\tau}|_{\mathscr{R}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{0}|_{\mathscr{R}}\qquad\text{in operator norm as }\tau\to 0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first identity follows directly from ( 5.5 ). Since  ℛ \mathscr{R}  is finite-dimensional, letting  τ → 0 \tau\to 0  in that identity also yields convergence in operator norm.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Finite-dimensional spectral continuity and Proposition  5.1  now yield 
 

 
 (5.57) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( − ℒ 0 | ℛ ) ⊆ [ 0 , ∞ ) ​ , λ 0 ≔ inf τ > 0 λ τ = lim τ → 0 λ τ = min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − ℒ 0 | ℛ ) ​ . {\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}_{0}|_{\mathscr{R}})\subseteq[0,\infty)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\lambda_{0}\coloneqq\inf_{\tau>0}\lambda_{\tau}=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\lambda_{\tau}=\min{\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}_{0}|_{\mathscr{R}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.2.  The killed two-particle system 

 
 We next turn the limiting particle description into an exact algebraic statement, by intertwining the hidden-model generator at  τ = 0 \tau=0  with a killed two-particle generator.
Set 
 

 
 (5.58) 
 
 [ n ] ≠ 2 ≔ { ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] 2 : x ≠ y } ​ . [n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\coloneqq\big\{(x,y)\in[n]^{2}:x\neq y\big\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 For every block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , define the killed two-particle transition operator  Π B , 2 † \Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}  on functions  ψ : [ n ] ≠ 2 → ℝ \psi:[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\to\mathbb{R}  by 
 

 
 (5.59) 
 
 Π B , 2 † ​ ψ ​ ( x , y ) ≔ { ψ ⁡ ( x , y ) if ​ x , y ∉ B Π B , 1 ​ ψ ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ​ ( x ) = ∑ z ∈ B π z | B ​ ψ ​ ( z , y ) if ​ x ∈ B , y ∉ B Π B , 1 ​ ψ ​ ( x , ⋅ ) ​ ( y ) = ∑ z ∈ B π z | B ​ ψ ​ ( x , z ) if ​ x ∉ B , y ∈ B 0 if ​ x , y ∈ B ​ . \Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}\psi(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\textstyle\psi(x,y)&\text{if}\ x,y\notin B\\
\textstyle\Pi_{B,1}\psi({\,\cdot\,},y)(x)=\sum_{z\in B}\pi_{z|B}\,\psi(z,y)&\text{if}\ x\in B,y\notin B\\
\textstyle\Pi_{B,1}\psi(x,{\,\cdot\,})(y)=\sum_{z\in B}\pi_{z|B}\,\psi(x,z)&\text{if}\ x\notin B,y\in B\\
0&\text{if}\ x,y\in B\,\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 Accordingly, in analogy with ( 2.4 ), define 
 

 
 (5.60) 
 
 L 2 † ≔ ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ ( Π B , 2 † − I ) ​ . L_{2}^{\dagger}\coloneqq\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\,\big(\Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}-I\big)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Before killing, each particle marginally follows the one-particle dynamics, with each block  B B  ringing at rate  w B w_{B}  and relocating any particle it contains according to  π B \pi_{B} , until the first update involving both particles, at which time they merge and the two-particle process is killed. 
 
 
 Remark 5.15 . 
 
 Even when  w w  induces a connected hypergraph, this killed dynamics need not be irreducible on  [ n ] ≠ 2 [n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq} ; the form and number of its communicating classes depend on the underlying geometry and will play a central role in Proposition  5.18  below. 
 
 
 
 Next, we present an intertwining relation involving  ℒ 0 \mathscr{L}_{0}  and  L 2 † L_{2}^{\dagger} . In what follows, let 
 

 
 (5.61) 
 
 ℋ ≠ ⊗ = ℋ 2 , ∗ , ≠ ⊗ ≔ { ψ : [ n ] ≠ 2 → ℝ } ​ , ℋ ≠ = ℋ 2 , ∗ , ≠ ≔ { ψ ∈ ℋ ≠ ⊗ : symmetric } ​ . \mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}=\mathscr{H}_{2,\ast,{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}\coloneqq\{\psi:[n]_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}=\mathscr{H}_{2,\ast,{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}}\coloneqq\left\{\psi\in\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}:\text{symmetric}\right\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition 5.16   (Intertwining at  τ = 0 \tau=0 ) . 
 
 The map
 Λ : ℋ ≠ → ℛ \Lambda:\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\to\mathscr{R} ,
given by 
 

 
 (5.62) 
 
 Λ ​ ψ ​ ( θ ) ≔ 1 2 ​ ∑ ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 π x ​ π y ​ ψ ​ ( x , y ) ​ ( θ x − θ y ) 2 ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ , ​ ψ ∈ ℋ ≠ ​ , \Lambda\psi(\theta)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(x,y)\in[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}}\pi_{x}\pi_{y}\,\psi(x,y)\left(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y}\right)^{2}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\;\psi\in\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 is a linear isomorphism and satisfies 
 

 
 (5.63) 
 
 ℒ 0 ​ Λ = Λ ​ L 2 † on ​ ℋ ≠ ​ . \mathscr{L}_{0}\Lambda=\Lambda L_{2}^{\dagger}\qquad\text{on}\ \mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , set 
 

 
 (5.64) 
 
 d θ ​ ( x , y ) ≔ ( θ x − θ y ) 2 ​ , ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 ​ . d_{\theta}(x,y)\coloneqq\left(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y}\right)^{2}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad(x,y)\in[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 For every block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , the definitions of the two limiting updates give 
 

 
 (5.65) 
 
 ∑ z ∈ B π z | B ​ d K B e z ​ θ ​ ( x , y ) = Π B , 2 † ​ d θ ​ ( x , y ) ​ , ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 ​ . \sum_{z\in B}\pi_{z|B}\,d_{K_{B}^{e_{z}}\theta}(x,y)=\Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}d_{\theta}(x,y)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad(x,y)\in[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, if exactly one of  x , y x,y  belongs to  B B , both sides average the corresponding coordinate according to  π B \pi_{B} ; if both belong to  B B , both sides vanish. 
 
 
 Moreover,  Π B , 2 † \Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}  is self-adjoint in
 L 2 ​ ( [ n ] ≠ 2 , π ⊗ π ) L^{2}([n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq},\pi\otimes\pi) . For instance, if  x , z ∈ B x,z\in B  and  y ∉ B y\notin B , this follows from
 π x ​ π y ​ π z | B = π z ​ π y ​ π x | B \pi_{x}\pi_{y}\,\pi_{z|B}=\pi_{z}\pi_{y}\,\pi_{x|B} ; the other cases are analogous. Hence, by ( 5.65 ), 
 

 
 (5.66) 
 
 ∑ z ∈ B π z | B ​ Λ ​ ψ ​ ( K B e z ​ θ ) = 1 2 ​ ⟨ ψ , Π B , 2 † ​ d θ ⟩ π ⊗ π = 1 2 ​ ⟨ Π B , 2 † ​ ψ , d θ ⟩ π ⊗ π = Λ ⁡ ( Π B , 2 † ​ ψ ) ​ ( θ ) ​ . \sum_{z\in B}\pi_{z|B}\,\Lambda\psi(K_{B}^{e_{z}}\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\psi,\Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}d_{\theta}\rangle_{\pi\otimes\pi}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}\psi,d_{\theta}\rangle_{\pi\otimes\pi}=\Lambda(\Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}\psi)(\theta)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Subtracting  Λ ​ ψ ​ ( θ ) \Lambda\psi(\theta) , multiplying by  w B w_{B} , and summing over  B B  proves ( 5.63 ). 
 
 
 It remains to prove that  Λ : ℋ ≠ → ℛ \Lambda:\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\to\mathscr{R}  is an isomorphism. Its image consists of homogeneous quadratic polynomials invariant under translations  θ ↦ θ + s ​ 𝟏 \theta\mapsto\theta+s\mathbf{1} , and is therefore contained in  ℛ \mathscr{R}  (Proposition  2.7 ). If  Λ ​ ψ = 0 \Lambda\psi=0 , comparison of the coefficient of  θ x ​ θ y \theta_{x}\theta_{y} , for  x ≠ y x\neq y , gives 
 

 
 (5.67) 
 
 − 2 ​ π x ​ π y ​ ψ ​ ( x , y ) = 0 ​ . -2\pi_{x}\pi_{y}\psi(x,y)=0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus,  ψ = 0 \psi=0 , so  Λ \Lambda  is injective. Since
 dim ℋ ≠ = ( n 2 ) = dim ℛ \dim\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}=\binom{n}{2}=\dim\mathscr{R} ,
it is also surjective.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Since every  Π B , 2 † \Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}  is self-adjoint in
 L 2 ​ ( [ n ] ≠ 2 , π ⊗ π ) L^{2}([n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq},\pi\otimes\pi) , the same holds for  L 2 † L_{2}^{\dagger} . The intertwining in Proposition  5.16  and ( 5.57 ) therefore imply 
 

 
 (5.68) 
 
 λ 0 = min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L 2 † | ℋ ≠ ) ​ . \lambda_{0}=\min{\rm spec}(-L_{2}^{\dagger}|_{\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In fact, the restriction to symmetric functions may be removed: recalling ( 5.61 ), 
 

 
 (5.69) 
 
 λ 0 = min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L 2 † | ℋ ≠ ⊗ ) ​ . \lambda_{0}=\min{\rm spec}(-L_{2}^{\dagger}|_{\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, transposition  ( x , y ) ↦ ( y , x ) (x,y)\mapsto(y,x)  commutes with  L 2 † L_{2}^{\dagger} . Consider a communicating class attaining the smallest principal Dirichlet eigenvalue. If the class is invariant under transposition, uniqueness of its positive principal eigenfunction makes this eigenfunction symmetric. Otherwise, the class and its transpose are distinct, and the sum of their positive principal eigenfunctions is now a symmetric eigenfunction. Thus, the smallest Dirichlet eigenvalue is always attained in  ℋ ≠ \mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq} . 
 
 
 This observation will be crucial below; see, e.g., ( 5.75 ). Indeed, we shall mainly consider antisymmetric  L 2 † L_{2}^{\dagger} -eigenfunctions of the form 
 

 
 (5.70) 
 
 ψ ⁡ ( x , y ) = ϕ ⁡ ( x ) − ϕ ⁡ ( y ) ​ , ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 ​ , \psi(x,y)=\phi(x)-\phi(y)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad(x,y)\in[n]_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{2}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 as
introduced in ( 5.72 ), which inherit their eigenvalues from one-particle eigenfunctions  ϕ \phi . A central part of the analysis will consist in characterizing the geometries for which such a  ψ \psi  has a fixed sign on each communicating class of the killed two-particle dynamics; in that case,  | ψ | |\psi|  is a symmetric, nonnegative eigenfunction with the same eigenvalue. 
 
 
 We may now complete the limiting comparison announced in Section  1.2 . 
 
 
 Proposition 5.17 . 
 
 For all positive site weights  α \alpha  and nonnegative block weights  w w , 
 

 
 (5.71) 
 
 λ 0 = lim τ → 0 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) ≤ gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ . \lambda_{0}=\lim_{\tau\to 0}{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w)\leq{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  0 ≠ ϕ ∈ ℋ 1 , ∗ 0\neq\phi\in\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}  be a nonconstant one-particle eigenfunction satisfying
 L 1 ​ ϕ = − λ ​ ϕ L_{1}\phi=-\lambda\phi , for some  λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 .
Define  𝒟 ​ ϕ ∈ ℋ ≠ ⊗ \mathcal{D}\phi\in\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  as the anti-symmetric function given by 
 

 
 (5.72) 
 
 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ ( x , y ) ≔ ϕ ⁡ ( x ) − ϕ ⁡ ( y ) ​ , ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 ​ . \mathcal{D}\phi(x,y)\coloneqq\phi(x)-\phi(y)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad(x,y)\in[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 For every block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n] , a direct verification gives, for every  ϕ ∈ ℋ 1 \phi\in\mathscr{H}_{1} , 
 

 
 (5.73) 
 
 Π B , 2 † ​ 𝒟 ​ ϕ = 𝒟 ​ Π B , 1 ​ ϕ ​ . \Pi_{B,2}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}\phi=\mathcal{D}\Pi_{B,1}\phi\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, when both particles belong to  B B , both sides vanish, since  Π B , 1 ​ ϕ \Pi_{B,1}\phi  is constant on  B B . Consequently, 
 

 
 (5.74) 
 
 L 2 † ​ 𝒟 ​ ϕ = 𝒟 ​ L 1 ​ ϕ = − λ ​ 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ . L_{2}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}\phi=\mathcal{D}L_{1}\phi=-\lambda\mathcal{D}\phi\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  ϕ \phi  is nonconstant,  𝒟 ​ ϕ ≠ 0 \mathcal{D}\phi\neq 0 . Hence, ( 5.74 ) shows that  𝒟 ​ ϕ ∈ ℋ ≠ ⊗ \mathcal{D}\phi\in\mathscr{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  is an anti-symmetric eigenfunction of  L 2 † L_{2}^{\dagger}  with eigenvalue  λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 . By ( 5.68 )–( 5.69 ), 
 

 
 (5.75) 
 
 λ 0 ≤ λ ​ . \lambda_{0}\leq\lambda\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The claim now follows from
 λ 0 = lim τ → 0 gap 2 , ∗ ​ ( τ ​ α , w ) \lambda_{0}=\lim_{\tau\to 0}{\rm gap}_{2,\ast}(\tau\alpha,w) ,
as established in ( 5.57 ), by taking  λ = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.3.  Geometries corresponding to  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0 

 
 The preceding section identifies  λ 0 \lambda_{0}  with the principal Dirichlet eigenvalue of the killed two-particle system. We now characterize equality in ( 5.71 ), which, by strict monotonicity, is precisely the regime  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0 . 
 
 
 Proposition 5.18   (Characterization of  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0 ) . 
 
 Assume that  w w  induces a minimal hypergraph. Then, for every positive  α \alpha , the following are equivalent: 
 
 (1)  
 
 τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0 ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 λ 0 = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda_{0}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) ; 
 
 (3)  
 
 after a relabeling of  [ n ] [n] , every  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  and  | B | ≥ 2 |B|\geq 2  is an interval. 
 
 
 Whenever these conditions hold, 
 

 
 (5.76) 
 
 λ 0 = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ < λ τ ​ , τ > ​ 0 ​ . \lambda_{0}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)<\lambda_{\tau}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\tau>0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Proposition  5.17  and the strict monotonicity in Proposition  5.1 , 
 

 
 (5.77) 
 
 τ c ( α , w ) = 0 ⟺ λ 0 = gap 1 ( α , w ) . \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\lambda_{0}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 We next prove that equality in ( 5.77 ) forces the interval geometry. Let  ϕ \phi  be a nonconstant one-particle eigenfunction associated to  λ = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) \lambda={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) , and let
 a 1 < a 2 < ⋯ < a m a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{m} 
be its distinct values, with level sets 
 

 
 (5.78) 
 
 N j ≔ { x ∈ [ n ] : ϕ ⁡ ( x ) = a j } ​ , j = 1 , … , m ​ . N_{j}\coloneqq\big\{x\in[n]:\phi(x)=a_{j}\big\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad j=1,\ldots,m\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  ϕ \phi  is nonconstant,  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 . By the intertwining used in the proof of Proposition  5.17 , 
 

 
 (5.79) 
 
 L 2 † ​ 𝒟 ​ ϕ = − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ 𝒟 ​ ϕ = − λ 0 ​ 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ . L_{2}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}\phi=-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\mathcal{D}\phi=-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{D}\phi\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore, by the Perron–Frobenius discussion following ( 5.69 ), the restriction of  𝒟 ​ ϕ \mathcal{D}\phi  to each communicating class of the killed chain is either identically zero or has a strict sign. 
 
 
 Fix  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 . Suppose first that  B B  meets  N i N_{i}  and  N k N_{k} , with  i < k i<k , but misses some  N j N_{j} ,  i < j < k i<j<k . Choose 
 

 
 (5.80) 
 
 x ∈ B ∩ N i ​ , y ∈ N j ∖ B ​ , z ∈ B ∩ N k ​ . x\in B\cap N_{i}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad y\in N_{j}\setminus B\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad z\in B\cap N_{k}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The states  ( x , y ) (x,y)  and  ( z , y ) (z,y)  communicate through  B B -updates of the  L 2 † L_{2}^{\dagger} -dynamics, whereas 
 

 
 (5.81) 
 
 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ ( x , y ) = a i − a j < 0 ​ , 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ ( z , y ) = a k − a j > 0 ​ , \mathcal{D}\phi(x,y)=a_{i}-a_{j}<0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathcal{D}\phi(z,y)=a_{k}-a_{j}>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 contradicting the preceding sign property. Hence, the level sets met by  B B  have consecutive indices. 
 
 
 Suppose next that  B B  meets at least two level sets but does not contain one of them. Then, for some  i ≠ j i\neq j , we may choose 
 

 
 (5.82) 
 
 x ∈ B ∩ N i ​ , y ∈ N i ∖ B ​ , z ∈ B ∩ N j ​ . x\in B\cap N_{i}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad y\in N_{i}\setminus B\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad z\in B\cap N_{j}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Again,  ( x , y ) (x,y)  and  ( z , y ) (z,y)  communicate, but 
 

 
 (5.83) 
 
 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ ( x , y ) = 0 ​ , 𝒟 ​ ϕ ​ ( z , y ) = a j − a i ≠ 0 ​ , \mathcal{D}\phi(x,y)=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathcal{D}\phi(z,y)=a_{j}-a_{i}\neq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which is impossible on a communicating class on which  𝒟 ​ ϕ \mathcal{D}\phi  is either identically zero or strictly signed. Thus, every positive-weight block is either contained in one level set or is a union of consecutive complete level sets. The partition 
 

 
 (5.84) 
 
 [ n ] = N 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N m [n]=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{m} 
 
 
 is therefore  w w -compatible. Minimality and  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  force  m = n m=n , so every  N j N_{j}  is a singleton. Relabeling the sites according to the increasing values of  ϕ \phi  proves the interval condition. 
 
 
 Conversely, assume that the sites are labeled so that every positive-weight
block of size at least two is an interval. Since every  Π B , 1 \Pi_{B,1}  preserves
nondecreasing functions, the one-particle semigroup is monotone. Hence, by
 [ LP17 , Lemma 22.17] , there exists a nonconstant
nondecreasing eigenfunction  ϕ \phi  such that 
 

 
 (5.85) 
 
 L 1 ​ ϕ = − gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ ϕ ​ . L_{1}\phi=-{\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\phi\,\text{.} 
 
 
 We claim that  ϕ \phi  is strictly increasing. 
 
 
 Suppose that  ϕ ⁡ ( i ) = ϕ ⁡ ( i + 1 ) \phi(i)=\phi(i+1)  for some  i = 1 , … , n − 1 i=1,\ldots,n-1 . Taking the difference
of ( 5.85 ) at  i + 1 i+1  and  i i  gives 
 

 
 (5.86) 
 
 0 = L 1 ​ ϕ ​ ( i + 1 ) − L 1 ​ ϕ ​ ( i ) = ∑ B ∋ i + 1 B ∌ i w B ​ ( π B ​ ( ϕ ) − ϕ ⁡ ( i + 1 ) ) + ∑ B ∋ i B ∌ i + 1 w B ​ ( ϕ ⁡ ( i ) − π B ​ ( ϕ ) ) ​ . 0=L_{1}\phi(i+1)-L_{1}\phi(i)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\ni i+1\\
B\not\ni i\end{subarray}}w_{B}\bigl(\pi_{B}(\phi)-\phi(i+1)\bigr)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\ni i\\
B\not\ni i+1\end{subarray}}w_{B}\bigl(\phi(i)-\pi_{B}(\phi)\bigr)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Every term on the right-hand side is nonnegative. Indeed, an interval containing
 i + 1 i+1  but not  i i  lies to the right of  i i , while an interval containing  i i  but
not  i + 1 i+1  lies to its left. Consequently, equality in ( 5.86 )
implies that  ϕ \phi  is constant on every positive-weight block starting at
 i + 1 i+1  or ending at  i i .
Let
 [ n ] = N 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N m [n]=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{m} 
be the partition into the level sets of  ϕ \phi , ordered according to their
values. Since  ϕ \phi  is nondecreasing, the sets  N j N_{j}  are intervals. We claim
that every positive-weight block  B B  is either contained in one  N j N_{j} , or is a
union of consecutive level sets.
Indeed, suppose that  B B  meets more than one level set and starts strictly
inside some  N j N_{j} . If  a = min ⁡ B a=\min B , then  ϕ ⁡ ( a − 1 ) = ϕ ⁡ ( a ) \phi(a-1)=\phi(a) , while  B B  starts
at  a a  and contains points where  ϕ > ϕ ⁡ ( a ) \phi>\phi(a) . This contradicts the conclusion
drawn from ( 5.86 ). The analogous argument at  max ⁡ B \max B  shows
that  B B  cannot end strictly inside a level set. The claim follows. 
 
 
 Thus, the partition  ( N 1 , … , N m ) (N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})  is  w w -compatible. Since  ϕ \phi  is
nonconstant,  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , while minimality excludes  2 ≤ m < n 2\leq m<n . Therefore
 m = n m=n , and 
 

 
 (5.87) 
 
 ϕ ⁡ ( 1 ) < ϕ ⁡ ( 2 ) < ⋯ < ϕ ⁡ ( n ) ​ . \phi(1)<\phi(2)<\cdots<\phi(n)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Before killing, the two-particle dynamics cannot change the order of the two coordinates: if an interval  B B  contains exactly one coordinate of  ( x , y ) (x,y) , relocating that coordinate inside  B B  cannot move it across the other one. Hence, every communicating class is contained in one of the two sets 
 

 
 (5.88) 
 
 { ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 : x < y } or { ( x , y ) ∈ [ n ] ≠ 2 : x > y } ​ . \big\{(x,y)\in[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}:x<y\big\}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\big\{(x,y)\in[n]^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\neq}:x>y\big\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By ( 5.87 ),  𝒟 ​ ϕ \mathcal{D}\phi  has a strict sign on every communicating class. Since it is a  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w) -eigenfunction of  − L 2 † -L_{2}^{\dagger} , Perron–Frobenius theory shows that  gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) {\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)  is the principal Dirichlet eigenvalue on every class. Recalling ( 5.68 )–( 5.69 ), we obtain 
 

 
 (5.89) 
 
 λ 0 = gap 1 ​ ( α , w ) ​ . \lambda_{0}={\rm gap}_{1}(\alpha,w)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The equivalence ( 5.77 ) and strict monotonicity then give ( 5.76 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.4.  Proof of Theorems  1.4 ,  1.5 ,  1.6  and  1.8 

 
 We collect the results established above. 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  1.4 . 
 
 The lower bound was proved in Corollary  5.8 , while the upper bound and its extension to general hypergraphs were established in Proposition  5.11  and Remark  5.12 . Sharpness of the two constants was proved in Remarks  5.9  and  5.13 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  1.5 . 
 
 For minimal hypergraphs, the claim follows from Proposition  5.1  and Remark  5.2 . The general case follows from Propositions  4.1 – 4.2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorems  1.6  and  1.8 . 
 
 For minimal hypergraphs, the characterizations of  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty  and  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0  were established in Propositions  5.10  and  5.18 , respectively. 
 
 
 We now remove the minimality assumption, retaining only ( 1.35 )–( 1.36 ). By Proposition  4.2 , passing to any  w w -compatible partition preserves  gap 1 {\rm gap}_{1}  and  gap 2 , ∗ {\rm gap}_{2,\ast}  at every scale  τ > 0 \tau>0 , and hence also  λ 0 \lambda_{0} ,  λ ∞ \lambda_{\infty} , and  τ c \tau_{c} . Moreover, the quotient hypergraph associated with the coarsest partition is minimal (Proposition  4.1 ). Applying Proposition  5.18  to this quotient shows that  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = 0 \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=0  if and only if, after ordering the quotient sites, every positive-weight quotient block is an interval. This is precisely the segment-like condition in Theorem  1.8 1 . Likewise, Proposition  5.10  yields the corresponding characterization of  τ c ​ ( α , w ) = ∞ \tau_{c}(\alpha,w)=\infty . This proves Theorem  1.8 . 
 
 
 Theorem  1.6  then follows from the observation following the statement of its hypergraph counterpart.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.  Boundary-driven  KMP {\rm KMP} 

 
 In this section, we prove Theorem  1.11 . The argument is based on a suitable decomposition of polynomials and on a killed-particle representation of their highest-degree components. In particular, it does not use the hidden model associated with the boundary-driven dynamics. That representation has instead played a central role in the description of the corresponding nonequilibrium steady state
 [ DMFG24 ,  GRT25 ] . 
 
 
 6.1.  Polynomials and killed particles 

 
 Recall from ( 2.1 ) and ( 1.20 ) that
 ℋ k ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  denotes the space of functions  ψ : [ n ] k → ℝ \psi:[n]^{k}\to\mathbb{R} , endowed with the
 L 2 ​ ( μ k ) L^{2}(\mu_{k})  scalar product, whereas  ℋ k ⊆ ℋ k ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  is the
subspace of symmetric functions. The generator  L k L_{k}  acts on
 ℋ k ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  and describes the labeled  k k -particle dynamics, while
 L k sym = L k | ℋ k L_{k}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}=L_{k}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k}}  describes its unlabeled counterpart.
The same hat notation as in ( 2.13 ) will be used for
polynomials on  Ω bd \Omega_{\rm bd} : for  ψ ∈ ℋ k \psi\in\mathscr{H}_{k} , 
 

 
 (6.1) 
 
 ψ ^ ( η ) ≔ ∑ x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] ψ ( x 1 , … , x k ) η x 1 ⋯ η x k , η ∈ Ω bd . \textstyle\widehat{\psi}(\eta)\coloneqq\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]}\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\,\eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega_{\rm bd}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  Ω bd \Omega_{\rm bd}  has nonempty interior, the map
in ( 6.1 ) is a linear isomorphism between  ℋ k \mathscr{H}_{k}  and
the homogeneous polynomials of degree  k k  on  Ω bd \Omega_{\rm bd} . 
 
 
 Recall from Section  1.3.2  that  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  denotes the space of polynomials of degree at most
 k k  on  Ω bd \Omega_{\rm bd} , with  𝒬 − 1 ≔ 0 \mathscr{Q}_{-1}\coloneqq{0} . The generator
 ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  preserves each  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k} , but its action is no longer
homogeneous: a reservoir update produces terms of degree strictly smaller than
the original one. In Proposition  6.1  below we identify the action of  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  on the
highest-degree component of a polynomial. 
 
 
 First, we introduce some notation. Recall ( 1.62 )–( 1.63 ) and ( 2.39 ), and define, for  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , the multiplication operator  L ∂ , k L_{\partial,k}  on  ℋ k ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  by 
 

 
 (6.2) 
 
 L ∂ , k ψ ( x 1 , … , x k ) ≔ − ψ ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ω x ( 1 − 𝐄 x [ U x 𝔫 x ​ ( x 1 , … , x k ) ] ) , \textstyle L_{\partial,k}\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\coloneqq-\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\sum_{x\in[n]}\omega_{x}\,(1-\mathbf{E}_{x}\big[U_{x}^{\mathfrak{n}_{x}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})}\big])\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for  x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n] .
Here,  ω x \omega_{x} ,  U x U_{x}  and  𝐄 x \mathbf{E}_{x}  are as in
( 1.61 )–( 1.62 ):  𝐄 x \mathbf{E}_{x}  denotes expectation with respect to the
reservoir variables  ( U x , ξ x ) (U_{x},\xi_{x})  associated with an update at  x x , while  ω x ≥ 0 \omega_{x}\geq 0  denotes the rate of such updates.
The corresponding boundary-driven particle generator is 
 

 
 (6.3) 
 
 L bd , k ≔ L k + L ∂ , k ​ , L bd , k sym ≔ L bd , k | ℋ k ​ . L_{{\rm bd},k}\coloneqq L_{k}+L_{\partial,k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad L_{{\rm bd},k}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}}\coloneqq L_{{\rm bd},k}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k}}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  L k L_{k}  is self-adjoint on  L 2 ​ ( μ k ) = L 2 ​ ( [ n ] k , μ k ) L^{2}(\mu_{k})=L^{2}([n]^{k},\mu_{k})  and  L ∂ , k L_{\partial,k}  is a nonpositive multiplication operator,  L bd , k L_{{\rm bd},k}  is self-adjoint and sub-Markovian. Moreover, it commutes with permutations of the labels, so that  ℋ k \mathscr{H}_{k}  is invariant. 
 
 
 The operator in ( 6.2 ) has the following probabilistic interpretation. At a reservoir update at  x x , conditionally on  U x U_{x} , each particle at  x x  survives independently with probability  U x U_{x} ; the process is killed as soon as at least one particle does not survive. Thus,  𝐄 x ​ [ U x 𝔫 x ] \mathbf{E}_{x}[U_{x}^{\mathfrak{n}_{x}}]  is precisely the probability that all particles currently at  x x  survive the update. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.1   (Top-degree intertwining) . 
 
 For every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  and every  ψ ∈ ℋ k \psi\in\mathscr{H}_{k} , 
 

 
 (6.4) 
 
 ℒ bd ​ ψ ^ − L bd , k sym ​ ψ ^ ∈ 𝒬 k − 1 ​ . \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}\widehat{\psi}-\widehat{L_{{\rm bd},k}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}}\psi}\in\mathscr{Q}_{k-1}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, the action induced by  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  on the quotient space 
 

 
 (6.5) 
 
 𝒬 k / 𝒬 k − 1 = { [ f ] k ≔ f + 𝒬 k − 1 : f ∈ 𝒬 k } \mathscr{Q}_{k}/\mathscr{Q}_{k-1}=\left\{[f]_{k}\coloneqq f+\mathscr{Q}_{k-1}:f\in\mathscr{Q}_{k}\right\} 
 
 
 is similar to  L bd , k sym L_{{\rm bd},k}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}}  and does not depend on  ρ \rho . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The conservative part satisfies the exact intertwining
 ℒ ​ ψ ^ = L k sym ​ ψ ^ \mathcal{L}\widehat{\psi}=\widehat{L_{k}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}}\psi}  by Proposition  2.3 . It remains to identify the highest-degree part of the reservoir generator. 
 
 
 Fix  x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]  and write  𝔫 x = 𝔫 x ​ ( x 1 , … , x k ) \mathfrak{n}_{x}=\mathfrak{n}_{x}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}) . For the monomial  η x 1 ⋯ η x k \eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}} , a reservoir update at  x x  gives 
 

 
 (6.6) 
 
 𝐄 x [ ∏ i = 1 k η x i x , U x , ξ x ] = 𝐄 x [ U x 𝔫 x ( η x + ξ x ) 𝔫 x ] ∏ y ≠ x η y 𝔫 y = 𝐄 x [ U x 𝔫 x ] η x 1 ⋯ η x k + q x ( η ) , \mathbf{E}_{x}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta^{x,U_{x},\xi_{x}}_{x_{i}}\bigg]=\mathbf{E}_{x}[U_{x}^{\mathfrak{n}_{x}}(\eta_{x}+\xi_{x})^{\mathfrak{n}_{x}}]\prod_{y\neq x}\eta_{y}^{\mathfrak{n}_{y}}=\mathbf{E}_{x}[U_{x}^{\mathfrak{n}_{x}}]\,\eta_{x_{1}}\cdots\eta_{x_{k}}+q_{x}(\eta)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  q x ∈ 𝒬 k − 1 q_{x}\in\mathscr{Q}_{k-1} . Indeed, every term in the binomial expansion of  ( η x + ξ x ) 𝔫 x (\eta_{x}+\xi_{x})^{\mathfrak{n}_{x}}  containing a positive power of  ξ x \xi_{x}  has degree at most  k − 1 k-1  in  η \eta  and, moreover,  ξ x ∼ Gamma ⁡ ( β x , ρ x ) \xi_{x}\sim{\rm Gamma}(\beta_{x},\rho_{x})  has finite moments of all orders. Multiplying ( 6.6 ) by the coefficients of  ψ \psi , summing over  x 1 , … , x k x_{1},\ldots,x_{k} , and then over the reservoir sites gives 
 

 
 (6.7) 
 
 ℒ ∂ ​ ψ ^ − L ∂ , k ​ ψ ^ ∈ 𝒬 k − 1 ​ . \mathcal{L}_{\partial}\widehat{\psi}-\widehat{L_{\partial,k}\psi}\in\mathscr{Q}_{k-1}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Together with the conservative intertwining, this proves ( 6.4 ). The operator  L bd , k L_{{\rm bd},k}  depends on the reservoir variables only through the moments of  U x U_{x} , and hence is independent of  ρ \rho .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Every polynomial in  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  decomposes uniquely into homogeneous
components. By ( 6.1 ), the homogeneous component of degree  j j  is
identified with a unique element of  ℋ j \mathscr{H}_{j} . Equivalently, the hat map
identifies the quotient  𝒬 j / 𝒬 j − 1 \mathscr{Q}_{j}/\mathscr{Q}_{j-1}  with
 ℋ j \mathscr{H}_{j} . Proposition  6.1  therefore gives the
following spectral decomposition. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.2   (Polynomial spectrum) . 
 
 For every  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , counting algebraic multiplicities, 
 

 
 (6.8) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( − ℒ bd | 𝒬 k ) = { 0 } ∪ ⋃ j = 1 k spec ⁡ ( − L bd , j sym ) ⊆ [ 0 , ∞ ) ​ . {\rm spec}(-\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{k}})=\{0\}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}{\rm spec}(-L_{{\rm bd},j}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}})\subseteq[0,\infty)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, the polynomial spectrum of  − ℒ bd -\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  is real, nonnegative, and independent of  ρ \rho . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Choose a basis of  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  adapted to the filtration 
 

 
 (6.9) 
 
 𝒬 0 ⊆ 𝒬 1 ⊆ ⋯ ⊆ 𝒬 k ​ . \mathscr{Q}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{Q}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathscr{Q}_{k}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By Proposition  6.1 , the matrix of  ℒ bd | 𝒬 k \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{k}}  is block triangular, with diagonal blocks  0 0  and  L bd , j sym L_{{\rm bd},j}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}} ,  1 ≤ j ≤ k 1\leq j\leq k . This proves ( 6.8 ). 
 
 
 Each  L bd , j sym L_{{\rm bd},j}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}}  is the restriction of the self-adjoint, nonpositive operator  L bd , j L_{{\rm bd},j}  to an invariant subspace. Its spectrum is therefore real and nonpositive. The final claim follows from the last statement of Proposition  6.1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 6.3   (The reversible case) . 
 
 The above characterization of the polynomial spectrum of  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}  is valid in the general, not necessarily reversible, case.
In the special case in which  ρ x ≡ ρ ∗ \rho_{x}\equiv\rho_{\ast}  on  supp ⁡ ( ω ) {\rm supp}(\omega) , the product Gamma measure in ( 1.64 ) is reversible. Then, self-adjointness and ( 1.65 ) imply that the orthogonal complement of  𝒬 k − 1 \mathscr{Q}_{k-1}  in  𝒬 k \mathscr{Q}_{k}  is invariant under  ℒ bd \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd} . Since polynomials are dense in  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu) , the generator admits a complete orthonormal family of polynomial eigenfunctions, and its  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu) -spectrum coincides with the closure of the polynomial spectrum in ( 6.8 ). In particular, the polynomial gap in ( 1.67 ) is the usual  L 2 ​ ( ν ) L^{2}(\nu) -spectral gap. 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.  One-particle domination 

 
 We next compare the principal eigenvalues of the killed particle generators. The comparison is most transparent through their common graphical construction. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.4   (Killing-time comparison) . 
 
 For every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (6.10) 
 
 min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , k ) ≥ min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , 1 ) ​ . \min{\rm spec}\left(-L_{{\rm bd},k}\right)\geq\min{\rm spec}\left(-L_{{\rm bd},1}\right)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  T k T_{k}  denote the killing time of the labeled  k k -particle process generated by  L bd , k L_{{\rm bd},k} . Couple simultaneously the systems with different numbers of particles as follows. At each bulk update, use the same block and the same Dirichlet vector, and move all particles in that block conditionally independently given this vector. At a reservoir update at  x x , use the same  U x U_{x}  and let each particle at  x x  survive independently with probability  U x U_{x} . 
 
 
 For  i = 1 , … , k i=1,\ldots,k , let  T k ( i ) T_{k}^{(i)}  be the killing time of the  i i -th label under this construction. Each label has the one-particle dynamics generated by  L bd , 1 L_{{\rm bd},1} , and 
 

 
 (6.11) 
 
 T k ≔ min 1 ≤ j ≤ k ⁡ T k ( j ) ≤ T k ( i ) a.s. ​ , T_{k}\coloneqq\min_{1\leq j\leq k}T_{k}^{(j)}\leq T_{k}^{(i)}\qquad\text{a.s.}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the inequality holds for every  i = 1 , … , k i=1,\ldots,k . Hence, for all  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 
 

 
 (6.12) 
 
 max x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] ⁡ ℙ x 1 , … , x k ​ ( T k > t ) ≤ max x 1 , … , x k ∈ [ n ] ⁡ ℙ x 1 , … , x k ​ ( T k ( i ) > t ) = max x ∈ [ n ] ⁡ ℙ x ​ ( T 1 > t ) ​ , \max_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]}\mathds{P}_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}(T_{k}>t)\leq\max_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in[n]}\mathds{P}_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}(T_{k}^{(i)}>t)=\max_{x\in[n]}\mathds{P}_{x}(T_{1}>t)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ℙ x 1 , … , x k \mathds{P}_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}  stands for the law of the  L bd , k L_{{\rm bd},k}  process started from
 ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∈ [ n ] k (x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in[n]^{k} , and  ℙ x \mathds{P}_{x}  for the law of the one-particle
process started from  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] . The equality in ( 6.12 ) follows because, under  ℙ x 1 , … , x k \mathds{P}_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}} , the marginal law of the  i i -th label is exactly that of the one-particle process started from  x i x_{i} . 
 
 
 For any finite sub-Markov generator  A A , positivity of its semigroup gives 
 

 
 (6.13) 
 
 ‖ e t ​ A ‖ ∞ → ∞ = max z ⁡ ( e t ​ A ​ 𝟏 ) ​ ( z ) = max z ⁡ ℙ z ​ ( T > t ) ​ , t ≥ 0 ​ , \|e^{tA}\|_{\infty\to\infty}=\max_{z}\,(e^{tA}\mathbf{1})(z)=\max_{z}\mathds{P}_{z}(T>t)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad t\geq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  T ≥ 0 T\geq 0  denotes the random killing time of the corresponding continuous-time chain.
Hence, the above identity and Gelfand’s formula yield 
 

 
 (6.14) 
 
 lim t → ∞ 1 t ​ log ⁡ max z ​ ℙ z ​ ( T > t ) = max ⁡ spec ⁡ ( A ) = − min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − A ) ​ . \lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\max_{z}\mathds{P}_{z}(T>t)=\max{\rm spec}(A)=-\min{\rm spec}(-A)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Applying ( 6.14 ) to both sides of ( 6.12 ) proves ( 6.10 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  1.11 . 
 
 Since  ℋ k ⊆ ℋ k ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  and  L bd , k L_{{\rm bd},k}  is self-adjoint, 
 

 
 (6.15) 
 
 min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , k sym ) ≥ min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , k ) ≥ min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , 1 ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . \min{\rm spec}(-L_{{\rm bd},k}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}})\geq\min{\rm spec}(-L_{{\rm bd},k})\geq\min{\rm spec}(-L_{{\rm bd},1})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, the second inequality is Lemma  6.4 , while the first is the variational characterization of the smallest eigenvalue. Since  ℋ 1 = ℋ 1 ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{1}=\mathscr{H}_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes} , equality holds throughout ( 6.15 ) when  k = 1 k=1 . 
 
 
 Proposition  6.2  now gives, for every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (6.16) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd | 𝒬 k ) = min 1 ≤ j ≤ k ⁡ min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , j sym ) = min ⁡ spec ⁡ ( − L bd , 1 ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd | 𝒬 1 ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{k}})=\min_{1\leq j\leq k}\min{\rm spec}(-L_{{\rm bd},j}^{{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle sym}}})=\min{\rm spec}(-L_{{\rm bd},1})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}|_{\mathscr{Q}_{1}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Taking the infimum over  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  proves ( 1.68 ). Independence of  ρ \rho  follows from Proposition  6.2 . 
 
 
 If this common gap is positive, let  ϕ ∈ ℋ 1 ∖ { 0 } \phi\in\mathscr{H}_{1}\setminus\{0\}  satisfy
 L bd , 1 ​ ϕ = − gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) ​ ϕ L_{{\rm bd},1}\phi=-{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})\,\phi . Proposition  6.1  gives, for some constant  c ∈ ℝ c\in\mathbb{R} , 
 

 
 (6.17) 
 
 ℒ bd ​ ϕ ^ = − gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) ​ ϕ ^ + c ​ . \mathcal{L}_{\rm bd}\widehat{\phi}=-{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})\,\widehat{\phi}+c\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore,  ϕ ^ − c / gap ⁡ ( ℒ bd ) \widehat{\phi}-c/{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm bd})  is a degree-one eigenfunction associated with the gap.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 6.5   (Further reservoir mechanisms) . 
 
 The family in ( 1.61 ) interpolates between several reservoir mechanisms considered in the literature. The choice 
 

 
 (6.18) 
 
 β x = α x \beta_{x}=\alpha_{x} 
 
 
 corresponds to the boundary update used, for instance, in
 [ DMFG24 ,  GRT25 ] . For finite  β x \beta_{x} , the factor  U x U_{x}  retains part of the energy present at  x x  before the update. At the opposite extreme, 
 

 
 (6.19) 
 
 U x ​ ( η x + ξ x ) ⟹ ξ x ′ ∼ Gamma ⁡ ( α x , ρ x ) ​ , as ​ β x → ∞ ​ , U_{x}(\eta_{x}+\xi_{x})\ \Longrightarrow\ \xi_{x}^{\prime}\sim{\rm Gamma}(\alpha_{x},\rho_{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{as}\ \beta_{x}\to\infty\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with  ξ x ′ \xi_{x}^{\prime}  being independent of  η x \eta_{x} , and one recovers the full-refresh mechanism
 [ KMP82 ] ; see also  [ CGGR13 ] . In particular, the original KMP reservoirs correspond, up to the choice of the boundary rates and temperatures, to  α x = 1 \alpha_{x}=1  and  β x = ∞ \beta_{x}=\infty : at each boundary update, the energy is replaced by an exponential random variable with mean  ρ x \rho_{x} . Conversely, as  β x → 0 \beta_{x}\to 0 , one has  U x → 1 U_{x}\to 1  and  ξ x → 0 \xi_{x}\to 0  in probability, so that the reservoir action becomes void. One may also allow  ρ x = 0 \rho_{x}=0 , interpreting  ξ x = 0 \xi_{x}=0  almost surely, in which case the update only removes energy through the multiplication  η x ⟼ U x ​ η x \eta_{x}\longmapsto U_{x}\eta_{x} . 
 
 
 More generally, our proof does not rely on the beta–gamma form of the variables in ( 1.61 ). It only uses that, at an update of the reservoir attached to  x x , the energy is replaced by 
 

 
 (6.20) 
 
 η x ⟼ U x ​ ( η x + ξ x ) ​ , \eta_{x}\longmapsto U_{x}(\eta_{x}+\xi_{x})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ( U x , ξ x ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] × [ 0 , ∞ ) (U_{x},\xi_{x})\in[0,1]\times[0,\infty)  is sampled independently of the current configuration and has finite moments of every order. The variables  U x U_{x}  and  ξ x \xi_{x}  need not be independent. The same top-degree computation gives ( 6.2 ), and the killing-time argument is unchanged. The full-refresh update is covered directly by setting the coefficient of every positive-degree term equal to zero. Analogous block-reservoir mechanisms may be treated in the same way, at the cost of heavier notation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.  Stochastic exchange models 

 
 The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem  1.12  from
Section  1.3.3 . We begin by giving a rigorous construction of
stochastic exchange models together with their associated hidden dynamics.
The core of the proof of Theorem  1.12  occupies
Sections  7.2 – 7.3 , whereas examples of stochastic exchange models are
discussed in Section  7.4 . 
 
 
 7.1.  Construction and first properties 

 
 Fix  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 .
Throughout, let 
 

 
 (7.1) 
 
 𝔐 ≔ { M = ( M x ​ y ) x , y ∈ [ n ] ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] n × n : M ​ 𝟏 = 𝟏 } ⊆ ℝ n × n \mathfrak{M}\coloneqq\{M=(M_{xy})_{x,y\in[n]}\in[0,1]^{n\times n}:M\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n\times n} 
 
 
 denote the compact space of row-stochastic  n × n n\times n  matrices, and let
 Υ \Upsilon  be a Borel measure on  𝔐 \mathfrak{M} , possibly with
 Υ ⁡ ( 𝔐 ) = ∞ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{M})=\infty , satisfying
( 1.72 ).
By row-stochasticity, this assumption equivalently reads 
 

 
 (7.2) 
 
 ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ( 1 − M x ​ x ) < ∞ ​ . \textstyle\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)\,\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(1-M_{xx}\right)<\infty\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Recall from Section  1.3.3  that the stochastic exchange model
associated with  Υ \Upsilon  is built from the updates, with infinitesimal rate  Υ ⁡ ( d ​ M ) \Upsilon({\rm d}M) , 
 

 
 (7.3) 
 
 η ⟼ η ​ M ​ , θ ⟼ M ​ θ ​ , \eta\longmapsto\eta M\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\longmapsto M\theta\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the first update acts on energy configurations
 η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega , regarded as row vectors, while the second defines the
corresponding hidden dynamics on column vectors  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} .
Row-stochasticity ensures that  η ​ M ∈ Ω \eta M\in\Omega  whenever
 η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega , as well as 
 

 
 (7.4) 
 
 M ​ [ a , b ] n ⊆ [ a , b ] n ​ , − ∞ < a < b < ∞ ​ , M[a,b]^{n}\subseteq[a,b]^{n}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad-\infty<a<b<\infty\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 since every coordinate of  M ​ θ M\theta  is a convex combination of the
coordinates of  θ \theta . In particular, although the hidden dynamics is
not conservative in general, every cube  [ a , b ] n [a,b]^{n}  is invariant and every
flat configuration is fixed. 
 
 
 In the following proposition, we rigorously construct the stochastic exchange model and its hidden model starting from their infinitesimal descriptions. We show that the sole integrability condition ( 7.2 ) is sufficient both to make the corresponding generators well defined on the corresponding polynomial spaces and, through standard Hille–Yosida theory, to construct the associated Feller processes; see, e.g.,  [ Lig05 ] . In particular, this construction allows  Υ ⁡ ( 𝔐 ) \Upsilon(\mathfrak{M})  to be infinite. If instead  Υ ⁡ ( 𝔐 ) < ∞ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{M})<\infty , as happens, for instance, for the KMP model, the conclusion is immediate, since the operators in ( 7.6 ) and ( 7.7 ) below extend uniquely to bounded Markov generators on the corresponding spaces of continuous functions. 
 
 
 Before stating the proposition, recall from Section  2.1.2  that  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  denotes the space of polynomial functions on  Ω \Omega  of degree at most  k k , while  ℛ k \mathscr{R}_{k}  denotes the corresponding space of degree- k k  homogeneous polynomials for the hidden model. Set, for  − ∞ < a < b < ∞ -\infty<a<b<\infty , 
 

 
 (7.5) 
 
 𝒫 ≔ ⋃ k ≥ 0 𝒫 k ​ , ℛ | [ a , b ] n ≔ ⨁ k ≥ 0 ℛ k | [ a , b ] n ​ , \textstyle\mathscr{P}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathscr{P}_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathscr{R}|_{[a,b]^{n}}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\mathscr{R}_{k}|_{[a,b]^{n}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ℛ k | [ a , b ] n \mathscr{R}_{k}|_{[a,b]^{n}}  denotes the restriction to  [ a , b ] n [a,b]^{n}  of the polynomials in  ℛ k \mathscr{R}_{k} . For a compact set  Ξ \Xi , we write  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ξ ) \mathcal{C}(\Xi)  for the Banach space of real-valued continuous functions on  Ξ \Xi , endowed with the uniform norm. Below, we shall take either  Ξ = Ω \Xi=\Omega  or  Ξ = [ a , b ] n \Xi=[a,b]^{n} . 
 
 
 Proposition 7.1   (Construction and Feller property) . 
 
 Assume ( 7.2 ).
Then, for every  f ∈ 𝒫 f\in\mathscr{P}  and
 g ∈ ℛ | [ a , b ] n g\in\mathscr{R}|_{[a,b]^{n}} ,  − ∞ < a < b < ∞ -\infty<a<b<\infty , the integrals 
 

 
 (7.6) 
 
 ℒ Υ ​ f ​ ( η ) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}f(\eta) 
 ≔ ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η ​ M ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ , η ∈ Ω ​ , \displaystyle\coloneqq\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)\left(f(\eta M)-f(\eta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 (7.7) 
 
 ℒ Υ ​ g ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}g(\theta) 
 ≔ ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ ( g ⁡ ( M ​ θ ) − g ⁡ ( θ ) ) ​ , θ ∈ [ a , b ] n ​ , \displaystyle\coloneqq\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)\left(g(M\theta)-g(\theta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in[a,b]^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 are well defined and finite. Moreover, for every  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , 
 

 
 (7.8) 
 
 ℒ Υ ​ 𝒫 k ⊆ 𝒫 k ​ , ℒ Υ ​ ℛ k | [ a , b ] n ⊆ ℛ k | [ a , b ] n ​ . \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}\mathscr{P}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}\mathscr{R}_{k}|_{[a,b]^{n}}\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{k}|_{[a,b]^{n}}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 As a consequence,
 ( ℒ Υ , 𝒫 ) (\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},\mathscr{P}) 
(resp.  ( ℒ Υ , ℛ | [ a , b ] n ) (\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},\mathscr{R}|_{[a,b]^{n}}) )
is a Markov pregenerator on  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathcal{C}(\Omega) 
(resp.  𝒞 ⁡ ( [ a , b ] n ) \mathcal{C}([a,b]^{n}) ), whose closure generates a unique
Feller process on  Ω \Omega  (resp.  [ a , b ] n [a,b]^{n} ). 
 
 
 
 Since the proof of Proposition  7.1  is rather standard, we postpone it to Appendix  B . 
 
 
 By Proposition  7.1 , the polynomial spaces  𝒫 \mathscr{P}  and
 ℛ | [ a , b ] n \mathscr{R}|_{[a,b]^{n}}  in ( 7.5 ) are cores for the
respective generators. Since we shall primarily study their restrictions to these polynomial cores, with a slight abuse of notation, we use  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  and  ℒ Υ \mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  for both the pregenerators and
their closures. 
 
 
 Remark 7.2   (Invariant measures) . 
 
 Both  Ω \Omega  and  [ a , b ] n [a,b]^{n}  are compact. Hence, by the Feller property
and, e.g.,  [ Lig05 , Proposition I.1.8(f)] , both
processes admit invariant probability measures. Henceforth,
 μ Υ \mu^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  denotes an arbitrary invariant probability measure of
the stochastic exchange model on  Ω \Omega . 
 
 
 Under the sole assumption ( 7.2 ),
 μ Υ \mu^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  need not be unique. For instance, if  Υ \Upsilon  is
supported on permutation matrices, the dynamics only permutes the
coordinates of configurations  η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega , and many invariant probability measures coexist.
Proposition  7.6  below provides several equivalent characterizations
of uniqueness, all involving only the second-order moments of the entries
of  M ∈ 𝔐 M\in\mathfrak{M}  with respect to  Υ \Upsilon . 
 
 
 In contrast, for the hidden process, invariant probability measures are
never unique: row-stochasticity gives  M ⁡ ( c ​ 𝟏 ) = c ​ 𝟏 M(c\mathbf{1})=c\mathbf{1}  for every
 M ∈ 𝔐 M\in\mathfrak{M}  and  c ∈ [ a , b ] c\in[a,b] , thus,  δ c ​ 𝟏 \delta_{c\mathbf{1}}  is invariant. 
 
 
 
 
 7.2.  Particle systems, polynomials, and intertwinings 

 
 This section develops, for general stochastic exchange models, the polynomial characterizations of the eigenvalue problem established for the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model in Section  2.1 . This amounts to introducing the natural labeled-particle dynamics, identifying the corresponding linearly isomorphic polynomial spaces, and establishing the associated intertwining relations.
Most of the objects introduced in
Section  2.1  have natural analogues in the
present setting, and the corresponding statements carry over verbatim. The main difference is that the particle system need not possess a canonical strictly positive reversible measure, which was used there to define the tilde maps ( 2.12 )–( 2.13 ) and characterize the spaces  ℋ k , ∗ \mathscr{H}_{k,\ast}  and  𝒫 k , ∗ \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}  (Proposition  2.5 ). For the algebraic study of the eigenvalue problem, it suffices to replace that measure, at each level  k k , by an arbitrary strictly positive symmetric probability measure on  [ n ] k [n]^{k} , say  ϖ k \varpi_{k} . Since  ϖ k \varpi_{k}  need be neither invariant nor
reversible for the particle dynamics, the corresponding statements
require minor modifications, which we record below and summarize in Proposition  7.3 . In this section, we retain most of the notation (e.g.,  ℋ k , 𝒫 k , ℛ k \mathscr{H}_{k},\mathscr{P}_{k},\mathscr{R}_{k} , and their tensor analogues) introduced in
Section  2.1 . 
 
 
 For  M ∈ 𝔐 M\in\mathfrak{M}  and  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , let  M ⊗ k M^{\otimes k}  be the
Markov operator on  [ n ] k [n]^{k}  under which the  k k  labels move independently according to the rows of  M M , and set 
 

 
 (7.9) 
 
 L k Υ ≔ ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ ( M ⊗ k − I ) ​ , \textstyle L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}\coloneqq\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)\left(M^{\otimes k}-I\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 as an operator acting on the coefficient space  ℋ k ⊗ \mathscr{H}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\otimes}  in ( 2.1 ). By ( 7.2 ), this is a well-defined Markov generator, as, for every

 More generally, for  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ,  L k Υ L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  is the generator of  k k  labeled particles, whose rate to jump from  ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∈ [ n ] k (x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in[n]^{k}  to another state  ( y 1 , … , y k ) ∈ [ n ] k (y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})\in[n]^{k}  reads

 As  M x 1 ​ y 1 ⋯ M x k ​ y k = M x ς ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ y ς ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋯ M x ς ⁡ ( k ) ​ y ς ⁡ ( k ) M_{x_{1}y_{1}}\cdots M_{x_{k}y_{k}}=M_{x_{\varsigma(1)}y_{\varsigma(1)}}\cdots M_{x_{\varsigma(k)}y_{\varsigma(k)}} ,  ς ∈ 𝔖 k \varsigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k} ,  L k Υ L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  leaves  ℋ k \mathscr{H}_{k}  in ( 1.19 ) invariant. 
 
 
 For every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , fix a strictly positive probability measure

choice of  ϖ k \varpi_{k}  is immaterial. One may take, for instance, the

 (7.13)

 induced by the  Υ \Upsilon -stochastic exchange model invariant measure  μ Υ \mu^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  fixed in
Remark  7.2 . It is immediate to check that  μ k Υ \mu_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  is  L k Υ L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon} -invariant. For the purpose of proving Theorem  1.12 , no invariance or consistency
assumption on the family  ( ϖ k ) k ≥ 1 (\varpi_{k})_{k\geq 1}  will be needed. 
 
 
 Let  L k Υ , ϖ L_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},{\scriptscriptstyle\varpi}}  be the adjoint of  L k Υ L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon} 
in  L 2 ​ ( ϖ k ) L^{2}(\varpi_{k}) . If  D ϖ k D_{\varpi_{k}}  denotes the diagonal matrix with

 Thus,  L k Υ , ϖ L_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},{\scriptscriptstyle\varpi}}  is similar to the transpose of

nonnegative, but it is a
Markov generator if and only if  ϖ k \varpi_{k}  is invariant for

 The spaces  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} ,  ℋ k \mathscr{H}_{k} , and  ℛ k \mathscr{R}_{k}  and the hat maps in ( 2.11 ) and ( 2.13 ) are

the zero eigenvalue corresponding to constants, with algebraic

 Proposition 7.3 . 
 
 For every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , counting algebraic multiplicities, 
 

 
 (7.26) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ​ on ​ 𝒫 k / 𝒫 k − 1 ) = spec ⁡ ( L k Υ , ϖ | ℋ k , ∗ ϖ ) = spec ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ ) ​ . {\rm spec}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}\ \text{on}\ \mathscr{P}_{k}/\mathscr{P}_{k-1})={\rm spec}(L_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},{\scriptscriptstyle\varpi}}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k,\ast}^{\varpi}})={\rm spec}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 (7.27) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) = { 0 } ​ ⋃ j = 1 k spec ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ j , ∗ ) {\rm spec}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})=\{0\}\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}{\rm spec}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{j,\ast}}) 
 
 
 as multisets, and 
 

 
 (7.28) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) = min 1 ≤ j ≤ k ⁡ gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ j , ∗ ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})=\min_{1\leq j\leq k}{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{j,\ast}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (7.29) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ ) ≔ min ⁡ { Re ⁡ ( λ ) : λ ∈ spec ⁡ ( − ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ ) } ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}})\coloneqq\min\{{\rm Re}(\lambda):\lambda\in{\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}})\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 In light of Proposition  7.3 ,
Theorem  1.12  admits an equivalent interpretation in terms of
unlabeled particle systems, namely, 
 

 
 (7.30) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) = inf k ≥ 1 gap ⁡ ( L k Υ | ℋ k ) = gap ⁡ ( L 2 Υ | ℋ 2 ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})=\inf_{k\geq 1}{\rm gap}(L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k}})={\rm gap}(L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{2}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where, consistently with Theorem  1.12  and the definition in ( 7.29 ), all the gaps are understood as the smallest real parts of the eigenvalues of the negatives of the corresponding generators (possibly excluding the eigenvalue associated to constant functions), with  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})  defined by restricting  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  to polynomial functions. A labeled-particle strengthening, bearing the same relation to
Theorem  1.12  as Theorem  1.2  does to
Theorem  1.1 , also remains valid: 
 

 
 (7.31) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( L k Υ ) = gap ⁡ ( L k Υ | ℋ k ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}(L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})={\rm gap}(L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, the preceding identifications and the proofs in the next section admit analogous synchronized tensor versions.
To keep the presentation concise and avoid introducing an additional
layer of tensor notation, we leave the details of this refinement to
the reader. 
 
 
 
 7.3.  Proof of Theorem  1.12 

 
 In view of Proposition  7.3  and, in particular, ( 7.28 )–( 7.29 ),  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})  in ( 1.74 ) is determined by its hidden-model counterpart, namely,  inf k ≥ 1 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ ) \inf_{k\geq 1}{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}}) .
The analysis of the latter lies at the core of the proof of the spectral-gap identities in Theorems  1.1 – 1.2 . To prove the identity in ( 1.75 ), the core of the argument from
Section  2.2  carries over essentially unchanged in this more general setting, after allowing for possibly complex eigenvalues and
eigenfunctions. 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  1.12 . 
 
 Fix any
strictly positive probability measure  π \pi  on  [ n ] [n] ; for instance,
one may take  π ≔ ϖ 1 \pi\coloneqq\varpi_{1} , where  ϖ 1 \varpi_{1}  is the reference

( 1.84 ), set, for every  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} ,

 As in Section  2.2 , fix  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , an even integer  2 ≤ ℓ ≤ k 2\leq\ell\leq k , and  − ∞ < a < b < ∞ -\infty<a<b<\infty . Then, by Lemmas  2.8 – 2.9  and ( 2.63 ), one has

 for every  g g  in the complexification of  ℛ k , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{k,\ast} , for every  θ ∈ [ a , b ] n \theta\in[a,b]^{n} , and some  C = C ⁡ ( g , k , π ) > 0 C=C(g,k,\pi)>0 . Further, let  λ ∈ ℂ \lambda\in\mathbb{C}  be
