 where, as in ( 2.65 ),  ‖ ⋅ ‖ a , b : ℛ ℓ , ∗ → [ 0 , ∞ ) \left\lVert{\,\cdot\,}\right\rVert_{a,b}:\mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}\to[0,\infty)  denotes the uniform norm over  θ ∈ [ a , b ] n \theta\in[a,b]^{n} .
Hence, 
 

 
 (7.35) 
 
 − Re ⁡ ( λ ) ≤ lim t → ∞ 1 t ​ log ⁡ ‖ e t ​ ℒ Υ | ℛ ℓ , ∗ ‖ a , b = max ⁡ { Re ⁡ ( σ ) : σ ∈ spec ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ ℓ , ∗ ) } = − gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ ℓ , ∗ ) ​ , -{\rm Re}(\lambda)\leq\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\|e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}}}\|_{a,b}=\max\{{\rm Re}(\sigma):\sigma\in{\rm spec}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}})\}=-{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the first identity comes from the fact that  ℛ ℓ , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}  is finite dimensional and Gelfand’s formula, whereas the second identity follows from the definition in ( 7.29 ).
Taking the minimum over the eigenvalues  λ ∈ ℂ \lambda\in\mathbb{C}  of  − ℒ Υ | ℛ ℓ , ∗ -\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}}  yields, for  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , 
 

 
 (7.36) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ ℓ , ∗ ) ≤ gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ ) ​ , for every ​  2 ≤ ℓ ≤ k ​ with  ℓ ≥ 2  even ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{\ell,\ast}})\leq{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for every}\ 2\leq\ell\leq k\ \text{with $\ell\geq 2$ even}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, choosing  ℓ = 2 \ell=2  and using
( 7.28 ) gives 
 

 
 (7.37) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) = min ⁡ { gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 1 , ∗ ) , gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) } = gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 2 ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})=\min\{{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{1,\ast}}),{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})\}={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This proves the identity in ( 1.75 ). 
 
 
 The existence of an invariant measure for  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  was established in Section  7.1 , Remark  7.2 ; the claim on its uniqueness is the equivalence of the assertions  1  and  2  of Proposition  7.6 . ∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 7.4 . 
 
 The ordering in ( 7.36 ), together with
the isospectrality in ( 7.26 ), yields the
 ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon} -analogue of the  KMP {\rm KMP} 
inequalities ( 2.69 ) from
Remark  2.10 . 
 
 
 
 Remark 7.5 . 
 
 As observed in Remark  3.10 , if
 k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  is even, the cone  𝒞 k , ∗ \mathscr{C}_{k,\ast}  of nonnegative
functions in  ℛ k , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}  is proper, has nonempty interior,
and is preserved by the hidden-model semigroup. Hence, the
Perron–Frobenius theorem shows that
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ ) {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}}) 
is a real, nonnegative eigenvalue of
 − ℒ Υ | ℛ k , ∗ -\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{k,\ast}} , admitting a nonzero
nonnegative eigenfunction. By the aforementioned isospectralities, it is
also an eigenvalue of  − ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k -\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}}  and
 − L k Υ | ℋ k -L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{k}} . In particular, this applies to
 k = 2 k=2 .
Consequently, if 
 

 
 (7.38) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) ≤ gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 1 , ∗ ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})\leq{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{1,\ast}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 then the identity in ( 7.37 ) shows that  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})  is a
real eigenvalue. Without ( 7.38 ), however,  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})  need not itself be an eigenvalue, as the following elementary example shows. 
 
 
 Let  n = 3 n=3  and  Υ = δ M ⋆ \Upsilon=\delta_{M^{\star}} , where 
 

 
 (7.39) 
 
 M ⋆ ≔ 1 6 ​ ( 3 2 1 1 3 2 2 1 3 ) M^{\star}\coloneqq\frac{1}{6}\begin{pmatrix}3&2&1\\
1&3&2\\
2&1&3\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 is row-stochastic.
The eigenvalues of  M ⋆ M^{\star}  are  1 , z , z ¯ ∈ ℂ 1,z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{C} , where 
 

 
 (7.40) 
 
 z = 1 4 + 3 12 ​ i ​ , | z | 2 = 1 12 ​ . z=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{12}\,{\rm i}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad|z|^{2}=\frac{1}{12}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, 
 

 
 (7.41) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( − ℒ Υ | ℛ 1 , ∗ ) = { 1 − z , 1 − z ¯ } ​ , gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 1 , ∗ ) = 3 4 ​ , {\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{1,\ast}})=\{1-z,1-\bar{z}\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad{\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{1,\ast}})=\frac{3}{4}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 although neither eigenvalue equals  3 / 4 3/4 . Moreover, 
 

 
 (7.42) 
 
 spec ⁡ ( − ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) = { 1 − z 2 , 1 − | z | 2 , 1 − z ¯ 2 } ​ , {\rm spec}(-\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})=\{1-z^{2},1-|z|^{2},1-\bar{z}^{2}\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and, therefore, 
 

 
 (7.43) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) = 11 12 > 3 4 = gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})=\frac{11}{12}>\frac{3}{4}={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, in this example, ( 7.38 ) fails and  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})  is the real
part of a nonreal eigenvalue. 
 
 
 
 It remains to prove the uniqueness statement in Theorem  1.12 . 
 
 
 Proposition 7.6   (Uniqueness of the invariant measure) . 
 
 Assume that the Borel measure  Υ \Upsilon  on  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  satisfies
( 7.2 ). Then, the following statements are
equivalent: 
 
 (1)  
 
 The  Υ \Upsilon -stochastic exchange model admits a unique invariant
probability measure. 
 
 (2)  
 
 The polynomial gap in ( 1.74 ) satisfies 
 

 
 (7.44) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) > 0 ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})>0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 (3)  
 
 The two-particle generator  L 2 Υ L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  admits a unique invariant
probability measure. 
 
 (4)  
 
 The hidden-model gap in ( 7.29 ) satisfies 
 

 
 (7.45) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) > 0 ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})>0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 (5)  
 
 For every strictly positive probability measure  π \pi  on  [ n ] [n]  and some  t > 0 t>0 , 
 

 
 (7.46) 
 
 e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) < Var π ​ ( θ ) ​ , for every nonconstant  ​ θ ∈ ℝ n ​ , e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)<{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for every nonconstant }\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}  was defined in ( 7.32 ). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 7.7   (A variant of condition  5 ) . 
 
 If  L 1 Υ L_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  admits a strictly positive invariant probability
measure  π \pi , then, for this choice of  π \pi , “some  t > 0 t>0 ” in
( 7.46 ) may be replaced by
“every  t > 0 t>0 ”. Indeed,  L 1 L_{1} -invariance of  π \pi  and Jensen’s inequality
give 
 

 
 (7.47) 
 
 e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π ≤ Var π ​ , t ≥ 0 ​ . e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}\leq{\rm Var}_{\pi}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad t\geq 0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, for every  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , the map
 t ↦ e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π ​ ( θ ) t\mapsto e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta) 
is nonincreasing and analytic. Equality at any positive time would
force equality at all times, contradicting ( 7.46 ). 
 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  7.6 . 
 
 We first prove the equivalence of
 2  and  3 . By
( 7.30 ) and ( 7.31 ), 
 

 
 (7.48) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) = gap ⁡ ( L 2 Υ | ℋ 2 ) = gap ⁡ ( L 2 Υ ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})={\rm gap}(L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{2}})={\rm gap}(L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Recall that, for a finite-state Markov generator, zero is always an eigenvalue of the corresponding generator, and its
multiplicity equals the number of closed communicating classes, while
all the remaining eigenvalues of the generator have strictly
negative real part. Hence,  L 2 Υ L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  admits a unique invariant
probability measure if and only if  gap ⁡ ( L 2 Υ ) > 0 {\rm gap}(L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})>0 . Together
with ( 7.48 ), this proves
 2   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   3 . 
 
 
 To compare  3  and  4 , let  1 ≤ n 1 ≤ n 1\leq n_{1}\leq n  and
 1 ≤ n 2 ≤ n ⁡ ( n + 1 ) / 2 1\leq n_{2}\leq n(n+1)/2  denote the numbers of closed communicating classes of,
respectively, the one-particle chain  L 1 Υ = L 1 Υ | ℋ 1 L_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}=L_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{1}}  and the unlabeled two-particle chain  L 2 Υ | ℋ 2 L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{2}} , respectively. Proposition  7.3 
and the spectral filtration in ( 7.27 ) give 
 

 
 (7.49) 
 
 dim ker ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) = n 2 − n 1 ​ , \dim{\rm ker}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})=n_{2}-n_{1}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 yielding, together with  n 2 ≥ n 1 ​ ( n 1 + 1 ) / 2 n_{2}\geq n_{1}(n_{1}+1)/2 , 
 

 
 (7.50) 
 
 ker ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) = { 0 } ⟺ n 2 = 1 . {\rm ker}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})=\{0\}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad n_{2}=1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 On the one hand, since all nonzero eigenvalues under consideration have strictly
negative real part, the left-hand side of ( 7.50 ) is equivalent to
 4 . On the other hand,  n 2 = 1 n_{2}=1  is equivalent to
positivity of  gap ⁡ ( L 2 Υ | ℋ 2 ) {\rm gap}(L_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{H}_{2}})  in ( 7.48 ), thus,
to  3 . This proves
 3   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   4 . 
 
 
 We next prove
 4   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   5 . Recall from ( 3.2 ) the definition of the proper
cone  𝒞 ⊂ ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{C}\subset\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast} . For any strictly positive probability measure  π \pi ,
 Var π ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) {\rm Var}_{\pi}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}) , while
 e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ 𝒞 ⊆ 𝒞 e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C} ,
 t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Assume  4 . Then, the hidden-model semigroup on
 ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}  converges exponentially to zero; in particular,
 e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π → 0 e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}\to 0  in  ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}  as  t → ∞ t\to\infty .
Since  Var π ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) {\rm Var}_{\pi}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C}) , for all sufficiently large
 t t , 
 

 
 (7.51) 
 
 Var π − e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π ∈ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) ​ , {\rm Var}_{\pi}-e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}\in{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which is precisely ( 7.46 ). Conversely, suppose that
( 7.46 ) (or, equivalently, ( 7.51 )) holds for some strictly
positive  π \pi  and some  t > 0 t>0 . Since
 Var π − e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π {\rm Var}_{\pi}-e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}  is strictly positive
on the compact set
 { θ ∈ ℝ n : π ( θ ) = 0 , Var π ( θ ) = 1 } \{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\pi(\theta)=0,{\rm Var}_{\pi}(\theta)=1\} ,
homogeneity and translation invariance yield some  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 
such that 
 

 
 (7.52) 
 
 e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π ≤ ( 1 − ε ) ​ Var π ​ , e^{t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}\leq(1-\varepsilon){\rm Var}_{\pi}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which, by positivity and iteration, further gives 
 

 
 (7.53) 
 
 e ℓ ​ t ​ ℒ Υ ​ Var π ≤ ( 1 − ε ) ℓ ​ Var π ​ , ℓ ≥ 1 ​ . e^{\ell t\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}{\rm Var}_{\pi}\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{\ell}{\rm Var}_{\pi}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\ell\geq 1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By Gelfand’s formula, ( 7.53 ) gives  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | ℛ 2 , ∗ ) ≥ − 1 t ​ log ⁡ ( 1 − ε ) > 0 {\rm gap}(\mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast}})\geq-\frac{1}{t}\log(1-\varepsilon)>0 , thus, proving  4 . 
 
 
 It remains to prove
 1   ⟺ \Longleftrightarrow   2 . Suppose that
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) = 0 {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})=0 . By ( 7.37 ),
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 2 ) = 0 {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})=0 .
Moreover, by ( 7.16 )–( 7.19 ),
there
exists a nonconstant real  f ∈ 𝒫 2 f\in\mathscr{P}_{2}  such that
 ℒ Υ ​ f = 0 \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}f=0 . Hence  e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ f = f e^{t\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}f=f  for every  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Define 
 

 
 (7.54) 
 
 Ω + ≔ { η ∈ Ω : f ⁡ ( η ) = max Ω ⁡ f } ​ , Ω − ≔ { η ∈ Ω : f ⁡ ( η ) = min Ω ⁡ f } ​ . \Omega_{+}\coloneqq\{\eta\in\Omega:f(\eta)=\max_{\Omega}f\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\Omega_{-}\coloneqq\{\eta\in\Omega:f(\eta)=\min_{\Omega}f\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  Ω \Omega  is compact and  f f  is continuous and nonconstant,
 Ω + \Omega_{+}  and  Ω − \Omega_{-}  are nonempty, compact, and disjoint.
Moreover, if  η ∈ Ω + \eta\in\Omega_{+} , then 
 

 
 (7.55) 
 
 max Ω ⁡ f = e t ​ ℒ Υ ​ f ​ ( η ) = 𝔼 η ​ [ f ⁡ ( η t ) ] ≤ max Ω ⁡ f ​ , \max_{\Omega}f=e^{t\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}}f(\eta)=\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[f(\eta_{t})]\leq\max_{\Omega}f\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 so  η t ∈ Ω + \eta_{t}\in\Omega_{+}  almost surely; the same estimate for  − f -f  shows that also  Ω − \Omega_{-}  is
invariant. Hence, each set supports an invariant probability measure. Since
 Ω + \Omega_{+}  and  Ω − \Omega_{-}  are disjoint, these measures are distinct.
This proves
 1   ⟹ \Longrightarrow   2 . 
 
 
 Conversely, let
 μ Υ , 1 ≠ μ Υ , 2 \mu^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},1}\neq\mu^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},2}  be invariant probability
measures. By density of  𝒫 \mathscr{P}  in  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathcal{C}(\Omega) , their
restrictions differ on some  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} . Set 
 

 
 (7.56) 
 
 ℓ i ≔ μ Υ , i | 𝒫 k ∈ 𝒫 k ′ ​ , i ∈ { 1 , 2 } ​ , \ell_{i}\coloneqq\mu^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},i}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}}\in\mathscr{P}_{k}^{\prime}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad i\in\{1,2\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  𝒫 k ′ \mathscr{P}_{k}^{\prime}  is the algebraic dual of  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} . Then
 ℓ 1 ≠ ℓ 2 \ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2} , while
 ℓ 1 ​ ( 𝟏 ) = ℓ 2 ​ ( 𝟏 ) = 1 \ell_{1}(\mathbf{1})=\ell_{2}(\mathbf{1})=1 . Hence, they cannot be
nontrivial scalar multiples of one another and are therefore linearly
independent. Further, for the algebraic adjoint
 ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) ′ : 𝒫 k ′ → 𝒫 k ′ (\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})^{\prime}:\mathscr{P}_{k}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}_{k}^{\prime} ,
invariance gives 
 

 
 (7.57) 
 
 ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) ′ ​ ℓ 1 = ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) ′ ​ ℓ 2 = 0 ​ . (\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})^{\prime}\ell_{1}=(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})^{\prime}\ell_{2}=0\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, zero has geometric, and hence algebraic, multiplicity at least
two for the adjoint. Since a finite-dimensional operator and its
adjoint have the same characteristic polynomial, zero has algebraic
multiplicity at least two also for
 ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}} . Therefore,
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 k ) = 0 {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}_{k}})=0 ,
and consequently  gap ⁡ ( ℒ Υ ) = 0 {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})=0 .
This proves  2   ⟹ \Longrightarrow   1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 7.4.  Examples 

 
 As already mentioned, the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model with site and block weights  α \alpha  and  w w 
corresponds to the stochastic exchange model with the update measure  Υ = Υ KMP \Upsilon=\Upsilon^{\rm\scriptscriptstyle KMP}  in ( 1.70 ). We next discuss
three further classes of stochastic exchange models: averaging-like processes,
immediate exchange models, and the harmonic process. Some of these models with all update blocks of size two were recently studied
in  [ KQS25 ,  CKMP25 ] . Throughout this section,
we fix  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 . 
 
 
 7.4.1.  Averaging processes and generalized exchanges 

 
 Fix a probability vector  π = ( π x ) x ∈ [ n ] \pi=(\pi_{x})_{x\in[n]}  with  π x > 0 \pi_{x}>0  for every
 x x , and nonnegative block weights  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} . Recall from
( 2.2 ) that  K B π K_{B}^{\pi}  is the row-stochastic matrix which
redistributes the total mass in  B B  deterministically according to the
conditional probability vector  ( π x / π ⁡ ( B ) ) x ∈ B (\pi_{x}/\pi(B))_{x\in B}  and leaves all
coordinates outside  B B  unchanged. The corresponding block averaging process
is obtained from 
 

 
 (7.58) 
 
 Υ = Υ AVG ≔ ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ δ K B π ​ . \textstyle\Upsilon=\Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle AVG}}\coloneqq\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\,\delta_{K_{B}^{\pi}}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This is precisely the update measure underlying the limiting hidden generator
 ℒ ∞ \mathscr{L}_{\infty}  in ( 5.17 ); its support is the
set  supp ⁡ ( Υ ∞ ) {\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{\infty})  introduced in ( 3.31 ).
For pairwise blocks, the update on the coordinates  { x , y } \{x,y\}  reads 
 

 
 (7.59) 
 
 ( η x , η y ) ​ ( π x π x + π y π y π x + π y π x π x + π y π y π x + π y ) = ( π x π x + π y ​ ( η x + η y ) , π y π x + π y ​ ( η x + η y ) ) ​ . (\eta_{x},\eta_{y})\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\pi_{x}}{\pi_{x}+\pi_{y}}&\frac{\pi_{y}}{\pi_{x}+\pi_{y}}\\[5.69046pt]
\frac{\pi_{x}}{\pi_{x}+\pi_{y}}&\frac{\pi_{y}}{\pi_{x}+\pi_{y}}\end{pmatrix}=(\tfrac{\pi_{x}}{\pi_{x}+\pi_{y}}(\eta_{x}+\eta_{y}),\tfrac{\pi_{y}}{\pi_{x}+\pi_{y}}(\eta_{x}+\eta_{y}))\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This recovers the weighted averaging process considered in, e.g.,
 [ AL12 ,  QS23 ] ; the corresponding
mean-field block dynamics was studied in  [ CQS24 ] .
The probability measure  δ π \delta_{\pi}  is invariant and, under the usual
connectivity assumption ( 1.36 ) on the hypergraph induced by  w w , it is the unique
invariant probability measure. 
 
 
 In fact, averaging processes satisfy the stronger one-particle spectral gap
identity. For pairwise blocks, this was proved in
 [ QS23 , Theorem 2.1] ; in the present block setting, it
also follows by combining ( 5.26 ) with
Theorem  1.12 . Indeed, writing
 ℒ AVG ≔ ℒ Υ AVG \mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle AVG}}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}^{\Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle AVG}}} , one has 
 

 
 (7.60) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ AVG ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ AVG | 𝒫 1 ) ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle AVG}})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle AVG}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{1}})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 The preceding example belongs to a broader class. Suppose that  Υ \Upsilon  is
a finite measure on  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  and that there exists a strictly positive
probability vector  π ∈ Ω \pi\in\Omega  such that 
 

 
 (7.61) 
 
 π ​ M = π ​ , for  Υ -a.e.  M ​ . \pi M=\pi\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for $\Upsilon$-a.e.\ $M$}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus,  π \pi  is invariant for  Υ \Upsilon -almost every  M M . Consequently,
 δ π \delta_{\pi}  is invariant for the stochastic exchange model, while
 π ⊗ k \pi^{\otimes k}  is invariant for  L k Υ L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  for every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , since
 π ⊗ k ​ M ⊗ k = π ⊗ k \pi^{\otimes k}M^{\otimes k}=\pi^{\otimes k}  for  Υ \Upsilon -almost every
 M M .
If, more strongly, 
 

 
 (7.62) 
 
 D π ​ M = M 𝖳 ​ D π ​ , for  Υ -a.e.  M ​ , D_{\pi}M=M^{\mathsf{T}}D_{\pi}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for $\Upsilon$-a.e.\ $M$}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  D π D_{\pi}  is the diagonal matrix with diagonal  π \pi , then  π \pi  is
reversible for  Υ \Upsilon -almost every  M M . Indeed, this condition is
equivalent to the detailed-balance equation  π x ​ M x ​ y = π y ​ M y ​ x \pi_{x}M_{xy}=\pi_{y}M_{yx} ,  x , y ∈ [ n ] x,y\in[n] , and makes
 M ⊗ k M^{\otimes k}  and  L k Υ L_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  self-adjoint on  L 2 ​ ( π ⊗ k ) L^{2}(\pi^{\otimes k}) . In particular, ( 7.62 ) guarantees that  spec ⁡ ( ℒ Υ | 𝒫 ) {\rm spec}(\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}|_{\mathscr{P}})  is real. 
 
 
 While ( 7.61 ) and ( 7.62 ) are equivalent for size-two block updates,
for updates involving three or more sites,
( 7.61 ) is strictly weaker than
( 7.62 ). For example, the cyclic permutation
matrix 
 

 
 (7.63) 
 
 ( 0 1 0 0 0 1 1 0 0 ) \begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 preserves the uniform probability vector  π ≡ 1 / n \pi\equiv 1/n , but is not reversible with respect
to it. 
 
 
 Remark 7.8   (Permutations and the interchange process) . 
 
 Permutation updates provide an interesting degenerate example of stochastic exchange models. Suppose that
 Υ \Upsilon  is supported on  n × n n\times n  permutation matrices, elements
of  𝔐 \mathfrak{M} . Starting from a configuration  η \eta  with pairwise distinct
coordinates, and regarding the values  ( η x ) x ∈ [ n ] (\eta_{x})_{x\in[n]}  as distinct labels,
the stochastic exchange model projects onto a block-interchange process;
see  [ CLR10 ,  BC24 ,  AP26 ] . 
 
 
 On the full state space  Ω \Omega , however, permutation updates preserve the
unordered collection of coordinates of  η \eta . Consequently, the dynamics
decomposes into infinitely many invariant subsets and admits infinitely many
invariant probability measures. In particular, by
Proposition  7.6 , its spectral gap vanishes. Thus, although
Theorem  1.12  applies to permutation updates, the ambient state space
 Ω \Omega  is too large to capture the nontrivial spectral-gap identities of the
corresponding interchange-process projections, such as
 [ CLR10 , Theorem 1.1]  in the graph case and its proposed extensions to hypergraphs
 [ BC24 , Conjecture 1.7] . 
 
 
 
 
 7.4.2.  Immediate exchange model 

 
 We next give a block version of the  immediate exchange model 
 [ HP14 ,  Kat15 ,  RS17 ] . As for the  KMP {\rm KMP}  model, fix positive parameters
 α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]}  and nonnegative block weights  w = ( w B ) B ⊆ [ n ] w=(w_{B})_{B\subseteq[n]} . For every block  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 , let
 A B = ( a x ​ y B ) x , y ∈ B A^{B}=(a^{B}_{xy})_{x,y\in B} 
be a symmetric matrix with strictly positive entries satisfying 
 

 
 (7.64) 
 
 ∑ y ∈ B a x ​ y B = α x ​ , x ∈ B ​ . \textstyle\sum_{y\in B}a^{B}_{xy}=\alpha_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x\in B\,\text{.} 
 
 
 For every  x ∈ B x\in B , independently sample 
 

 
 (7.65) 
 
 U ( x ) = ( U y ( x ) ) y ∈ B ∼ Dir ⁡ ( ( a x ​ y B ) y ∈ B ) U^{(x)}=(U^{(x)}_{y})_{y\in B}\sim{\rm Dir}\big((a^{B}_{xy})_{y\in B}\big) 
 
 
 and let  J B J_{B}  be the stochastic matrix whose  x x -th row equals
 U ( x ) U^{(x)}  on  B B  for  x ∈ B x\in B , while  J B J_{B}  agrees with the identity
outside  B B . The immediate exchange model corresponds to the choice 
 

 
 (7.66) 
 
 Υ IEM = ∑ B ⊆ [ n ] w B ​ Law ​ ( J B ) ​ . \textstyle\Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}}=\sum_{B\subseteq[n]}w_{B}\,{\rm Law}(J_{B})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 For  B = { x , y } B=\{x,y\} ,  x ≠ y x\neq y , after writing 
 

 
 (7.67) 
 
 A B = ( α x ​ x α x ​ y α x ​ y α y ​ y ) ​ , α x ​ x + α x ​ y = α x ​ , ​ α y ​ y + α y ​ x = α y ​ , ​ α x ​ y = α y ​ x ​ , A^{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{xx}&\alpha_{xy}\\
\alpha_{xy}&\alpha_{yy}\end{pmatrix}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\alpha_{xx}+\alpha_{xy}=\alpha_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;\alpha_{yy}+\alpha_{yx}=\alpha_{y}\;\text{,}\;\;\alpha_{xy}=\alpha_{yx}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and sampling independent random variables
 U ∼ Beta ⁡ ( α x ​ x , α x ​ y ) U\sim{\rm Beta}(\alpha_{xx},\alpha_{xy}) ,
 V ∼ Beta ⁡ ( α y ​ y , α y ​ x ) V\sim{\rm Beta}(\alpha_{yy},\alpha_{yx}) , the update restricted to the coordinates  { x , y } \{x,y\}  takes the matrix form 
 

 
 (7.68) 
 
 ( η x , η y ) ⟼ ( η x , η y ) ​ ( U 1 − U 1 − V V ) = ( U ​ η x + ( 1 − V ) ​ η y , ( 1 − U ) ​ η x + V ​ η y ) ​ . (\eta_{x},\eta_{y})\longmapsto(\eta_{x},\eta_{y})\begin{pmatrix}U&1-U\\
1-V&V\end{pmatrix}=\big(U\eta_{x}+(1-V)\eta_{y},(1-U)\eta_{x}+V\eta_{y}\big)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, in contrast with the  KMP {\rm KMP}  update in ( 1.77 ), the rows in
the updated block are independent rather than identical. 
 
 
 We claim that  μ = Dir ⁡ ( α ) \mu={\rm Dir}(\alpha)  is reversible for every single-block
update and, consequently, for
 ℒ IEM ≔ ℒ Υ IEM \mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}^{\Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}}} . This follows once again from the standard
Gamma representation of the Dirichlet distribution, and symmetry of  A B A^{B} . We leave the details to the reader.
The same symmetry also makes the one-particle dynamics reversible. Indeed,
for distinct  x , y ∈ [ n ] x,y\in[n] , set 
 

 
 (7.69) 
 
 t x ​ y ≔ ∑ B ⊇ { x , y } w B ​ a x ​ y B = t y ​ x ​ , \textstyle t_{xy}\coloneqq\sum_{B\supseteq\{x,y\}}w_{B}\,a^{B}_{xy}=t_{yx}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and  t x ​ x ≔ 0 t_{xx}\coloneqq 0 .
Then, the one-particle rate from  x x  to  y y  is  t x ​ y / α x t_{xy}/\alpha_{x} , and its
reversible probability measure is  π = α / α 0 \pi=\alpha/\alpha_{0} , with
 α 0 ≔ α ⁡ ( [ n ] ) \alpha_{0}\coloneqq\alpha([n]) . 
 
 
 The following corollary combines Theorem  1.12  with reversibility
with respect to  μ = Dir ⁡ ( α ) \mu={\rm Dir}(\alpha)  and density of polynomials in
 L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu) , whereas the comparison estimate ( 7.71 ) below extends

hypergraphs; we omit the details. Here, the polynomial spaces
 𝒫 k , ∗ \mathscr{P}_{k,\ast}  are those defined in ( 1.27 ). 
 
 
 Corollary 7.9   (Immediate exchange model) . 
 
 For the block immediate exchange model,
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}})  coincides with the ordinary

 (7.70) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM | 𝒫 2 ) = min ⁡ { gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM | 𝒫 1 , ∗ ) , gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM | 𝒫 2 , ∗ ) } ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})=\min\{{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{1,\ast}}),{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2,\ast}})\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Further, 
 

 
 (7.71)

 (7.72)

 then  γ IEM ≥ 1 \gamma_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}\geq 1  and, thus,  gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ IEM | 𝒫 1 , ∗ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{\rm\scriptscriptstyle IEM}|_{\mathscr{P}_{1,\ast}}) .

 7.4.3.  Harmonic process 

 
 The  harmonic process 
 [ FG22 ,  FFG23 ]  provides an
important example for which the update measure is not finite. Fix symmetric
conductances  b x ​ y = b y ​ x ≥ 0 b_{xy}=b_{yx}\geq 0 , with  b x ​ x = 0 b_{xx}=0 , and positive
parameters  α = ( α x ) x ∈ [ n ] \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[n]} . For  x ≠ y x\neq y  and  u ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) u\in(0,1) ,
let  H x ​ y , u H^{xy,u}  be the stochastic matrix equal to the identity except
for its  x x -th row, which is given by 
 

 
 (7.75) 
 
 e x ​ H x ​ y , u = u ​ e x + ( 1 − u ) ​ e y ​ . e_{x}H^{xy,u}=ue_{x}+(1-u)e_{y}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, restricted to the coordinates  { x , y } \{x,y\} , the update reads 
 

 
 (7.76) 
 
 ( η x , η y ) ​ ( u 1 − u 0 1 ) = ( u ​ η x , ( 1 − u ) ​ η x + η y ) ​ . (\eta_{x},\eta_{y})\begin{pmatrix}u&1-u\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=\big(u\eta_{x},(1-u)\eta_{x}+\eta_{y}\big)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The harmonic process is built from the update measure 
 

 
 (7.77) 
 
 Υ HP ​ ( 𝑑 M ) = ∑ x , y ∈ [ n ] b x ​ y ​ ∫ 0 1 u α x − 1 1 − u ​ δ H x ​ y , u ​ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ 𝑑 u ​ . \Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}({\rm d}M)=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}b_{xy}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{u^{\alpha_{x}-1}}{1-u}\,\delta_{H^{xy,u}}({\rm d}M)\,{\rm d}u\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The measure  Υ HP \Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}  has infinite total mass. Nevertheless,
it satisfies ( 7.2 ), since the only nonzero
off-diagonal entry of  H x ​ y , u H^{xy,u}  is  ( H x ​ y , u ) x ​ y = 1 − u (H^{xy,u})_{xy}=1-u  and 
 

 
 (7.78) 
 
 ∫ 0 1 u α x − 1 1 − u ​ ( 1 − u ) ​ 𝑑 u = 1 α x < ∞ ​ . \int_{0}^{1}\frac{u^{\alpha_{x}-1}}{1-u}\left(1-u\right)\,{\rm d}u=\frac{1}{\alpha_{x}}<\infty\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The harmonic process is reversible with respect to  μ = Dir ⁡ ( α ) \mu={\rm Dir}(\alpha) ;
see  [ FFG23 ] . 
 
 
 As similarly done for the immediate exchange model in Corollary  7.9 , we collect the main findings on the harmonic process’ spectral gap in the following corollary.
The bound in ( 7.80 ) follows from
 [ KQS25 , Theorem 1.2] .
Write
 ℒ HP ≔ ℒ Υ HP \mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}^{\Upsilon^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}} . 
 
 
 Corollary 7.11   (Harmonic process) . 
 
 For the harmonic process,
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}})  coincides with the ordinary
 L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu)  spectral gap of  ℒ HP \mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}  and satisfies 
 

 
 (7.79) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP | 𝒫 2 ) = min ⁡ { gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP | 𝒫 1 , ∗ ) , gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP | 𝒫 2 , ∗ ) } ​ . {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2}})=\min\{{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{1,\ast}}),{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2,\ast}})\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Further, 
 

 
 (7.80) 
 
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP | 𝒫 2 , ∗ ) ≥ γ HP ​ gap ​ ( ℒ HP | 𝒫 1 , ∗ ) ​ , {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{2,\ast}})\geq\gamma_{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}\,{\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{1,\ast}})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (7.81) 
 
 γ HP ≔ ( 1 + 1 α 0 ) min x , y : b x ​ y > 0 { α x α x + 1 + α y α y + 1 } . \gamma_{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}\coloneqq\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\right)\min_{x,y\,:\,b_{xy}>0}\left\{\frac{\alpha_{x}}{\alpha_{x}+1}+\frac{\alpha_{y}}{\alpha_{y}+1}\right\}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, if 
 

 
 (7.82) 
 
 min x , y : b x ​ y > 0 α x α y ≥ 1 , \min_{x,y\,:\,b_{xy}>0}\alpha_{x}\alpha_{y}\geq 1\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 then  γ HP ≥ 1 \gamma_{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}\geq 1  and, consequently,
 gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP ) = gap ⁡ ( ℒ HP | 𝒫 1 , ∗ ) {\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}})={\rm gap}(\mathcal{L}^{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle HP}}|_{\mathscr{P}_{1,\ast}}) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix A  Refining Lemma  3.8 

 
 This appendix proves the refinement of Lemma  3.8  announced in Remark  3.9 , and crucial in the proof of item  2  of Theorems  1.6  and  1.8 ; see Proposition  5.10 . The main result of this section is Proposition  A.1 . Its proof requires some technical lemmas, inspired by the works  [ CHM81 ,  BXHR12 ] , and whose complete proofs are postponed to the end of this appendix. The weights  α \alpha  and  w w  are fixed throughout, while  π = α / α 0 \pi=\alpha/\alpha_{0}  is as in ( 1.84 ). 
 
 
 Before stating the result, recall
that  supp ⁡ ( Υ ∞ ) {\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{\infty})  in ( 3.31 ) contains only update matrices of the form  K B π K_{B}^{\pi} , one for each  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 . For such a block, 
 

 
 (A.1) 
 
 K B π = ∑ x ∈ B π x π ⁡ ( B ) ​ K B e x ​ , with ​ π ​ ( B ) = ∑ y ∈ B π y ​ . K_{B}^{\pi}=\sum_{x\in B}\frac{\pi_{x}}{\pi(B)}K_{B}^{e_{x}}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{with}\ \pi(B)=\sum_{y\in B}\pi_{y}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, every product of matrices from  supp ⁡ ( Υ ∞ ) {\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{\infty})  is a linear combination of products of matrices in  supp ⁡ ( Υ 0 ) {\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{0})  from ( 3.29 ). Hence, with the notation in ( 3.30 ) and ( 3.32 ), 
 

 
 (A.2) 
 
 𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) ⊆ 𝒮 Υ 0 ​ ( θ ) ​ , θ ∈ ℝ n ​ . \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{0}}(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 As observed in Remark  3.9 , this smaller range already indicates why recovering a full-range property for  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)  is a delicate task. 
 
 
 Moreover, the updates preserve the  π \pi -mean and fix the constant configurations, that is, 
 

 
 (A.3) 
 
 π ⁡ ( K B π ​ θ ) = π ⁡ ( θ ) ​ , K B π ​ 𝟏 = 𝟏 ​ . \pi(K_{B}^{\pi}\theta)=\pi(\theta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad K_{B}^{\pi}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, 
 

 
 (A.4) 
 
 ℋ 1 , ∗ = { v ∈ ℝ n : π ⁡ ( v ) = 0 } ​ , \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}=\big\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\pi(v)=0\big\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 is invariant under every  K B π K_{B}^{\pi} . Thus, for every  0 ≠ θ ∈ ℋ 1 , ∗ 0\neq\theta\in\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} ,
 𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) ⊆ ℋ 1 , ∗ ≠ ℝ n \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}\neq\mathbb{R}^{n} .
Therefore,  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) = ℝ n \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)=\mathbb{R}^{n}  (cf. ( 3.20 )) cannot hold uniformly over all nonconstant  θ \theta . The appropriate full-range statement is instead the analogue of ( 3.19 ), that is, ( A.5 ) below. 
 
 
 Proposition A.1 . 
 
 Assume that the hypergraph induced by  w w  is minimal; see Section  3.2  and Proposition  3.3 . Then, for every nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , 
 

 
 (A.5) 
 
 𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = ℝ n ​ . \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Moreover, the hidden model introduced in Section  5.2 , whose generator  ℒ ∞ \mathscr{L}_{\infty}  is given in ( 5.17 ), is  𝒞 \mathscr{C} -strongly positive on  ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast} : for every  t > 0 t>0 , 
 

 
 (A.6) 
 
 e t ​ ℒ ∞ ​ ( 𝒞 ∖ { 0 } ) ⊆ int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) ​ . e^{t\mathscr{L}_{\infty}}\left(\mathscr{C}\setminus\{0\}\right)\subseteq{\rm int}(\mathscr{C})\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Before presenting the proof of Proposition  A.1 , we introduce some combinatorial tools related to the theory of partitive families and modular decomposition; see  [ CHM81 ]  and  [ BXHR12 ]  for a modern account. In fact, Lemma  A.3  below, which constitutes a key ingredient in the proof of Proposition  A.1 , could alternatively be obtained as a direct consequence of the decomposition theorem for partitive families; see, e.g.,  [ BXHR12 , Theorem 2] . Nevertheless, we include an elementary direct proof in order to keep the argument self-contained. 
 
 
 For a symmetric matrix  Q = ( Q x ​ y ) x , y ∈ [ n ] Q=(Q_{xy})_{x,y\in[n]} , a set  A ⊆ [ n ] A\subseteq[n]  is called a  Q Q -module if every index outside  A A  sees all indices in  A A  in the same way, namely, 
 

 
 (A.7) 
 
 z ∉ A , x , y ∈ A ⟹ Q z ​ x = Q z ​ y . z\notin A\;\text{,}\;\;x,y\in A\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad Q_{zx}=Q_{zy}. 
 
 
 Then, 
 

 
 (A.8) 
 
 ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) ≔ { A ⊆ [ n ] : A ​  is a  Q -module } \mathcal{M}(Q)\coloneqq\left\{A\subseteq[n]:A\text{ is a $Q$-module}\right\} 
 
 
 denotes the family of all  Q Q -modules. 
 
 
 We first record the only closure property of modules that will be used. We say that two sets  A , D ⊆ [ n ] A,D\subseteq[n]   overlap  if  A ∩ D A\cap D ,  A ∖ D A\setminus D , and  D ∖ A D\setminus A  are all nonempty. 
 
 
 Lemma A.2   (Closure of modules) . 
 
 If  A , D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A,D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  overlap, then 
 

 
 (A.9) 
 
 A ∩ D ​ , ​ A ∪ D ​ , ​ A ∖ D ​ , ​ D ∖ A ​ , ​ A △ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) ​ . A\cap D\;\text{,}\;\;A\cup D\;\text{,}\;\;A\setminus D\;\text{,}\;\;D\setminus A\;\text{,}\;\;A\mathbin{\triangle}D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  A , D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A,D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  overlap. If  x , y ∈ A ∩ D x,y\in A\cap D  and  z ∉ A ∩ D z\notin A\cap D , then  z ∉ A z\notin A  or  z ∉ D z\notin D , and the module property of the corresponding set gives  Q z ​ x = Q z ​ y Q_{zx}=Q_{zy} . Hence,  A ∩ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\cap D\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . 
 
 
 Next, let  x , y ∈ A ∪ D x,y\in A\cup D  and  z ∉ A ∪ D z\notin A\cup D . If  x , y x,y  both belong to  A A  or both to  D D , the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise, after exchanging  x x  and  y y , we may assume that  x ∈ A ∖ D x\in A\setminus D  and  y ∈ D ∖ A y\in D\setminus A . Choosing  u ∈ A ∩ D u\in A\cap D , we obtain 
 

 
 (A.10) 
 
 Q z ​ x = Q z ​ u = Q z ​ y ​ . Q_{zx}=Q_{zu}=Q_{zy}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus,  A ∪ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\cup D\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . 
 
 
 Consider now  A ∖ D A\setminus D . Let  x , y ∈ A ∖ D x,y\in A\setminus D  and  z ∉ A ∖ D z\notin A\setminus D . If  z ∉ A z\notin A , the module property of  A A  applies. Otherwise,  z ∈ A ∩ D z\in A\cap D . Choose  u ∈ D ∖ A u\in D\setminus A . Since  x , y ∉ D x,y\notin D ,  u ∉ A u\notin A , and  Q Q  is symmetric, 
 

 
 (A.11) 
 
 Q z ​ x = Q x ​ z = Q x ​ u = Q u ​ x = Q u ​ y = Q y ​ u = Q y ​ z = Q z ​ y ​ . Q_{zx}=Q_{xz}=Q_{xu}=Q_{ux}=Q_{uy}=Q_{yu}=Q_{yz}=Q_{zy}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence,  A ∖ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\setminus D\in\mathcal{M}(Q) , and the same argument with  A A  and  D D  exchanged gives  D ∖ A ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) D\setminus A\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . 
 
 
 Finally, let  x , y ∈ A △ D x,y\in A\mathbin{\triangle}D  and  z ∉ A △ D z\notin A\mathbin{\triangle}D . The conclusion follows from the difference case if  x , y x,y  belong to the same one of the two sets  A ∖ D A\setminus D  and  D ∖ A D\setminus A . Otherwise, assume that  x ∈ A ∖ D x\in A\setminus D  and  y ∈ D ∖ A y\in D\setminus A . If  z ∉ A ∪ D z\notin A\cup D , choose  u ∈ A ∩ D u\in A\cap D  and write  Q z ​ x = Q z ​ u = Q z ​ y Q_{zx}=Q_{zu}=Q_{zy} . If  z ∈ A ∩ D z\in A\cap D , the module properties of  D D  and  A A  give 
 

 
 (A.12) 
 
 Q x ​ z = Q x ​ y ​ , ​ Q y ​ z = Q y ​ x ​ . Q_{xz}=Q_{xy}\;\text{,}\;\;Q_{yz}=Q_{yx}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By symmetry,  Q z ​ x = Q z ​ y Q_{zx}=Q_{zy} . Therefore,  A △ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\mathbin{\triangle}D\in\mathcal{M}(Q) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma A.3   (Triviality of modules) . 
 
 Let  Q = ( Q x ​ y ) x , y ∈ [ n ] Q=(Q_{xy})_{x,y\in[n]}  be symmetric, and suppose that every  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  is a  Q Q -module, see ( A.7 ).
If the hypergraph induced by  w w  is minimal, then there exists  c ∈ ℝ c\in\mathbb{R}  such that 
 

 
 (A.13) 
 
 Q x ​ y = c ​ , x ≠ y ​ . Q_{xy}=c\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x\neq y\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Fix the family  ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}(Q)  in ( A.8 ). Call a module  A ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\in\mathcal{M}(Q)   strong  if it overlaps no member of  ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}(Q) . 
 
 
 Step 1: the only nonempty proper strong modules are the singletons. 
Strong modules form a laminar family: two intersecting strong modules are comparable by inclusion. Every singleton is strong and, since  [ n ] [n]  is finite, is contained in a maximal proper strong module. Let 
 

 
 (A.14) 
 
 N 1 , … , N m N_{1},\ldots,N_{m} 
 
 
 be the distinct maximal proper strong modules. They are pairwise disjoint and cover  [ n ] [n] , and hence 
 

 
 (A.15) 
 
 [ n ] = N 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ N m ​ . [n]=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{m}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This partition is compatible with every module in  ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}(Q) . Indeed, if  A ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  meets  N i N_{i} , then, since  N i N_{i}  is strong, either  A ⊆ N i A\subseteq N_{i}  or  N i ⊆ A N_{i}\subseteq A . Thus, either  A A  is contained in one atom of ( A.15 ), or it is the union of all atoms that it meets. 
 
 
 In particular, ( A.15 ) is  w w -compatible, because every block  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  belongs to  ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}(Q) . Minimality excludes  1 < m < n 1<m<n , while  m ≠ 1 m\neq 1  because every  N i N_{i}  is proper. Therefore,  m = n m=n , and every  N i N_{i}  is a singleton. Since every proper strong module is contained in a maximal proper one, the claim follows. 
 
 
 Step 2: there exists a two-element module. 
Minimality, together with  w [ n ] = 0 w_{[n]}=0 , ensures that some block  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0  satisfies 
 

 
 (A.16) 
 
 2 ≤ | B | ≤ n − 1 ​ . 2\leq\left\lvert B\right\rvert\leq n-1\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, if every positive-weight block were a singleton, the two-atom partition  [ n ] = { 1 } ⊔ ( [ n ] ∖ { 1 } ) [n]=\{1\}\sqcup([n]\setminus\{1\})  would be  w w -compatible. 
 
 
 Choose  A ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) A\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  with  2 ≤ | A | ≤ n − 1 2\leq\left\lvert A\right\rvert\leq n-1  and minimal cardinality. By Step 1,  A A  is not strong, so there exists  D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  overlapping  A A . Lemma  A.2  gives 
 

 
 (A.17) 
 
 A ∩ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) ​ , ​ A ∖ D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) ​ . A\cap D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)\;\text{,}\;\;A\setminus D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Both sets are nonempty proper subsets of  A A . By the minimality of  | A | \left\lvert A\right\rvert , they are singletons, and hence  | A | = 2 \left\lvert A\right\rvert=2 . 
 
 
 Step 3: all two-element sets are modules. 
Define a relation on  [ n ] [n]  by 
 

 
 (A.18) 
 
 x ∼ y ⟺ x = y or { x , y } ∈ ℳ ( Q ) . x\sim y\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad x=y\quad\text{or}\quad\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This is an equivalence relation. Only transitivity requires verification: if  x , y , z x,y,z  are pairwise distinct and  { x , y } , { y , z } ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \{x,y\},\{y,z\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q) , these two modules overlap, and their symmetric difference  { x , z } \{x,z\}  belongs to  ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}(Q)  by Lemma  A.2 . 
 
 
 Every equivalence class  C C  belongs to  ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}(Q) . Indeed, this is clear for singletons. Otherwise, fix  x 0 ∈ C x_{0}\in C  and enumerate  C = { x 0 , x 1 , … , x r } C=\{x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\} . Starting from  { x 0 , x 1 } ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \{x_{0},x_{1}\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q) , repeatedly apply the union part of Lemma  A.2  to the overlapping modules already constructed and  { x 0 , x k } \{x_{0},x_{k}\} ,  k = 2 , … , r k=2,\ldots,r . 
 
 
 We claim that every equivalence class  C C  is strong. Suppose otherwise, and choose  D ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) D\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  of minimal cardinality among the modules overlapping  C C . Fix 
 

 
 (A.19) 
 
 x ∈ D ∩ C ​ , ​ y ∈ C ∖ D ​ , ​ z ∈ D ∖ C ​ . x\in D\cap C\;\text{,}\;\;y\in C\setminus D\;\text{,}\;\;z\in D\setminus C\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  x ∼ y x\sim y , one has  { x , y } ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) \{x,y\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . 
 
 
 First,  D ∩ C = { x } D\cap C=\{x\} . Otherwise, choosing  x ′ ∈ ( D ∩ C ) ∖ { x } x^{\prime}\in(D\cap C)\setminus\{x\} , the modules  D D  and  { x , y } \{x,y\}  overlap. Lemma  A.2  then gives 
 

 
 (A.20) 
 
 D ∖ { x , y } = D ∖ { x } ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) ​ . D\setminus\{x,y\}=D\setminus\{x\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 This set still overlaps  C C : it contains  x ′ ∈ C x^{\prime}\in C , misses  y ∈ C y\in C , and contains  z ∉ C z\notin C . Its cardinality is smaller than that of  D D , a contradiction. 
 
 
 Set
 O ≔ D ∖ { x } = D ∖ C O\coloneqq D\setminus\{x\}=D\setminus C .
Again by Lemma  A.2 ,  O ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) O\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . We show that  O O  is a singleton. Suppose that  | O | ≥ 2 \left\lvert O\right\rvert\geq 2 . Then  O O  is a nontrivial proper module and, by Step 1, is not strong. Choose  H ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) H\in\mathcal{M}(Q)  overlapping  O O . 
 
 
 If  x ∉ H x\notin H , then  D D  and  H H  overlap, and Lemma  A.2  gives  D ∖ H ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) D\setminus H\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . This set contains  x ∈ C x\in C , misses  y ∈ C y\in C , and contains a point of  O ∖ H ⊆ [ n ] ∖ C O\setminus H\subseteq[n]\setminus C ; hence it overlaps  C C . Since  D ∩ H ≠ ∅ D\cap H\neq\varnothing , it is smaller than  D D , a contradiction. 
 
 
 If  x ∈ H x\in H  and  H ⊆ D H\subseteq D , then  H H  itself overlaps  C C : it contains  x ∈ C x\in C  and a point of  H ∩ O ⊆ [ n ] ∖ C H\cap O\subseteq[n]\setminus C , while it misses  y ∈ C y\in C . Moreover,  O ∖ H ≠ ∅ O\setminus H\neq\varnothing , so  | H | < | D | \left\lvert H\right\rvert<\left\lvert D\right\rvert , again a contradiction. 
 
 
 Finally, if  x ∈ H x\in H  and  H ⊈ D H\not\subseteq D , then  D D  and  H H  overlap, and Lemma  A.2  gives  D ∩ H ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) D\cap H\in\mathcal{M}(Q) . This set contains  x ∈ C x\in C  and a point of  O ∩ H ⊆ [ n ] ∖ C O\cap H\subseteq[n]\setminus C , while it misses  y ∈ C y\in C ; hence it overlaps  C C . Since  O ∖ H ≠ ∅ O\setminus H\neq\varnothing , it is smaller than  D D , which is impossible. 
 
 
 Consequently,  O = { z } O=\{z\}  for some  z ∉ C z\notin C . But then  D = { x , z } ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) D=\{x,z\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q) , so  x ∼ z x\sim z , contradicting  x ∈ C x\in C  and  z ∉ C z\notin C . Thus, every equivalence class is strong. 
 
 
 By Step 2, at least one equivalence class contains two distinct points. Since this class is strong, Step 1 forces it to be  [ n ] [n] . Therefore, 
 

 
 (A.21) 
 
 { x , y } ∈ ℳ ⁡ ( Q ) ​ , x ≠ y ​ . \{x,y\}\in\mathcal{M}(Q)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x\neq y\,\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 We may now conclude. Fix  x ∈ [ n ] x\in[n] . For distinct  y , z ∈ [ n ] ∖ { x } y,z\in[n]\setminus\{x\} , the module property of  { y , z } \{y,z\}  gives 
 

 
 (A.22) 
 
 Q x ​ y = Q x ​ z ​ . Q_{xy}=Q_{xz}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, there exists  c x ∈ ℝ c_{x}\in\mathbb{R}  such that  Q x ​ y = c x Q_{xy}=c_{x}  for every  y ≠ x y\neq x . If  x ≠ y x\neq y , symmetry yields 
 

 
 (A.23) 
 
 c x = Q x ​ y = Q y ​ x = c y ​ . c_{x}=Q_{xy}=Q_{yx}=c_{y}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, all the  c x c_{x}  coincide, proving ( A.13 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 We are finally in shape to present the proof of Proposition  A.1 . 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  A.1 . 
 
 Decompose  ℝ n = ℋ 1 , ∗ ⊕ ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathbb{R}^{n}=\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}\oplus\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1} , where  ℋ 1 , ∗ ⊆ ℝ n \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}  is given in ( A.4 ).
For every  B ⊆ [ n ] B\subseteq[n]  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 ,  K B π K_{B}^{\pi}  is the  L 2 ​ ( π ) L^{2}(\pi) -orthogonal projection onto the configurations which are constant on  B B . Hence, it is self-adjoint, fixes  𝟏 \mathbf{1} , and leaves  ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}  invariant. 
 
 
 We show that every nonzero subspace  𝒲 ⊆ ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{W}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}  invariant under all  K B π K_{B}^{\pi} ,  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 , equals  ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} . Suppose otherwise, and let  P P  be the  L 2 ​ ( π ) L^{2}(\pi) -orthogonal projection onto  𝒲 ⊕ ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathscr{W}\oplus\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1} . Since  K B π K_{B}^{\pi} 
is self-adjoint and leaves  𝒲 ⊕ ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathscr{W}\oplus\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}  invariant, it also leaves its orthogonal complement invariant. Hence,
 P ​ K B π = K B π ​ P PK_{B}^{\pi}=K_{B}^{\pi}P .
Write  P = ( P x ​ y ) x , y ∈ [ n ] P=(P_{xy})_{x,y\in[n]} , and set 
 

 
 (A.24) 
 
 Q x ​ y ≔ P x ​ y π y ​ , x , y ∈ [ n ] ​ . Q_{xy}\coloneqq\frac{P_{xy}}{\pi_{y}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x,y\in[n]\,\text{.} 
 
 
 The matrix  Q = ( Q x ​ y ) x , y ∈ [ n ] Q=(Q_{xy})_{x,y\in[n]}  is symmetric. Moreover, since  P P  commutes with  K B π K_{B}^{\pi} , it also commutes with  I − K B π I-K_{B}^{\pi}  and therefore leaves  ran ⁡ ( I − K B π ) {\rm ran}(I-K_{B}^{\pi})  invariant. This range consists precisely of the vectors supported on  B B  with zero  π \pi -mean. Thus, for  x , y ∈ B x,y\in B , the vector  v ≔ e x / π x − e y / π y v\coloneqq e_{x}/\pi_{x}-e_{y}/\pi_{y} 
belongs to  ran ⁡ ( I − K B π ) {\rm ran}(I-K_{B}^{\pi}) , and so does  P ​ v Pv . In particular,  P ​ v Pv  vanishes at  z ∉ B z\notin B , yielding
 0 = ( P ​ v ) z = P z ​ x / π x − P z ​ y / π y = Q z ​ x − Q z ​ y 0=(Pv)_{z}={P_{zx}}/{\pi_{x}}-{P_{zy}}/{\pi_{y}}=Q_{zx}-Q_{zy} .
Hence, for every  B B  with  w B > 0 w_{B}>0 , 
 

 
 (A.25) 
 
 Q z ​ x = Q z ​ y ​ , z ∉ B ​ , ​ x , y ∈ B ​ . Q_{zx}=Q_{zy}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad z\notin B\;\text{,}\;\;x,y\in B\,\text{.} 
 
 
 By Lemma  A.3 , symmetry of  Q Q , ( A.25 ), and our assumption of hypergraph minimality, 
 

 
 (A.26) 
 
 Q x ​ y = c ​ , x ≠ y ​ , Q_{xy}=c\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x\neq y\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for some  c ∈ ℝ c\in\mathbb{R} .
Since  P ​ 𝟏 = 𝟏 P\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1} , ( A.26 ) yields 
 

 
 (A.27) 
 
 π x ​ Q x ​ x = 1 − c + c ​ π x ​ , x ∈ [ n ] ​ . \pi_{x}Q_{xx}=1-c+c\pi_{x}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad x\in[n]\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Using  P 2 = P P^{2}=P  in an off-diagonal entry  x ≠ y x\neq y  and ( A.26 )–( A.27 ), we obtain 
 

 
 (A.28) 
 
 c = Q x ​ y = ∑ z ∈ [ n ] π z ​ Q x ​ z ​ Q z ​ y = c ⁡ ( 1 − c + c ​ π x ) + c ⁡ ( 1 − c + c ​ π y ) + c 2 ​ ( 1 − π x − π y ) = 2 ​ c − c 2 ​ . \textstyle c=Q_{xy}=\sum_{z\in[n]}\pi_{z}Q_{xz}Q_{zy}=c(1-c+c\pi_{x})+c(1-c+c\pi_{y})+c^{2}(1-\pi_{x}-\pi_{y})=2c-c^{2}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus  c ∈ { 0 , 1 } c\in\{0,1\} . If  c = 0 c=0 , then  P = I P=I ; if  c = 1 c=1 , then  P x ​ y = π y P_{xy}=\pi_{y}  for all  x , y ∈ [ n ] x,y\in[n] , so  P P  is the projection onto  ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathbb{R}\mathbf{1} . Both alternatives contradict  0 ≠ 𝒲 ⊊ ℋ 1 , ∗ 0\neq\mathscr{W}\subsetneq\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} . Hence  𝒲 = ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{W}=\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} . 
 
 
 Finally, fix a nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , and let  θ ¯ ≔ θ − π ⁡ ( θ ) ​ 𝟏 \bar{\theta}\coloneqq\theta-\pi(\theta)\mathbf{1} .
Since  θ \theta  is nonconstant,  0 ≠ θ ¯ ∈ ℋ 1 , ∗ 0\neq\bar{\theta}\in\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} . The linear span 
 

 
 (A.29) 
 
 𝒮 Υ ∞ ( θ ¯ ) ≔ span { K ℓ ⋯ K 1 θ ¯ : ℓ ≥ 0 , K 1 , … , K ℓ ∈ supp ( Υ ∞ ) } \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\bar{\theta})\coloneqq{\rm span}\big\{K_{\ell}\cdots K_{1}\bar{\theta}:\ell\geq 0\,,\,K_{1},\ldots,K_{\ell}\in{\rm supp}(\Upsilon_{\infty})\big\} 
 
 
 is a nonzero common invariant subspace of  ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} , and therefore equals  ℋ 1 , ∗ \mathscr{H}_{1,\ast} . As every  K B π K_{B}^{\pi}  fixes  𝟏 \mathbf{1} , 
 

 
 (A.30) 
 
 K B π ​ θ = K B π ​ θ ¯ + π ⁡ ( θ ) ​ 𝟏 ​ . K_{B}^{\pi}\theta=K_{B}^{\pi}\bar{\theta}+\pi(\theta)\mathbf{1}\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence,  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = 𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ¯ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = ℋ 1 , ∗ + ℝ ​ 𝟏 = ℝ n \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\bar{\theta})+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathscr{H}_{1,\ast}+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}=\mathbb{R}^{n} , namely, ( A.5 ), as desired. 
 
 
 It remains to prove ( A.6 ). Fix  t > 0 t>0  and  0 ≠ g ∈ 𝒞 0\neq g\in\mathscr{C} . By Markovianity,
 e t ​ ℒ ∞ ​ g ∈ 𝒞 e^{t\mathscr{L}_{\infty}}g\in\mathscr{C} .
Suppose that this function does not belong to  int ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) {\rm int}(\mathscr{C}) . By ( 3.3 ), there exists a nonconstant  θ ∈ ℝ n \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}  such that
 ( e t ​ ℒ ∞ ​ g ) ​ ( θ ) = 0 (e^{t\mathscr{L}_{\infty}}g)(\theta)=0 .
The propagation-of-zeros argument in Lemma  3.7 , applied to the update support  Υ ∞ \Upsilon_{\infty}  from Remark  3.9 , shows that  g g  vanishes on  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta) . Moreover, by definition of  ℛ = ℛ 2 , ∗ \mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{2,\ast} , every  g ∈ ℛ g\in\mathscr{R}  vanishes on  ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathbb{R}\mathbf{1} , and the zero set of the nonnegative quadratic form  g g  is a linear subspace. Hence,  g g  vanishes on  𝒮 Υ ∞ ​ ( θ ) + ℝ ​ 𝟏 \mathcal{S}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}_{\infty}}(\theta)+\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1} . Thus, by ( A.5 ),  g = 0 g=0 , a contradiction. This proves ( A.6 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix B  Feller property of stochastic exchange models 

 
 In this section we present the proof Proposition  7.1 , which essentially amounts to verify the standard criteria in  [ Lig05 ] . 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  7.1 . 
 
 For every  η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega  and  M ∈ 𝔐 M\in\mathfrak{M} , using that the rows of  M M  are probability vectors, 
 

 
 (B.1) 
 
 ‖ η M − η ‖ 1 ≔ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] | ( η M ) x − η x | ≤ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ‖ M x ⋅ − e x ‖ 1 = 2 ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ( 1 − M x ​ x ) . \textstyle\left\lVert\eta M-\eta\right\rVert_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{x\in[n]}|(\eta M)_{x}-\eta_{x}|\leq\sum_{x\in[n]}\left\lVert M_{x{\,\cdot\,}}-e_{x}\right\rVert_{1}=2\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(1-M_{xx}\right)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Now fix  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0  and  f ∈ 𝒫 k f\in\mathscr{P}_{k} . Since  f f  is Lipschitz on the compact set  Ω \Omega , ( B.1 ) and ( 7.2 )
show that the integral in ( 7.6 ) is well defined.
Moreover, for every  M ∈ 𝔐 M\in\mathfrak{M} , the map
 η ↦ f ⁡ ( η ​ M ) − f ⁡ ( η ) \eta\mapsto f(\eta M)-f(\eta)  belongs to  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} , with
 M M -dependent coefficients which, by  0 ≤ M x ​ y ≤ 1 0\leq M_{xy}\leq 1  and the preceding bound, are all
 Υ \Upsilon -integrable under ( 7.2 ). Thus, the
integral in ( 7.6 ) is well defined and belongs to
 𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} , proving the first inclusion in
( 7.8 ). 
 
 
 Similarly, for  θ ∈ [ a , b ] n \theta\in[a,b]^{n} , 
 

 
 (B.2) 
 
 ‖ M ​ θ − θ ‖ 1 = ∑ x ∈ [ n ] | ∑ y ≠ x M x ​ y ​ ( θ y − θ x ) | ≤ ( b − a ) ​ ∑ x ∈ [ n ] ( 1 − M x ​ x ) ​ . \textstyle\left\lVert M\theta-\theta\right\rVert_{1}=\sum_{x\in[n]}|\sum_{y\neq x}M_{xy}\left(\theta_{y}-\theta_{x}\right)|\leq(b-a)\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(1-M_{xx}\right)\,\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, the same argument yields the well definition of
 ℒ Υ \mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  on  ℛ | [ a , b ] n \mathscr{R}|_{[a,b]^{n}} , and
the second inclusion in ( 7.8 ). 
 
 
 Given these facts, the construction follows from the standard theory of Markov pregenerators. We treat only  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon} , the argument for  ℒ Υ \mathscr{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  being identical. 
 
 
 According to  [ Lig05 , Definition I.2.1] ,
 ( ℒ Υ , 𝒫 ) (\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon},\mathscr{P})  is a Markov pregenerator on  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathcal{C}(\Omega) . Indeed,  𝒫 \mathscr{P}  given in ( 7.5 ) is dense in  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathcal{C}(\Omega) , contains the constants,
 ℒ Υ ​ 𝟏 = 0 \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}\mathbf{1}=0 , and  ℒ Υ \mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  satisfies the positive
minimum principle: if  f ∈ 𝒫 f\in\mathscr{P}  and  η ∈ Ω \eta\in\Omega  satisfy
 f ⁡ ( η ) = min Ω ⁡ f f(\eta)=\min_{\Omega}f , then 
 

 
 (B.3) 
 
 ℒ Υ ​ f ​ ( η ) = ∫ Υ ⁡ ( 𝑑 M ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η ​ M ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ≥ 0 ​ , \textstyle\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}f(\eta)=\int\Upsilon({\rm d}M)\,\left(f(\eta M)-f(\eta)\right)\geq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 since  η ​ M ∈ Ω \eta M\in\Omega  for every  M ∈ 𝔐 M\in\mathfrak{M} . By  [ Lig05 ,  Proposition I.2.2] , this yields condition (c) in  [ Lig05 , Definition I.2.1] .
Moreover, for every  λ > 0 \lambda>0 ,  ( I − λ ​ ℒ Υ ) ​ 𝒫 = 𝒫 (I-\lambda\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon})\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P} .
Indeed, by ( 7.8 ),  I − λ ​ ℒ Υ I-\lambda\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon} 
leaves each  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k}  invariant, and it is
injective there by the positive minimum principle; hence, by finite dimensionality of the space  𝒫 k \mathscr{P}_{k} , it is bijective. 
 
 
 Therefore, by  [ Lig05 , Propositions I.2.5–6] ,
the closure  ¯ ​ ℒ Υ \bar{}\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  is a Markov pregenerator and
 I − λ ​ ¯ ​ ℒ Υ I-\lambda\bar{}\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  has closed range. Since this range
contains the dense subspace  𝒫 \mathscr{P} , it equals  𝒞 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathcal{C}(\Omega) .
Thus,  ¯ ​ ℒ Υ \bar{}\mathcal{L}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Upsilon}  is a Markov generator  [ Lig05 , Definition I.2.7]  and, by
 [ Lig05 , Theorem I.2.9] , generates a unique
Feller process on  Ω \Omega .
∎

 Notation guide 

 
 For the reader’s convenience, we collect the main recurring notation in order of appearance. All objects are defined at the indicated locations.
Superscripts indicate restrictions to a block, as in  α B \alpha^{B}  and
 η B \eta^{B} , whereas parentheses denote the corresponding total masses, as in
 α ⁡ ( B ) = ∑ x ∈ B α x \alpha(B)=\sum_{x\in B}\alpha_{x}  and
 η ⁡ ( B ) = ∑ x ∈ B η x \eta(B)=\sum_{x\in B}\eta_{x} . Conditional quantities are denoted by
 μ B ​ ( f ) \mu_{B}(f)  and
 π x | B = π B ​ ( 𝟏 x ) = π ⁡ ( 𝟏 x ∣ B ) = π x / π ⁡ ( B ) \pi_{x|B}=\pi_{B}(\mathbf{1}_{x})=\pi(\mathbf{1}_{x}\mid B)=\pi_{x}/\pi(B) .
For a linear operator  A A  defined on a vector space of functions,  gap ⁡ ( A ) {\rm gap}(A)  always denotes the smallest (real part of an) eigenvalue of  − A -A , possibly excluding the eigenvalue associated to constant functions.

     Instructions for reporting errors 
     We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: 
     
       Click the "Report Issue"  ( 
             
           )  button, located in the page header. 
     
     Tip:  You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
     Our team has already identified  the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. 
     Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a  list of packages that need conversion , and welcome  developer contributions .
